Preface to the Second Edition

In the first edition preface, I noted the regrettable absence of Zeno's paradoxes. This was understandable, given that much of the book focuses on two questions:  
1. Does time pass?  
2. Does space exist?  

Several chapters examine motion-related questions, and Zeno's paradoxes raise serious issues about how motion occurs, though we are confident it does. These paradoxes also relate to infinite divisibility and the microstructure of the continuum, topics concerning the nature of time and space rather than their existence.

In this second edition, I now discuss Zeno's paradoxes in detail. Conscious of the risks of superficial treatment—especially with a perplexing topic like infinity—the coverage is quite thorough. These new chapters represent the most significant divergence from the first edition, though there are several other changes.

Some of these appear in Chapter 6, where various "dynamic" conceptions of time are explored. Interest in Presentism—the idea that only the present is real—has grown over the past decade, and my treatment has expanded accordingly. One major problem for Presentism is the past: how can there be facts about what happened if the past is wholly unreal? Recent literature offers several ingenious responses, outlined in section 6.10. This expansion has led to changes elsewhere: in the discussion of whether Presentism is compatible with time travel (8.8), and the difficulties Presentism faces with our immediate experience of change (7.8). Other modifications include the doctrine of "Compound Presentism" (6.11) and a challenge to the "Growing Block" model (6.6).

Other significant additions reflect recent advances in cosmology and physics. Chapter 22 includes a new section, "Branes and Bulk," discussing recent string theory speculations, including Steinhardt and Turok's cyclic universe model. The section on relativistic cosmology in Chapter 20 has also been significantly expanded. The first edition index lacked entries on "dark matter" and "dark energy," omissions now rectified. Since 2003, data from the Explorer 80 satellite (WMAP) have confirmed that about 95 percent of the universe's mass and energy is mysterious "dark" matter and energy. These data also support the view that spacetime is flat rather than curved. Section 20.7 details the path to this new consensus on the cosmos’s composition and shape.

Along with additions to the main text, there are several stylistic and terminological updates; most are minor but some more substantial. The list of web resources and bibliography have been updated with over a hundred new entries, though these do not represent a complete record of all recent publications.

I am grateful to many who helped and encouraged me during these revisions. Special thanks to Steven Gerrard for suggesting the new edition, Kate Williams for skillfully managing changes, Daniel Hill for his detailed review of the Zeno chapters, and Graham Nerlich for fruitful discussions on spatial and temporal matters.

Preface to the First Edition

Is space a real ingredient of reality, or simply nothingness? What does time’s passage amount to? Is only the present real, or does the past have reality as well? And what about the future? Are space and time finite or infinite? These questions are central for anyone interested in the cosmos or in understanding the framework of our lives. They are also among the most challenging.

Thinking clearly about space is difficult—how does one conceive of nothing? Time is even harder. While it seems the past is real differently from the future, the present seems uniquely real compared to the past. What sort of reality does the past really have? It seems obvious time passes, and the present advances into the future, but what does the passage of time involve? These fundamental questions defy obvious answers.

Many answers have been proposed, often fascinating and subtle. The philosophical literature, ancient and modern, is vast and diverse, covering semantics, causation, modality, and phenomenology. The scientific and mathematical literature is no less extensive and difficult, including the physics of motion, the nature of the continuum, geometry, relativity, and quantum mechanics. This range makes the study of space and time uniquely rewarding and challenging.

The book aims to introduce the contemporary philosophical debate, assuming little prior knowledge, but still guiding the interested reader toward advanced work and a reasonable understanding of current physics. It is primarily intended for philosophy students in metaphysics or philosophy of science courses from the second year upwards, but should also be accessible to interested newcomers. Philosophical jargon is used sparingly and explained, with helpful terms in the Glossary. Those familiar with popular science and curious about the implications of frontier physics will find much of interest. Much contemporary philosophy of space and time focuses precisely on these questions.

Given the subject’s complexity, the focus is on two key questions:  
1. Does time pass?  
2. Does space exist?

Since these cannot be addressed in isolation, many other topics are included, though some are omitted, such as Zeno's paradoxes. This was to avoid superficial treatment and maintain interest.

The book is in three parts. The first, comprising Chapters 2 to 8, focuses exclusively on time. Much analytic philosophy of time has centered on linguistic and semantic issues, but this work emphasizes metaphysical and scientific questions, reflecting recent developments. I begin with McTaggart's paradox (1908), which has influenced recent thought. While some see McTaggart’s argument as disproving all dynamic conceptions of time, I argue for a more moderate view: some dynamic conceptions fail, others survive. Surviving dynamic views are examined in Chapters 5 and 6, including the "growing universe" model and Presentism. Prior to this, Chapters 3 and 4 consider the alternative "Block" or eternalist conception—that time is like space and does not pass. A key concern here is why time seems to pass if it does not in reality. This question is revisited in Chapter 7, devoted to the phenomenology of time—how time appears in our consciousness.This puzzling and important issue is too often treated very superficially or not at all. To round off this opening stage, the possibility (or otherwise) of time travel is examined in Chapter 8.

My approach to space follows a different trajectory. After a general overview in Chapter 9, we take a step back into the past and look in some detail at the sixteenth- and seventeenth-century debates concerning space and motion, with the main protagonists being Descartes, Galileo, Leibniz, and Newton (with the latter pair having starring roles). This historical detour provides a change of atmosphere after the largely a priori investigation into time, but is also justified on other grounds. Since purely metaphysical investigations into the nature of space do not carry one very far, empirical considerations have to be introduced at an earlier stage, and the Leibniz-Newton controversy provides a useful point at which to start, not to mention the fact that this controversy is still being discussed in the contemporary literature.

Chapter 12 takes the debate about motion and dynamics a step further by examining how the transition to neo-Newtonian spacetime affects the Leibniz-Newton controversy. The move towards the present day is continued in Chapters 13 and 14, where the impact of non-Euclidean geometries is considered, together with Kant's "hand" argument and Poincaré's conventionalism. Chapter 15 is purely metaphysical: the topic is Foster's powerful but complex argument for adopting an anti-realist stance toward physical space.

The final part of the book is devoted entirely to contemporary scientific theories and their interpretation. I provide an informal introduction to Einstein's special theory of relativity and Minkowski spacetime in Chapter 18, and consider their metaphysical implications in Chapter 19. It is sometimes assumed that the special theory, by virtue of the way it relativizes simultaneity (and hence the present), establishes the Block view of time beyond all doubt, but this is not so. There are dynamic models that are compatible with the special theory, and while these models have some counterintuitive consequences, so too does the Block conception, not to mention the special theory itself. Some of the rudiments of Einstein's general theory are expounded in Chapter 20, and its metaphysical implications (mainly concerning substantivalism) are examined in Chapter 21. I close by briefly considering some of the more speculative developments in recent physics.

The book's three parts could be seen as being devoted to time, space, and spacetime respectively—at one point I considered separating and labeling them as such—but this would have been misleading. The "Block universe" considered in Chapters 3 and 4 is a four-dimensional spacetime continuum, and spacetime returns again (albeit in neo-Newtonian guise) in Chapter 12. Upon reflection, it seemed better to acknowledge the interrelated character of the issues by not imposing a formal division into parts.

A problem facing any book such as this concerns the balance between philosophy and science. How much science should be included? How advanced should it be? Where should it go? Needless to say, there is no perfect solution. I have opted for a policy of progressive integration: as the story unfolds, the balance tilts from "philosophy plus a little physics" to "physics plus philosophy of physics," but the latter stage is reached only in the last few chapters, and the level of exposition remains elementary. A more difficult choice was what physics to include, and here, too, hard choices had to be made: there is no separate or systematic treatment of quantum theory. This is a deficiency, for it may well be that developments in this area will have a decisive impact in years to come, when quantum gravity theory comes into its own. But we are not there yet, and given the plethora of competing interpretations of quantum theory in its current guise, it is impossible to know what impact it will eventually have. Hence, I came to the conclusion that the omission was justifiable; remedying it would have made an already long work even longer, and sacrificing relativity was never an option. Quantum theory does, however, make an occasional unsystematic appearance in Chapter 8.1 illustrates the difference between these two competing views; to simplify the picture, only a small volume of space is shown, and time is omitted altogether.

It is important to note that in denying the existence of space and time as objects in their own right, relationists are not claiming that statements about where things are located or when they happen are false; they readily admit that there are spatial and temporal relations or distances between objects and events. What they claim is that recognizing the existence of spatial and temporal relations between things doesn't require us to subscribe to the view that space and time are entities in their own right. The relationist is, of course, required to give a convincing account of what these "relations" are and how they manage to perform the roles required of them.

The substantivalist is sometimes said to regard space and time as akin to containers, within which everything else exists and occurs. In one sense, this characterization of substantivalism is accurate, but in another, it is misleading. An ordinary container, such as a box, consists of a material shell and nothing else. If all the air within an otherwise empty box is removed, the walls of the box are all that remain. Contemporary substantivalists do not think of space and time in this way; they think of them as continuous and pervasive mediums that extend everywhere and "everywhen." The air can be removed from a box, but the space cannot, and if space is substantival, a so-called empty box remains completely filled with substantival space, as does the so-called "empty space" between the stars and galaxies. Roughly speaking, a substantivalist holds that space and time "contain" objects in the way that an ocean contains the solid things that float within it.

It is plain that the verdict we reach on the ontological question concerning space and time has momentous implications for what the universe contains. If the substantivalist is correct, space and time are the biggest things there are. The relationist view is much more economical: the vast, pervasive mediums posited by the substantivalist simply do not exist.

We will start to explore this issue in Chapter 9, and it will remain center stage for much of the remainder of the book. Although the initial focus will be on space, the ontological status of time will not be ignored, even though it will not receive separate treatment (in Chapters 2–8, where time occupies center stage, the substantivalism-relationism controversy will not feature at all). Since any spatial point also has a temporal location, it is more properly viewed as a point in space and time, i.e., as a "spacetime point." The substantivalist takes the totality of these spacetime points to be a real entity: "spacetime." If this conception is correct, time is just as real as space.

### 1.2 Questions of Structure

Irrespective of how the debate between substantivalists and relationists is resolved, there are questions to be addressed concerning what can loosely be called the "structural" characteristics of space and time. Whereas substantivalists take these questions to be about actual entities, relationists take them to be about the ways material things are related in spatial and temporal ways.

To get some impression of the flavor of these structural questions, consider the following claims:
- Space is infinite in size, three-dimensional, Euclidean (so travelers moving along parallel trajectories will never meet), isotropic (there is no privileged spatial direction), and continuous (infinitely divisible).
- Time is infinite, one-dimensional, linear, anisotropic (there is a privileged temporal direction), and continuous.
- There is just one space and one time.
- Time and space are absolute, in that they are unaffected by the presence or absence (or distribution) of material things.

Some readers may find this characterization quite plausible. Those with some knowledge of ancient and medieval cosmologies will realize that it is similar to one defended by the ancient atomists but soon rejected in favor of the Aristotelian system, which posited, among other things, a finite, non-homogeneous, and anisotropic space, and which returned to favor only in the 1500s. Those with some scientific or science fiction background will realize that this essentially Newtonian conception was overturned by Einstein's theories of special and general relativity in the early twentieth century. More recently, some quantum theorists have argued that time is branching rather than linear, while others maintain that time does not exist; relativistic cosmologists have argued that there may be multiple spacetimes or "baby universes" sprouting from the other end of black holes; and superstring theorists argue for the astonishing view that there are nine or more spatial dimensions.

Fascinating as many of these recent theories are, this is not the place for a thorough discussion of them, although their implications cannot be ignored either. Where space is concerned, my main focus will be on the substantivalist-relationist controversy, and I will only be looking at the structural issues that are relevant to it. Since many structural issues are relevant, we will at least touch upon some of these complex topics.

### 1.3. Physics and Metaphysics

Are there any facts about space and time that are necessary, meaning they hold in all possible worlds and can be established by a priori reasoning? Or are all interesting questions about space and time contingent and a posteriori, to be answered only by physics and cosmology?

If we answer yes to the latter, the philosophy of space and time reduces to a branch of the philosophy of science, and we must focus on contemporary scientific theories, especially relativity and quantum theory. There is something to be said for this position. Scientists have made claims about space and time that provide answers to many questions philosophers have struggled with; many of the relevant theories are well confirmed and cannot be ignored. Science and mathematics have provided new ways of thinking about space and time that philosophers might never have discovered alone.

A simple but influential argument dates back to ancient Greek times: Suppose space were finite. If you traveled to the outermost boundary and tried to go further, what would happen? Surely, you could move through this supposed barrier. What could stop you, since there is nothing on the other side? It follows that such a limit cannot exist, so space must be infinite.

There are metaphysical replies. For example, by definition, to move is to change your place in space. It makes no sense to suppose you could move beyond the edge of space because there are no places beyond this limit to occupy. Hence, assuming space is finite does not lead to an absurdity.

This may seem reasonable, but further arguments arise. Does this reply assume a substantivalist view of space? A relationist would reject the idea that motion consists of moving onto pre-existing spatial locations; instead, motion is an object changing its spatial relations to other objects. What, then, prevents an object from moving ever further from others? The debate continues. However, as we will see in Chapter 13, the discovery of non-Euclidean geometries and the possibility that space could be curved transformed these assumptions in the nineteenth century. If space is positively curved—a three-dimensional counterpart of the surface of a sphere—a traveler could keep moving straight and eventually return to their starting point. Space can thus be finite without edges or obstacles. This example shows how philosophical discussions of space and time have been influenced—and sometimes made obsolete—by developments in physics and mathematics.

However, while science is important, it would be a mistake to suppose that only science matters and that philosophical considerations are dispensable. Physics and metaphysics are interdependent. Scientists evaluating or inventing theories are influenced by metaphysical considerations: ideas about what the world is like. Moreover, the metaphysical implications of scientific theories—how mathematical formalism relates to reality—are often unclear, especially in relativity and quantum theory. Thus, metaphysical inquiry is needed even when intertwined with scientific theorizing.

Metaphysics also has its own distinctive domains and methods. While some questions about space and time require physics for answers, others do not.Some of these questions are about topics that lie altogether outside the domain of physics, for example, the role space plays in our ordinary modes of thought; the way time manifests itself in human consciousness; our different attitudes toward the past, present, and future, and their rationality or otherwise. 

However, even if we confine ourselves to talking about time and space themselves, rather than how we think about or experience them, there are conceptual issues of a distinctively philosophical sort that remain of interest. Assuming (as most do) that the laws of nature are contingent, there are possible worlds of a recognizably physical sort—worlds that resemble ours in superficial (observable) ways, but where the laws of nature are different. Many of these possible worlds are spatiotemporal (for example, those governed by Newton's laws or Aristotle's laws). The fact that these worlds are spatiotemporal means that our concepts of space and time are to some degree independent of particular scientific theories, allowing us to investigate these concepts independently of any one scientific theory. 

What must a world be like in order for it to be spatial and temporal? What are the different forms that space and time can take? In their attempts to answer these questions, philosophers have introduced many novel and counterintuitive ideas, quite a few of which are stranger than anything seriously entertained by scientists engaged in the struggle to ascertain the particular forms that space and time take in this world. 

Finally, our current fundamental physical theories are imperfect; quantum theory and general relativity have yet to be fully reconciled. It may well be that the theory that emerges from this eventual union will imply a very different nature of space and time than those of currently accepted theories. Therefore, it would be short-sighted to consider current scientific theories as the last word on space and time in our universe—or in other possible universes.

### 1.4 Time: The Great Divide

The issues introduced so far, concerning ontology and structure, pertain equally to space and time. However, there is one issue concerning time that has no spatial counterpart. If asked "How does time differ from space?", after some reflection, you might say, "It's not so easy to travel through, it has one dimension rather than three, and it has a direction."

Focusing on the last answer: why do we say that time has a direction whereas space doesn't? Since there are three dimensions of space and one of time, someone might point out that space has more directions than time, and in one sense, that is correct. But this response omits that none of these spatial directions is singled out as special or distinctive. When we draw a diagram with an axis representing time, we usually insert a single-headed arrow pointing towards the future. Although we may insert arrows indicating different spatial directions, no one spatial direction is privileged—up, down, right, left, north, south, east, west—each direction is treated equally. This convention has a linguistic counterpart: we sometimes talk of "the arrow of time," but never "the arrow of space." But what exactly does time's arrow involve?

The idea that "things always get worse, never better," often associated with the second law of thermodynamics, which states that entropy (or disorder) tends to increase, provides one example of a temporal arrow—certain processes favor one temporal direction. But there seems to be more; the arrow of time runs deeper—there is a directedness that belongs to time itself, rather than to anything in time. Many are inclined to think this way.

We speak of "time passing," "the march of time," "the flow of time," without spatial analogues like "space passing" or "the march of space." When we say that time passes, what we mean is essentially this: what is future will become present; what is present will become past; what is past was once present.

For example, your birth was once present, but now lies in the past. Your next birthday lies in the future but gets closer every day—to the present. We can think of the present as advancing into the future or the future advancing towards the present; either way, the distance between them decreases. This applies to all times and events: they start in the future, become present, then recede into the past. This process is called "temporal passage" and has no obvious spatial counterpart.

This is how we talk, but what in reality corresponds to this? Here is the great divide in the philosophy of time.

Some believe temporal passage is a real phenomenon, an objective feature of reality independent of conscious beings' perspectives. These subscribe to a dynamic view of time. Some hold that passage involves a special property of "presentness" moving along the timeline. Others explain passage by denying the future's reality: only the past and present exist, but reality grows as future possibilities become present actualities. Others deny reality to both past and future, seeing time as a succession of fleeting presents.

Opposing them are many philosophers who reject the idea that temporal passage is real and objective. They adhere to the "Block view," "four-dimensionalism," or "eternalism." According to this view, all moments of time and events are equally real; there is no moving present—nothing becomes present and then ceases to be so. Differences between past, present, and future are differences of perspective. For example, when someone says "It's sunny here" in Australia and someone else says "It's raining here" in New Zealand, both statements are true relative to their locations; neither place is special because of the term "here." Similarly, according to eternalism, when someone in 1801 says, "It's snowing now," and someone in 2001 says the same, both statements are true at their respective times. Time and space are ontologically equivalent in this regard: space consists of a three-dimensional arrangement of coexisting locations, none special; time consists of a linear one-dimensional series of coexisting times, none special. Saying that times coexist means they all exist together in the universe, just as simultaneous locations in space do, although times occur in succession rather than simultaneously.

This conception is often called the "Block view." Imagine a long, solid crystalline block within which small plastic figures are embedded in various poses. If the substantivalist view is correct, this illustrates our condition, except the block extends both through space and time, making it a four-dimensional entity. All the people frozen in different poses at different places and times in this block are equally real—some are you, as you were yesterday, and as you will be tomorrow. Although the block's contents are permanent fixtures, the block itself does not endure through time; rather, time is a dimension within the block. Viewed as a whole, the block is an eternal or sempiternal entity.

If, on the other hand, the relationist view is correct, we are not embedded in a crystal-like block; the world consists only of material bodies in spatial and temporal relations. Yet these interrelated things form a four-dimensional ensemble; only the surrounding medium posited by substantivalists is absent.

These two views of reality—the eternal and the dynamic—are as old as Western philosophy. Heraclitus, around 540–480 BC, held that the world was entirely dynamic, an endless process of change, creation, and annihilation. Parmenides, around 520–430 BC, maintained the opposite: that being is ungenerated, undestroyed, whole, motionless, and balanced; nothing changes or will be other than what is. This issue still resonates today.

When first encountering the Block view, many people find it utterly absurd.That we live out our lives in a constantly advancing present, that past and future events are different from those that are present—what could be more obvious? But as Block theorists are fond of pointing out, there are plenty of other cases where our everyday experience seems to provide overwhelming support for beliefs that are false. Can you remember how you felt when, as a child, you were told that there are folk in other countries for whom our "down" is their "up"? Even now, are you entirely comfortable with the thought that the Earth is shooting through space at a rate of many miles per second?

Others find the Block view more repellent than absurd. Most of us tend to assume that, even if the past is fixed, the future is open: what it will bring is as yet undetermined. If the Block view is true, this is wrong, for the future is just as real, solid, and immutable as the past. How our lives will unfold from now until the moment of our deaths is (in a manner of speaking) already laid down. How could it be otherwise if the future stages of our lives are just as real as the past stages? This is not to say that we have no power over the ways our lives will unfold, for we do. We will all make choices, and the choices we make will contribute to the ways our lives will turn out. But if the Block view is true, the choices we will make are inscribed in the fabric of reality in precisely the same way as the choices we have already made. While those who find this "temporal determinism" unsettling will be inclined to reject the conception of time from which it derives, it would be a mistake to suppose that the Block view must be false simply because it has unpalatable consequences. The universe is under no obligation to conform to our preconceptions or preferences.

The issue of whether or not time is dynamic may impact how we think of our lives, but it also has consequences beyond individual concerns. A universe in which the sum total of reality is limited to what is momentarily present is far smaller than a universe in which the sum total of reality includes both past and future as well. Similarly, a universe in which only the past and present are real is larger than one that includes only the present. Although this concerns temporal rather than spatial extent, it is no less significant. The debate between substantivalism and relationism has important ontological implications regarding the nature of space, but the implications of the eternal versus dynamic time dispute are even more vast.

### 1.5 Two Frameworks

Common sense may tell us that time is dynamic rather than eternal or passage-free, but our ordinary ways of talking about time reflect both perspectives. There are two families of temporal terms and concepts that we commonly use, one embodying the dynamic view and the other the opposing non-dynamic (or Block) view. McTaggart is credited with clearly distinguishing these two ways of thinking. Since this distinction and McTaggart's terminology continue to play a significant role in contemporary debates, they are introduced here.

Imagine finding the following message in a bottle: "The bomb will explode under the Eiffel Tower four days from now." The message is uninformative because, without knowing when it was written, you cannot tell which time the word "now" refers to. Thus, you have no way of knowing whether the bomb was intended to explode a hundred years ago, in two days, or fifty years in the future. The same confusion would occur with phrases like "the day after tomorrow" or "today." This contrasts with a message like "The bomb will explode under the Eiffel Tower on 1 June 1987," which specifies an absolute date.

We can think of events as situated at a distance or direction from the present time: for example, "E lies in the past," "E is occurring now," "E will occur three days from now," or "E occurred fifty years ago." Alternatively, we can think of events in relation to other events without referencing the present at all: for example, "E occurred seven years after the Battle of Trafalgar," "E happened three days before the first Moon landing," or "E took place in 1977."

McTaggart called the time series that runs from the far past, through the present "now," and on into the future, the "A-series." The time series that is independent of the present he termed the "B-series." Each series has distinctive temporal characteristics: pastness, presentness, and futurity for the A-series; and "simultaneous with," "earlier than," and "later than" for the B-series. Later writers also discuss A- and B-concepts, A- and B-sentences, and A- and B-facts. For convenience, these diverse items are grouped under the terms "A-framework" and "B-framework."

The ways of thinking about time in the A- and B-frameworks are sometimes called the "tensed" and "tenseless" conceptions. This terminology is based on the basic grammatical tenses—past, present, and future—that underlie the A-framework but are absent from the B-framework.

Although the A-series and the B-series represent different ways of thinking about time, they are not wholly independent. The basic B-relations of simultaneity and temporal order exist within the A-framework. For example, "today" is one day later than "yesterday," and "a week ago" is seven days earlier than today. Both frameworks order the same events in the same way; "today" corresponds to December 1, "yesterday" to November 30, and "tomorrow" to December 2. Although we think of time in terms of both frameworks, we do not usually consider there to be two distinct sequences of times running in parallel—there is just one sequence.

The two frameworks are intertwined but different. The B-series is a static ordering of events and times. For example, the first Moon landing in 1969 took place 24 years after the end of the Second World War and 31 years before the end of the second millennium. These B-facts remain true at all times. In contrast, A-statements and A-facts are dynamic and change with time. For example, saying "There are now 25 days until Christmas" is true at the start of December but false a few days later, because the referent of "now" changes as time passes. Thus, the A-series is a constantly changing temporal ordering, with the present always advancing into the future, making A-facts transitory and A-statements subject to changes in truth value.

### 1.6 Matters Terminological

The following characterizations are simplified for present purposes:

- The Block View: All times and events coexist timelessly and are equally real; temporal passage is unreal.
- The Dynamic View: Temporal passage is real, and time is not merely an ensemble of coexisting times and events. Thus, the block-universe view is false.
- The Tensed Theory: A-concepts such as past, present, and future are essential and cannot be removed from any adequate account of the nature of time.
- The Tenseless Theory: B-concepts such as earlier than, later than, and simultaneous with suffice for an adequate account of time.

The dispute between dynamists and eternalists concerns the nature of time itself, while the tensed-tenseless dispute concerns the concepts used to describe time and its contents. These are distinct issues; no assumptions should be made at this point about how they will be resolved. Therefore, terms like "A-theorist" and "B-theorist" must be used carefully. Here, a B-theorist is someone who subscribes to the Block View, and an A-theorist is someone who does not.

## 2. McTaggart on Time's Unreality

### 2.1 Could Time Be Unreal?

To encourage serious reflection on the possibility that reality might be very different from how it appears, Descartes introduced the idea of an all-powerful demon. This demon is imagined to be causing us to experience hallucinations—sensory experiences that do not correspond to anything real. These experiences include not only our current perceptions but also memories of past experiences, which we normally take to reveal the world to us. The purpose of this hypothetical scenario is to make us aware that our experience does not guarantee that reality is as it seems.

Let us modify the scenario: The all-powerful demon tells us it has not been deceiving us with hallucinations; the world is basically as it appears. However, it has decided to abolish time. What should we make of this announcement? Would it matter? What difference would it make? Would the world cease to exist when time is abolished, with the present and the past vanishing? Or would everything simply come to a stop?People and things wouldn't cease to exist; they would simply stop changing. They would be frozen in mid-sentence, in mid-air, and timelessly remain in this condition. Most of us would surely agree that the absence of time would make a very significant difference.

McTaggart did not believe in supernatural explanations, but he maintained that time is unreal. His claim is remarkable because he did not argue that our perceptions are illusions; rather, he held that time would be unreal even if the world appeared exactly as it does. Though this may sound ridiculous, McTaggart uses a metaphysical argument to claim that time could not possibly exist. This argument has continued to be discussed and taken seriously, with many believing it reveals something important about the nature of time.

### 2.2 Change as the Essence of Time

McTaggart's argument centers on two ways of understanding time: the A-series, which orders events as past, present, or future, and the B-series, which orders events as earlier or later. He suggests that time is always observed as forming both series and that anyone who believes in time's reality must believe both series are real. His argument for the unreality of time is:

1. If the A-series does not exist, time does not exist.
2. The A-series is metaphysically incoherent and therefore does not exist.
3. Therefore, time does not exist.

If the first two premises hold, the conclusion follows. For now, the focus is on the first.

McTaggart argues two points here. First, time is the dimension of change. This doesn't mean something is always changing, but time allows change to occur. A world without time would be one without change. His key claim is that a world where change cannot occur is a world without time.

Second, change requires the A-series. To illustrate, consider a poker that is red hot at one end and cool at the other. This spatial difference is not a change. But if the poker cools over several minutes, this is change over time—a change in properties as time passes. If time existed without the A-series, and only the B-series held, a poker hot at one moment and cold at another would not involve change, because these facts are permanent. The B-theorist treats all times as equally real, similar to how we treat space: as an extended dimension with coexisting locations. So, having different properties at different times in a B-series world is like spatial variation at a single time—static and not change.

With the A-series, however, properties are associated with changing temporal positions: an event moves from future, to present, to past. So, the poker’s being hot and cold corresponds to different changing times. McTaggart concludes that without the A-series and its changing properties, there is no change and therefore no time.

Though McTaggart's argument may not fully convince, it raises a valid point: B-theorists conceive time more like space, which challenges common-sense views of change. Next, we examine McTaggart's reasons for denying the reality of the A-series.

### 2.3 McTaggart's A-paradox

McTaggart’s well-known objection to the A-series starts with the fact that past, present, and future are incompatible attributes. An event cannot be present and past or future at the same time. However, events seem to possess all three attributes over time, as they start as future, become present, and then become past. Since these attributes cannot simultaneously belong to one event, McTaggart argues the A-series is paradoxical and therefore cannot exist.

A common reply is that these properties apply successively, not simultaneously. For example, an event "is present, will be past, and has been future." The characteristics are incompatible only if simultaneous, and none of these occur at the same time.

McTaggart counters that this reply is circular because it uses the notion of time to explain time. While some concepts may be fundamental, McTaggart presses further with a deeper objection.

He argues that this explanation leads to an infinite regress. Saying an event "will be past" or "has been future" presupposes a second A-series to order these statements in time, which itself would require a third, and so on endlessly.

To clarify, consider the statement:  
M is present, will be past, and has been future.

This can be rephrased as:  
M is present at a moment of present time, past at a moment of future time, and future at a moment of past time.

Or simply:  
M is present in the present, past in the future, and future in the past.

These restatements introduce a second-level temporal ordering. Though this might seem to solve the paradox, McTaggart argues that it only delays the problem, because the second-level ordering repeats the same issues as the first.

Thus, the attempt to avoid the paradox just leads to an endless series of temporal orders, none of which can satisfactorily explain the reality of the A-series.There are, in fact, nine second-level predicates, not just three:

- is past in the past  
- is past in the present  
- is past in the future  
- is present in the past  
- is present in the present  
- is present in the future  
- is future in the past  
- is future in the present  
- is future in the future  

Assuming that time passes, the position of an event in the A-series is constantly changing, so every event falls under each and every one of these second-level predicates. Take an event such as the death of Kennedy and consider the predicate "is past in the past." This was true yesterday, since yesterday is now past, and Kennedy's death occurred before it. The predicate "is past in the present" is true now, while "is past in the future" will be true tomorrow; "is present in the past" is true on the date Kennedy died, as is "is present in the present." The predicate "is present in the future" applies to the event before it happened, as do "is future in the past," "is future in the present," and "is future in the future."

Some of these second-level temporal predicates are compatible with one another; for example, "is past in the future," "is present in the present," and "is future in the past" were introduced to avoid the initial paradox. But some are not compatible; for example, predicates that together state that an event is past in the present, present in the present, and future in the present contradict one another. McTaggart can therefore claim that introducing second-order tenses fails to eradicate the initial paradox since the same paradox exists at the higher level of temporal predication.

McTaggart on Time's Unreality

In reply, the A-theorist might insist that events don't have all nine second-level properties simultaneously; rather, they have them successively. Describing this succession requires third-level temporal predicates. But once again, the passage of time ensures that each and every third-level predicate applies to everything, and not all of them are compatible. This leads to fourth-level predicates, and so on in an endless regress through ever more complex temporal predicates. The difficulty is not removed by ascending the hierarchy, no matter how many levels we add. The basic predicates—past, present, future—appear at every level, so any inconsistency connected with the first level also appears at every level in this infinite hierarchy.

This regress is vicious. We begin with a paradoxical situation, and to eliminate the problem, we appeal to a higher level of temporal predicates. But since each level harbors essentially the same problem, the inconsistency is never removed. McTaggart thus concludes that the A-series is inherently paradoxical and cannot correspond to anything real. It doesn't make sense to describe things as present, past, or future if these terms are properties that all events eventually possess. Given that he also maintains that without A-change, the relations of "earlier than" and "later than" cannot be real, he concludes that time does not exist. What does exist, according to McTaggart, is an entirely atemporal "C-series": an eternal four-dimensional block of events, whose contents are ordered but not in a temporal way.

Other Routes to the Same Place

What are we to make of McTaggart's A-paradox? If you find yourself thinking, "Interesting, but I'm not convinced; there's something fishy going on, but I'm not quite sure what," you would not be alone. Broad observed of the A-paradox: "I suppose that every reader must have felt about it as any healthy-minded person feels about the Ontological Argument for the existence of God, viz., that it is obviously wrong somewhere, but that it may not be easy to say precisely what is wrong with it." However, some contemporary philosophers of time—Horwich, Le Poidevin, Mellor, Oaklander, and Schlesinger—have argued that McTaggart's paradox is genuine.

For those inclined to resist McTaggart's reasoning, one plausible diagnosis is to deny that the alleged vicious regress ever begins. McTaggart assumes that a tensed theorist who believes time passes is committed to the claim that, for any event E, "E is past, present, and future." Are tensed theorists really obliged to use this unnatural and problematic formulation? Smith, himself an advocate of the tensed theory, rejects this formulation in favor of the following: for any event E, "E will be past, is now present, and was future; or E is now past, was present, and was (still earlier) future; or E is now future, will be present, and will (still later) be past." This disjunctive formulation is more natural and seems free from paradox.

However, the fact that McTaggart's argument is flawed does not mean that his conclusion is false. The particular dynamic model of time he was seeking to discredit is deeply problematic, even if not for the reason he proposed.

McTaggart's target is a theory of time that could be called the "transitory tensed property" view. In its simplest form, this theory posits a static timeline (the B-series) and a moving present, with the latter typically construed as a distinctive property, "presentness," which is successively instantiated at different times. More graphically, this conception is sometimes known as the Moving Spotlight model. C. D. Broad described this position as imagining the history of the world existing eternally in a certain order of events, with the characteristic of presentness moving along it like a spot of light from a policeman's bull's-eye traversing the fronts of the houses in a street. What is illuminated is the present; what has been illuminated is the past; and what has not yet been illuminated is the future.

Whatever the label, the model faces two problems. First, what is the nature of the transitory property? When objects and events take their turn under the moving spotlight of the present, how are they affected or influenced? Second, does it really make sense to suppose that this distinctive property of presentness is transitory? These issues are considered in turn.

The Nature of A-properties

Properties can be divided into intrinsic and relational. A sugar cube's shape, size, and composition are intrinsic properties. Its relational properties include being smaller than Mount Everest, being to the left of a teacup, or being above the floor but beneath the ceiling. The cube has many relational properties because of its intrinsic ones, but the two types are quite different. Having a relational property means being related in some way to other objects. An object's intrinsic properties concern how it is in itself, independently of relationships.

The doctrine that A-properties are intrinsic (or "qualities," in McTaggart's terms) can take different forms. There could be just one intrinsic A-property of presentness, with pastness and futurity defined relationally. Alternatively, there could be three intrinsic A-properties: pastness, presentness, and futurity. Or two could be intrinsic, with the third defined relationally. The key point is that possessing an intrinsic A-property adds a feature to an object that does not depend on its relations to others. If A-properties are all relational, then possessing one makes no difference to how an object is in itself; it is simply a matter of being related to something else.

McTaggart suggests that A-properties are more likely to be relational rather than intrinsic. He does not fully explain why, but it is hard to see how they could be intrinsic. For example, consider the statements: "Here is a hand. The hand you see is present, your dinner this evening is future, and your breakfast this morning is past." What intrinsic feature does the present hand have that a past hand lacks? Does the past hand lack color, mass, shape, or size? If so, why is it still a hand? If the past hand has all these, what property does it lack that the present hand possesses? Saying "the property of presentness" helps little, for even if true, we don't understand what difference possessing this property makes to an object.So should an A-theorist say that A-properties are relational, the option McTaggart thought most plausible? Almost certainly not. We know that all B-type relations within the B-series are permanent; since events are always changing their A-properties, if the latter are relational, it follows that an event's A-properties must involve a relationship with something external to the B-series—call it X—and hence external to the time-series itself. But what could this X be? As McTaggart observes, "it is difficult to say" (1908: 468). But even if we could say, the relationist is not out of the woods. We are now supposing that the passage of time consists of X moving along the B-series, momentarily bestowing presence on the times it touches: first X confers presentness on t1, then on t2, then on t3, and so on. The trouble is, since we are also assuming that being present does not involve any alteration in a thing's intrinsic properties, coming to be related to X in the presence-conferring way makes no difference to how a thing is in itself. Consequently, if X were moving in the "wrong" direction—towards what we call the past—we would be none the wiser. The passage of time, thus construed, is simply not worth having: any serious A-theorist will want to insist that time's passing makes a significant difference of some sort to the things in time.

In which case, the A-theorist is obliged to return to the first option and hold that A-properties are intrinsic. As for the problem of the character of the A-properties, one option is to say something like this: "It is a mistake to think that presentness is a single intrinsic property, an ingredient to be found in every presently existing thing; there is no such property. The movement of the present consists of a distinctive and transitory alteration in at least some of the intrinsic properties objects possess."

By way of an analogy, suppose a reel of a black-and-white movie film is unwound and glued to a long glass wall. As you walk along the wall looking at the film, you see a long series of frames or "stills," each depicting the action in the film at a different instant. This static series of images corresponds to the B-series. Suppose you now see, far to your right, a single image shining brightly in full color. An instant later, the image has returned to black and white, but now the next image is illuminated, and then the next. This fleeting change in the intrinsic properties of individual images corresponds to the present moving along the B-series. Not all intrinsic properties of the image change; for example, shapes do not, but others do.

Our own experience confirms this general picture. We know perfectly well what some intrinsic properties are like when they are present, as they relate to our own conscious experiences. We all know what pain feels like. A pain that exists in the past is not being felt and so lacks this phenomenal quality. The same applies to sounds, colors, and textures as they feature in experience. Of course, we do not know what the intrinsic character of a past experience is like, simply because as soon as an experience ceases to be present, it is no longer experienced. But we can be confident that, whatever this intrinsic character is like, it is different from that of present experiences. As for material things, although we know quite a lot about their causal and structural properties, such as the shape, size, and mass of an electron, and how it reacts with other particles, we have no knowledge of their non-structural intrinsic properties—what the intrinsic nature of an electron is like in itself. Given this ignorance, we certainly cannot rule out the possibility that the non-structural intrinsic properties of material things undergo changes when they become present.

I am not suggesting that this is a viable position, but it may be the best available. In any event, even if we can make sense of A-properties as intrinsic, a problem remains.

---

All actual events have permanent locations somewhere along the static B-series.

1. A-properties are intrinsic properties of events.
2. A-properties are transitory: every event changes from being future to present and then past.

Here, I use the term "event" broadly to refer to what obtains at a given time, or over an interval of time, irrespective of whether the objects involved undergo changes. To simplify, let us return to the idea that there is a single intrinsic property of "presentness" which objects possess only when they are present. This simplification will not affect the main point.

Consider some event E at time t. On the assumption that A-properties are both intrinsic and transitory, E gains and then loses presentness. An enduring object, such as a lump of clay, can gain and lose intrinsic properties: one and the same lump could be spherical at noon, non-spherical at midnight, or red all over on Monday, black all over on Tuesday, and green all over on Wednesday. But this sort of change consists in possessing different properties at different times. The sort of change we are envisaging E undergoing is not like this: E both possesses and lacks presentness at precisely the same time, t, for the only time that E exists is the time at which it occurs: t. This seems quite absurd; just as absurd as supposing that our lump of clay could be both spherical and not spherical at precisely the same time, or be differently colored all over at the same time.

It is true that some parts of an extended event can be present when others are past and future, but the problem remains. Just focus on any single instantaneous or very brief part of this event; suppose this part occurs at t1. Can this event-slice both possess and lack presentness at t1? It is hard to see how it could if presentness is an intrinsic property; to suppose otherwise would be akin to thinking that the same material object could be both spherical and cubic at the same time. So-called "substantial change" occurs when an object loses an essential property and ceases to exist—for example, an ice cube melting into liquid water. But we cannot think of E's gaining and losing presentness in this way, for E does not cease to exist: it exists at the same location in the static B-series when it is both future and past.

I will call this the "overdetermination problem." It may seem insuperable, but in fact, there are responses to it available to the A-theorist. Whether these responses really help is another matter.

First Response: Enduring Times

Our problem is that, while one and the same object can have incompatible intrinsic properties by having them at different times, it seems incoherent to suppose that a single time (or the events at that time) can have incompatible intrinsic properties at that very time. Posing the problem in this way suggests a solution: why not say that a single time can possess incompatible properties in just the same way as an enduring object—that is, by possessing them at different times? For this to be the case, there must exist an additional dimension of time, meta-time, such that ordinary moments of time endure along this extra dimension. There is now no longer any difficulty in supposing that the same ordinary times can change their properties.

The present moves from one moment to the next. This movement is possible because the universe as a whole—and so ordinary time—is a persisting entity: it endures through meta-time. The present is at one time in ordinary time during an earlier meta-time; at a later meta-time, that ordinary time is no longer present, but the next is. We are supposing that times—and the events that occur at them—are enduring entities; they endure through the additional dimension of meta-time, and one and the same ordinary time or event can have different intrinsic properties at different meta-times, just as an ordinary material object can have different properties at different ordinary times.

But if one problem is solved, others remain. From an ontological perspective, introducing meta-time is an extravagance: it posits an additional dimension of reality. Is this a blatant case of multiplying entities beyond necessity? To this obvious objection, the A-theorist might reply:

"On the contrary. It is perfectly true that if we want to record when an event occurred, a one-dimensional timeline suffices, but what we do not usually recognize is that this one-dimensional representation is drastically incomplete: it provides no information at all about the movement of the present."

We know from our ordinary experience that there is a privileged moving present. A one-dimensional representation of time is thus inadequate; we need meta-time.

Introducing unobservable entities in order to explain observational data is a perfectly respectable scientific strategy. But even if we grant the A-theorist that our ordinary experience supports the doctrine that there is a unique and moving present, it is not clear that the proposed meta-time serves the required job, as the following objection makes clear:

"Although we are supposing that different ordinary times possess presentness at different meta-times, since all meta-times are equally real, doesn't it remain the case that every moment of ordinary time possesses presentness? In which case, the uniqueness of the 'now' is lost. Moreover, this model of time is not in the least dynamic: the posited two-dimensional system is entirely static."The proposed second temporal dimension fails to explain what it is claimed to explain and so is entirely without justification.

This objection has considerable force. The appeal of the meta-time model lies in its purported ability to explain how presentness can be gained and lost; but in fact, the model yields the result that every moment permanently possesses presentness, albeit at different moments of meta-time. Since the theory fails to explain what it sets out to explain, there is no reason to accept it.

To meet this objection, the defender of meta-time might say:

"Ah, but you are making the mistake of forgetting that meta-time is itself a temporal dimension, and as such, it has its own unique present: the meta-now. The ordinary 'now' is that time which possesses presentness in the meta-now. Since there is just one meta-now, and it is always moving, there is a single privileged ordinary 'now', and this too is always moving."

However, this clearly solves nothing. To make sense of a unique moving meta-now, we would have to introduce a further dimension of time, meta-meta-time, so that different meta-times could be present at different meta-meta-times. But we are now launched on a regress that is as absurd as it is infinite: to secure a moving present in meta-meta-time, an additional temporal dimension is required; this meta-meta-meta-time then requires its own moving "now", and hence yet a further temporal dimension, and so on, endlessly.

Second Response: Other Worlds

Instead of positing additional dimensions of time, the A-theorist might seek to overcome the overdetermination problem by positing additional worlds. Bigelow has proposed a model of this sort, observing:

"Not only can contrary properties attach to the same object at different times, but also in different possible worlds. Something may be small at one time and large in another; but similarly, it may be small at one possible world and large at another. (Which is just to say that, although it may actually be small, it could have been large.) Possible worlds can be used to keep contrary properties out of each other's hair."

If temporal passage is real, then statements such as "What is now past was present" are true. As Bigelow notes, if something was present, then it is clearly possible for this thing to be present, which means that it is present in some possible world other than the actual world.

More generally, we can interpret the claim "What is present was future and will be past" in the following modal way: "What is actually present could have been future and could have been past." In terms of possible worlds, something that has the property of presentness in the actual world also exists in other possible worlds where it lacks this property; in some of these other worlds, it possesses futurity, and in others, pastness. Since the thing has these contrary properties in different worlds, there is no problem with overdetermination.

Each possible world represents the entire history of that world, identical in all respects except the distribution of temporal properties like pastness, presentness, and futurity over its objects. These worlds can be ordered with respect to one another in terms of pastness and futurity. For example, one world can be "in the past" or "in the future" of another, based on how these temporal properties correspond across them. We can say that something was true in a world when it is true in some world in that world's past; something will be true when it is true in some world in that world's future. This framework allows claims about what was present or future to hold true across these related possible worlds.

Bigelow also defines a general earlier/later relation using these concepts and explains how the model can be adapted to accommodate different conceptions of time, such as branching futures or non-existent pasts.

Before assessing this modal model, it is important to clarify the status of these other possible worlds. There are two main positions: modal realism and modal actualism. Modal realism holds that all possible worlds are equally real and exist in the same way, each regarding itself as actual. Modal actualism maintains that only one world is actual and the others are merely possible. These views, combined with Bigelow's model, yield very different conceptions of time. Unfortunately, neither conception offers an appealing or truly dynamic account of time.

McTaggart on Time's Unreality

If realism is true, there are as many B-series as there are moments of time, with each series having a different moment as present. In effect, Bigelow's theory resembles the two-dimensional meta-time model and faces similar problems. Our lives would be spread across an infinite number of four-dimensional planes in a wholly static world, lacking temporal dynamism. As Bigelow notes, such a theory can be described in ways that make time seem like a static structure, thus missing the point of temporal passage as a dynamic process.

Modal realism is an extreme and highly controversial view. Suppose modal actualism is true instead. Then there is just one B-series and only one present time. This position is also problematic. The A-theorist might claim that since our current experience is present, we are enjoying a privileged moment. But this implies experiencing only a single moment, which is unappealing. Alternatively, if the present is located at any point in history, there would be no passage since the present is fixed, and presentness would lose its significance, as people who never experience this present moment live unaffected lives. Thus, the modal theory fails to provide a truly dynamic account of time or explain why time seems dynamic.

Consequences

Since none of the responses to the overdetermination problem are convincing, we must reject conceptions of time vulnerable to this problem. Versions of the tensed theory that treat tensed predicates as intrinsic tensed properties should be rejected, as should dynamic conceptions like the Moving-Spotlight view that posit intrinsic and transitory A-properties. This does not mean, however, that all dynamic conceptions of time are refuted. There are dynamic theories that do not posit transitory tensed intrinsics, such as the "Growing Block" view, which holds that an event is present by being the most recent addition to the universe rather than by possessing a special intrinsic property. Another view claims that only the present exists, sometimes combined with the idea of a succession of presents. These models will be considered further later.

Interestingly, McTaggart himself recognized that there are models of time that are clearly dynamic but differ from the problematic form of dynamic model he argued against. He writes:

"Could we say that, in a time which formed a B series but not an A series, the change consisted in the fact that an event ceased to be an event, while another event began to be an event? If this were the case, we should certainly have got a change. But this is impossible. An event can never cease to be an event."It can never get out of any time series in which it once is... Since, therefore, what occurs in time never begins or ceases to be, or to be in itself, and since, again, if there is to be change, it must be change of what occurs in time (for the timeless never changes), I submit that only one alternative remains. Changes must happen to the events of such a nature that the occurrence of these changes does not hinder the events from being events, and the same events both before and after the change." (1908: 459-60)

He goes on to claim that the only properties an event can come to possess and then lose while remaining the same event are A-properties such as pastness, presentness, and futurity.

What is interesting here is that McTaggart clearly appreciated that someone might argue that the universe is dynamic by virtue of the fact that events come into existence or depart from existence. Yet he rejects this view out of hand with little or nothing in the way of supporting argument. Why? Because he took it for granted that our ordinary conception of time comprises both the A-series and the B-series, and the B-series consists of a sequence of events that are all equally real and permanent.

But this is moving far too quickly. While there is no denying that history books and memories provide us with information about what occurred in the past, and calendars and diaries mention future times, facts such as these do not, in themselves, entail that the past and future are just as real as the present, nor that most people believe as much. Indeed, most people believe future events have no existence at all, and many believe that past events cease to exist when they cease to be present. These beliefs may turn out to be incoherent, but they are beliefs that many hold.

By simply assuming that the B-series is real, and hence that times and events cannot be created or annihilated, McTaggart is guilty of begging the question against those dynamic conceptions of time that maintain the opposite. In effect, his starting premise is that the world is fundamentally Parmenidean. He then considers whether such a world can also possess a dynamic Heraclitean character while retaining its essentially timeless Parmenidean nature, and, not surprisingly, he concludes that it cannot. Strangely, or perhaps not so strangely, contemporary Parmenideans, such as Mellor, who argue that McTaggart's A-paradox deals a lethal blow to all dynamic theories, do not draw attention to this point.

The Block Universe

Time without Passage

Many contemporary philosophers are convinced that McTaggart was essentially correct: our world is a block-like four-dimensional ensemble, lacking a moving present, wherein all times and events are equally real. However, few if any of these philosophers follow McTaggart in holding that time is unreal.

According to contemporary Block or B-theorists, what we call "time" is simply the dimension that exists in addition to the three dimensions of space, allowing for the properties of things to change. A thing "changes" by having different properties at different locations in this additional dimension. Since not everything happens at once, and things do change, there clearly is such a dimension, and so time is undeniably real.

McTaggart concluded that time was unreal because he believed that time essentially involves a moving present, and that no such thing could exist. Modern B-theorists take a different approach. While they agree with McTaggart that there cannot be a moving present, they argue that this signifies a discovery about the real nature of time. We are accustomed to advances in knowledge overturning commonly held beliefs about how things are. For example, the Earth does not seem to move, but it turns out that it does; similarly, time seems to pass, but it turns out that, in a certain sense, it does not.

Where is the difference? This chapter and the next will focus on an examination of the Block view of time. The theory is not assumed to be true. While there are powerful arguments against the view that A-properties are transitory intrinsics, there are also dynamic models of time that take a different form. We cannot conclude that the B-theory is true unless and until these alternative accounts prove untenable. However, there are good reasons for taking a closer look at the B-theory before considering these wider issues.

Of all the models of time considered, the B-theory is currently the most popular among philosophers and physicists. However, in many respects, it is also the strangest and most counterintuitive. Most people believe that time passes; they believe there is an objective difference between the past, present, and future. B-theorists maintain that these beliefs are false. Like anyone seeking to defend a view that runs counter to common sense, the B-theorist faces two tasks: one positive and one negative. The negative task is to provide reasons for thinking that the common-sense beliefs in question are false. McTaggart's A-paradox provides one such reason, but other B-theorists lean more on scientific considerations. The positive task is more complex. B-theorists must provide a clear account of the nature of time and change, and also convince us that this account could be true.

Most people believe that time passes because it seems to; this belief is not merely sustained by custom or tradition. There are features of our experience and features of the world itself that provide seemingly good reasons for holding this belief. Hence, the B-theorist must explain why so many of us have false beliefs about the nature of time and show that, surprisingly, nothing in our everyday experience requires us to hold that there is an objective difference between the past, present, and future. This is no mean task, and although it will occupy us for this chapter and the next, it will be returned to in Chapter 7.

Just as different B-theorists take different approaches to the negative task, they also approach the more complex positive task in various ways. In parts of this chapter, Mellor's work will be drawn on. Although influential, not all B-theorists agree with all aspects of his position, and on occasion, some divergences will be indicated.

Passage and Experience

Common sense tells us something like this: Only the present is fully real; the future is wholly non-existent and as yet unfixed (in the sense that it is not yet determined what will happen), whereas the past is fixed but not real in the same way as the present; the present is constantly advancing. This picture of time is imprecise and, upon reflection, puzzling, but it is also very appealing. Let’s call it the "natural view."

It is not surprising that we find this view appealing for it seems firmly grounded in the character of our everyday experience. Someone trying to describe the most general temporal features of everyday experience might well conclude with something like the following: We have a great deal of highly detailed knowledge of what happened in the past but little or none about what will happen in the future except in highly stable and regular systems whose behavior can be accurately predicted. We can anticipate experiences that we will have in the future and we can remember experiences that we had in the past, but only present experiences are fully and properly real; only sensations that are occurring now are actually felt.

The passage of time is something we can directly observe in the world; we can see one event happening after another, such as a car moving along a street, passing one house, then another, then another. Deliberation and choice are oriented toward the future. I am now, as always, in the position of having to decide what I will do—never what I was doing. We cannot change or affect the past, but by choosing to do one thing rather than another, we can help determine what happens in the future.

If this is how we are inclined to describe our experience, the appeal of the natural view of time is readily understandable: the two fit together perfectly. The idea that only present events are fully real is supported by the confinement of experience to the "now." The fact that we can perceive things changing and find the experienced present to be continually advancing into the future reinforces the idea that the present is continually advancing. Our epistemic access to the past, in a way that we do not have to the future, supports the fixed past and unfixed future doctrine.

While everyday experience makes the appeal of the natural view understandable, the real question is whether there is anything inherently incompatible with the competing B-conception of time. B-theorists argue that there is not. The discussion now turns to what the B-theorist can say about two aspects of the natural view that concern experience.

The Presence of Experience

Consider first the idea that we can only experience what is present. If we take this to mean "we can only perceive what is present," then this claim is simply false. Light is fast, but not infinitely so. When you look at the Sun, you are seeing it as it was eight minutes or so ago; when you look at a star, you may well be seeing it as it was millions of years ago. Whether a thing is past, present, or future does not make any difference to how it looks: we cannot, for example, refute someone who claims to see the future in a crystal ball by pointing to the visible pastness of the image; there is no such thing."Our reasons for thinking we cannot see the future rest not on observation but on theory" (Mellor, 1998: 16). This truth, which becomes obvious once our attention is drawn to it, points the way to a more promising formulation of the "confinement doctrine": the objects we perceive may not be present, but the sensory experiences we have when we perceive them certainly are.

But are they? Let us suppose (with the B-theorist) that all events are equally real. It follows that all experiences are equally real: your pain five days ago is just as real as your pain today and your pain next week. From this perspective, it is clearly not true that experience is "confined to the present." What is true is this straightforward tautology: an experience happens when it happens. Every experience occurs at some time and is "restricted" to the time at which it occurs, but this is true of everything that is located in time. It does not follow from this tautology that the only experiences are those occurring at the time in question. This would only follow if earlier and later events are unreal.

Thus, it seems that the notion that experience is confined to the present does not support or confirm the doctrine that past and future are unreal; it presupposes this doctrine. Even so, there is something that needs explaining. Why do we feel that only present experience is truly felt, and hence that experience occurs only in the present?

Imagine waking up to find yourself in a strange place. You are sitting in a field of grass, next to a lamp that illuminates the surrounding area. There is complete silence. As you look around, you can see nothing at all. Apart from the small patch of grass illuminated by the lamp, there is darkness everywhere. Not surprisingly, you conclude that you are alone. However, you could not be more wrong. A few yards to your right, there is another lamp, and another person is also waking up to find themselves surrounded by total darkness. Likewise, to your left, there is yet another person. In fact, you are in a line of people stretching for many miles in either direction, all of whom are sitting in their own small pools of light, each alarmed to find themselves alone in a strange place.

Why is it that nobody can see anyone else? The answer lies with the strange form of light emitted by the lamps, which only extends a few feet before fading away. According to the B-theorist, we find ourselves in an analogous position in time.

What stretches only a short distance is not light through space but consciousness over time: the temporal span of direct awareness is very brief. And, as in the analogous spatial case, the fact that at any given time we are not aware of experiences occurring at other times does not mean that these experiences are not there.

According to the B-theorist, the impression we have that reality is confined to a very brief present is partly due to the fact that the span of our direct awareness, or "specious present," is very brief. The "lived present" of a being with a wider span of awareness, such as an alien, would include times we would regard as past and future.

How can the B-theorist account for the experience we have of time passing if time doesn't really pass? According to many B-theorists, Mellor included, the solution lies in the ways our memories accumulate. Suppose you are in a dentist's waiting room. Every few minutes, you take an anxious glance at the clock. Although the minute hand of this clock looks stationary, it is in a different position every time you look at it. Your first glance shows it to be 10:15. When you next look, you see it is 10:25; a while later, you see that it is 10:29. Is this all you experience? Very probably not: in addition to what you perceive, you also have various memories of what you have recently been doing. So, for example, when you see that it is 10:25, you also remember seeing the clock showing it to be 10:15; when you see the clock showing 10:29, you remember observing it showing 10:25 while also remembering having seen it showing 10:15. According to the B-theorist, our sense that time is passing is due to the fact that our perceptions and memories combine in this sort of way, not only over the span of a few seconds or minutes, but over days, months, and years.

On the face of it, this account seems quite plausible. Vary the scenario and suppose you are suffering from Korsakoff syndrome: you forget everything that happens to you within a minute or so. When you see the clock showing 10:25, not only do you have no memory of having seen it showing 10:15, but you have no recollection of what you were doing two minutes earlier. Likewise, when you see the clock showing 10:29. In this memory-impoverished condition, what sense would you have that time is passing?

Memory also plays a part in explaining why we feel that our lives unfold in the direction of the future. Even if our future experiences are just as real as our past ones, as the B-theorist maintains, at any given point during our lives we have detailed knowledge only of the earlier parts of our life; indeed, for all we know, we might be about to drop dead, and so not have a future at all. It is thus easy to believe that our lives don't extend beyond the present, even if in fact they do.
