This belief leads to another: that any continuation of our lives involves the addition of something new to the already completed past. Since we remember having these same beliefs at past stages of our lives and anticipate having them in the future, we always have the impression of living at the outermost limit of an ongoing future-directed process.

Whether this memory-based account of experiencing passage is entirely adequate—especially over the very short term—is something we will be looking into in more detail in Chapter 7. It is enough for now to recognize that the B-theorist has at least the beginnings of a plausible account, one that does not require a moving present or the non-existence of times other than the present. All that it requires is for people to have different memories at different stages of their lives.

There is a further reason why it is natural to believe that there must be an objective difference between past, present, and future, one that we have not yet considered. Quite generally, when a claim about the world is true, there is something about the world, a fact that obtains, that makes the claim true. This fact is the claim's "truthmaker." If I say "Canada is larger than France," the relevant truthmaker is the fact that Canada extends over a greater area of the Earth's surface than France does. Suppose I say, "The battle of Hastings is in the past." I am making an A-statement, and this statement is clearly true, but what is its truthmaker? The obvious candidate is the tensed A-fact that Hastings took place in the past. Likewise for other A-statements. Not all A-statements are true, but many are. If those that are true have tensed A-facts as truthmakers, it seems that we live in an A-world after all.

How can the B-theorist respond to this? Simply denying that there are any true A-statements is bold, heroic even, but hopelessly implausible. One response, which dates back to Russell in 1915, is to hold that A-statements do not require A-facts as truthmakers because they are equivalent in meaning to B-statements. If this were the case, the absence of any A-facts would not matter, since there are B-facts, and these could serve as truthmakers for both sorts of statement. This approach is nowadays widely known as the "old tenseless theory," and it is generally agreed to fail: A-statements cannot be translated into B-statements without loss of meaning.

It is worth pausing to consider some of the difficulties involved in such translations. It seems clear that two statements cannot have the same meaning if one can be true when the other one is false. Consider:

"S1: Event E is past."  
"S2: Event E is earlier than t."

S1 is a typical A-statement; S2 is a B-statement. It is easy to see that these can have different truth-values: if S2 is true, it is true at all times, whereas S1 is only true when E is in the past. A-statements have time-varying truth-values; they are true at some times but not at others, whereas B-statements have time-invariant truth-values. This alone seems to show that A- and B-statements differ in meaning.

But there are further difficulties. Suppose the following is uttered at 3:25 p.m.:

"S3: Bill is happy now."

We might try to translate S3 by the B-sentence:

"S4: Bill is happy at 3:25 p.m."

There are two reasons for rejecting the claim that S3 and S4 have the same meanings. First, the two sentences employ different concepts. Since someone might understand "now" but have no grasp at all of what "occurs at 3:25 p.m." means, how could S3 and S4 have the same meaning? Secondly, someone could understand both S3 and S4, but nonetheless believe S3 to be true while believing S4 to be false. How is this possible? Simple. The person in question sees that Bill has a broad grin on his face but mistakenly believes that it is 12 noon.

Advocates of the "new tenseless theory" of time, such as Smart, Mellor, and many others, agree that A-claims cannot be translated into B-claims, but argue that this doesn't matter: A-claims can nonetheless have tenseless B-truthmakers, or truth-conditions. Here is how Mellor has recently stated the case.Mellor distinguishes between beliefs, statements of beliefs, the sentences that are used in stating beliefs, and the contents of beliefs, which he calls "propositions," and takes to be what sentences expressing beliefs mean. Thus, when Jack says "Bill is happy" and Jacques says "Bill est heureux," they are using different sentences to make the same claim or statement; that is, they are using different sentences to express the same proposition and have beliefs with the same contents.

On the face of it, it is not easy to see how an A-belief could have a B-truthmaker. A-beliefs are true at some times but not at others, whereas B-beliefs always have the same truth-value. For example:

B1: Jim races tomorrow.  
B2: Jim races on 2 June.

B2 is always true, but, assuming that Jim does indeed race on 2 June, B1 is only true on 1 June. Clearly, since the truth-value of any given B-belief is constant across time, whereas an A-belief such as "Jim races tomorrow" is true at some times and false at others, no single B-fact can make such an A-belief true. How many B-beliefs does it take? The answer, it turns out, is a great many: roughly speaking, a different B-fact is required for each different time at which a given A-belief can be true or false. How can this be? What are the relevant B-facts? How can a single A-belief have many different B-truthmakers?

The solution relies on the type-token distinction. A token of a type is a particular instance, specimen, or exemplar of it. For example, Mary is a token of the type humanity; "the" and "the" are two tokens of the same type of word, "the." Just as the type-token distinction can be applied to words, it can be applied to beliefs, sentences, and statements.

Our problem was that a given A-belief type can have different truth-values at different times, so no A-belief type can have a single B-truthmaker. However, when we switch our attention to tokens of A-beliefs, a way forward opens up. A token of an A-belief is a particular person holding a particular belief at a particular time. The fact that a person holds a given belief at a certain time is a B-fact and hence holds at all times. Consequently, all we need is a way of stating the conditions under which a token A-belief is true in tenseless B-terms. There are two general strategies available, which Mellor sketches in these terms:

* The token-reflexive account: For every A-proposition P about any event E, any token of P is true if and only if it is as much earlier or later than E as P says the present is than E.

The Date Account

For every A-proposition P about any event E, any token of P is made true at any time t by t's being as much earlier or later than E as P says the present is than E. Both accounts seem to give the right results. Consider the A-proposition "E occurred yesterday." My saying "E occurred yesterday" is a token of this proposition. The first account tells us that this A-statement is true if and only if E does indeed occur a day earlier than the token itself. It is because this account makes reference to the token itself that it is token-reflexive. The second account says that if I make this statement on June 2, then what I am saying is true if and only if E occurs on June 1. Analogous considerations apply for the A-belief "E is occurring now." According to the token-reflexive account, a token of this belief is true if and only if it occurs when E occurs—that is, the token itself is neither earlier nor later than E. The date account yields the result that, if the belief is tokened on June 2, it is true if and only if E also occurs on June 2.

Underlying these differences is a crucial similarity: both accounts state the conditions under which tokens of A-propositions (and hence A-beliefs, A-statements, etc.) are true in entirely B-terms. In *Real Time*, Mellor preferred the token-reflexive account; in *Real Time II*, he prefers the date theory to handle certain tricky cases that critics have developed in the intervening years. We do not need to go into these details here. The important point is that the B-theorist has a plausible strategy for explaining how A-statements and beliefs can be true even if there are no A-facts to make them true.

Another A-Paradox

Mellor takes a further step in arguing that if we try to supply A-sentences (statements, beliefs, etc.) with tensed truthmakers, we end up in a paradoxical situation, and consequently, we have no option but to accept that A-statements have tenseless truthmakers. More specifically, Mellor argues that if tensed sentences have tensed truth-conditions, the statements that we make using these sentences will undergo changes in their truth-value—changes that do not in fact occur.

By way of an example, suppose that on Monday I see a man with short hair, and I mistakenly believe that this man is John, who has worn his hair long for many years. I exclaim, "So, John has short hair now." Call this claim C. In actual fact, at this time, John has long hair, so C is false. Suppose that it is now a month later, and John has just taken the plunge, visited the barber, and emerged with short hair. Is C now true? Of course not. The claim that I made a month ago remains false. If I were now to claim "John has short hair now," my claim would be true, but the claim I made a month ago—that very claim—was false then and is false now. Mellor’s allegation is that if tensed statements have tensed truthmakers, then statements such as C would change their truth-value.

The argument runs thus: Suppose a tensed sentence S = "E is happening now" has a tensed truthmaker, the obvious candidate being the fact that E is now happening. The tensed theorist will thus say: Any token of S = "E is happening now" is true if and only if E is happening now. In other words, all tokens of the tensed sentence S, irrespective of when they occur, are made true by this tensed fact, and hence if this fact does obtain, then all tokens of S are true, irrespective of when they occur. Suppose that on Monday I say, "It is now Tuesday." Call this statement ST. Applying the tensed account, this statement is true if and only if it is now Tuesday. Since it is now Monday, the statement is false. However, a day later, on Tuesday, the truthmaker for ST now obtains, so the statement in question is now true: ST has undergone a change of truth-value!

Mellor is certainly right in maintaining that we do not normally suppose that truth-value changes of this sort occur. Moreover, they do not occur if the truthmakers of tensed statements are of the tenseless sort considered above: for example, if a token of "It is now Tuesday" is true if and only if the token in question is uttered on a Tuesday, then a token of this sentence uttered on Monday remains false even on Tuesday. So the idea that tensed claims are made true by tensed facts does look to be in difficulty. Whether this difficulty is insuperable is another matter. In section 5.1, we will encounter a proposal for a cure.

The Indispensability of the A-Framework

If there are no A-facts, why do we constantly think in A-terms? Mellor argues that the A-framework, in particular A-beliefs, are of vital practical importance even though there are no A-facts. Beliefs contribute to our actions; what we do is a product of what we desire or want and what we believe. If I want an ice cream and believe that there is ice cream in the fridge, then I will go to the fridge to get some ice cream; my belief helps direct my action in a way appropriate to satisfy my wants. Of course, my wants will only be satisfied if there really is ice cream in the fridge: successful action requires true belief.

Now, it is very often the case that the success of an action depends on when it is carried out. If I want to buy some food from the shops, then my visit to the shops will only be successful if I go when they are open. I have to act at the right time. Suppose that I believe (truly) that the shops are open between 8 a.m. and 8 p.m. Since this B-belief is always true, it cannot successfully guide my actions; success depends on my going to the shops at some times but not others. Clearly, the belief that will successfully guide me must be true at some times but not others; it must be an A-belief. An example of the right sort of belief would be "It is now 3 p.m." The belief "If it were 3 p.m., then the shops would be open," a B-belief, will not do, for although this belief is true, it is always true, and so it cannot guide timely action.

To act at the appropriate times, we must have A-beliefs that are constantly updated to reflect the different times at which they are held. I should believe "The shops are open now" between 8 a.m. and 8 p.m., but not at other times. I should believe "The banks are closed today" only on days when the banks are indeed closed. How do we update our A-beliefs? Usually by using our senses. To put it crudely, we believe what we see, and, cases of known time-lag apart, we believe that what we see is happening as we see it—that is, happening now. If I look at my watch and it says "4 p.m.," then I believe "It is now 4 p.m." and act accordingly.

Again, there is a good deal more to be said about these topics, but the important point is that we can understand why the A-framework is indispensable for practical purposes without assuming that there are any A-facts. The account just sketched appeals only to B-facts and B-relationships. A-beliefs play an ineliminable role in successful timely actions, but, as we have seen, B-facts can serve as truthmakers for A-beliefs.

Questions of Attitude

Experiences that we are currently undergoing are of more concern to us while we are undergoing them than experiences we have yet to have, and experiences we have already had.This is understandable: if I am now in pain, this present pain is real to me now in a way that my past and future pains are not. But we also have differing attitudes to past and future experiences. Although I suffered horribly at the dentist last week, this fact doesn’t much bother me at all. If someone were to say, "Give me £10 and I will change the past, and make it so that pain never occurred," I would not be inclined to pay, even if I believed that the past could be changed in this way. But if someone were to say, "Give me £100 and I will ensure that you feel no pain when you visit the dentist next week," I might well be tempted by the offer, especially if this were the only way of avoiding the pain in question. Quite generally, our future experiences are of far greater concern to us than our past experiences, especially where the experiences in question are painful or pleasurable.

Perhaps the most dramatic example of our bias towards the future is Lucretius's observation that while death—our own future non-existence—is something we deeply dread, the vast periods of personal non-existence preceding our births are of no concern to us whatsoever. Some A-theorists have held that this asymmetry in our attitudes is problematic for the B-theory. The best-known argument originated with Prior (1959): After several long hours of torment, a fierce headache at last fades away, and you think to yourself, "Thank goodness that's over!" What is it that you are expressing relief about? Could it be the tenseless fact that this thought occurs later than the headache in question? According to the B-theorist, this tenseless fact makes the thought true, but this seems plainly false: the trouble is that this was as much a fact before and during the headache as it is now. It always was and always will be a fact that this particular thought occurs later than the headache it refers to.

Furthermore, that fact could have been recognized in advance. You could have decided to say "That's over" after the headache and known that fact beforehand. So if that were the fact you were thankful for, you could just as well have been thankful before or during the headache, which is nonsense. It seems that you must be thankful for some other fact, something that was not a fact until the headache ceased. The tensed conclusion appears irresistible; the pastness of the headache is an extra fact over and above the tenseless fact that makes "That's over" true.

A straightforward response to this objection is to agree that the fact you are thankful for is indeed a tensed fact. Mellor (1994) recognizes that there are tensed facts, at least in the trivial sense that every true statement states a fact. However, it is also possible to hold that the relevant tensed fact is identical to—or refers to the same state of affairs as—a tenseless fact.

Why do we thank goodness for the tensed fact but not the tenseless fact? Consider Lois Lane, who believes Superman is a superhero but does not believe that Clark Kent is a superhero, although Superman and Clark Kent are the same person. Our beliefs are attitudes toward objects or states of affairs represented in a certain way, and the same object can be represented differently. The same applies to attitudes such as dread and relief. Trapped in a burning building, Lois Lane would feel relieved if she thought Superman were about to arrive; the thought that Clark Kent would arrive would leave her indifferent.

However, recognizing that the same object can be represented differently only solves part of the problem. It is clear why Lois Lane has the attitudes she does: she believes Superman to be a superhero and Clark Kent to be a mild-mannered reporter. It is less obvious why we tend to care more about states of affairs when characterized in tensed rather than tenseless terms. Why do we have such different attitudes toward the past and future?

Do we care more about our future experiences because we can still do something about them, while the past is beyond our control? No. We dread future pains that we believe are inevitable in just the same way as those we believe are avoidable. Imagine yourself in prison, facing inescapable torture this afternoon. As Parfit notes, "Our ground for concern about such [inevitable] future pains is not that, unlike past pains, we can affect them. We know that we cannot affect them. We are concerned about these future pains simply because they are not yet in the past."

That we care more about future pains just because they are future may seem to pose a problem for the Block theorist. If there is no ontological difference between past and future—if all experiences are equally real—what could justify our bias toward the future? While it is not obvious how this bias could be justified in the context of the Block view, this is only a problem if the bias in question is justified. It may be rooted in nothing more than irrational instincts implanted in us by natural selection.

An evolutionary explanation is that natural selection has ensured we are motivated to act in ways that enhance our survival prospects; since we can only influence our futures, we have an overriding concern with the future compared to the past.

However, even adopting a tensed or dynamic conception of time may not change things. Why should we care less about pains that were present than pains that will become present? What about pastness makes experiences less worthy of concern?

B-theorists can also note that our "bias towards the future" is less straightforward than it seems. As Gallois (1994) points out, our bias is not simply toward the future. For example, if you were to travel back to the last ice age, the thought of enduring terrible cold might fill you with dread; but those experiences lie in the past of the world, not the future. Rather, they lie in your personal future, a reference frame determined by the ordering of your life stages.

Parfit's case of the "past or future suffering of those we love" exposes a different ambiguity:

"I am an exile from some country, where I have left my widowed mother. Though I am deeply concerned about her, I very seldom get news. I have known for some time that she is fatally ill and cannot live long. I am now told something new: my mother's illness has become very painful in a way that drugs cannot relieve. For the next few months, before she dies, she faces a terrible ordeal. That she will soon die, I already knew. But I am deeply distressed to learn of the suffering that she must endure.

A day later, I am told that I had been partly misinformed. The facts were right, but not the timing. My mother did have many months of suffering, but she is now dead."

If past pains are of no significance, I should be relieved. But am I? Obviously not. The tragedy for my mother is just as great; she suffered just as much from her illness, and I am just as upset on her account. So it seems that, where other people are concerned—especially those we love—the timing of any pains they suffer is of little significance.

This striking self-other asymmetry poses a problem for anyone seeking to justify the bias toward the future in the first-person case. Might the pattern of concern we display toward others be more rational than our self-oriented concern? More rational because it is less influenced by instinctive self-preservation? If so, it may be unreasonable for us to care greatly about our own future experiences while being largely indifferent to our past experiences. The rational pattern of self-concern, if such there is one, may be entirely in tune with the Block view of time.

3.7 B-theories of change

In its simplest form, the B-theory of change states that an object changes by having different properties at different times. McTaggart objected that a poker's being hot at one time and cold at another doesn't amount to change, because such facts always obtain. The B-theorist responds by asking why some changeless facts can't be changes. If the poker is hot at one time and cold at another, why can't these facts constitute a change in the poker? Does it matter that these facts are and always will be facts? McTaggart denies that the B-theorist can talk of times at all without A-change. Since this assumption is precisely what B-theorists reject, it does not trouble them.

While agreeing on these fundamentals, B-theorists often disagree when it comes to making sense of change. Objects like trees, people, and houses are often thought to be different from events like concerts or battles because of the kinds of parts they have. Since a battle typically covers a large area, it has many spatial parts...What is going on at different places at a given time also has temporal parts. To put it crudely, it has a beginning, a middle, and an end, and these different phases are as much parts of the battle as any of its spatial components.

Ordinary material objects seem to be different. While a typical person, such as Alice, has various spatial parts (her hands, feet, bones, organs, etc.), it seems wrong to think she has temporal parts in the same way as a battle. Alice exists now, and she existed yesterday, but it seems that Alice in her entirety exists at both these times. If you met Alice, you didn't meet just part of her; you met the whole thing—Alice herself. Alice-yesterday and Alice-today are not two parts of a person; they are each a whole person in their own right. Moreover, they are the very same person: Alice-yesterday and Alice-today are numerically identical (the very same object), even though they are very likely qualitatively different (they don't have exactly the same properties).

This distinction between objects (or substances, continuants) and events comes very naturally to us, and some B-theorists, such as Mellor, not only subscribe to it, but they deploy it in defending themselves against McTaggart's charge that the B-theory reduces change to mere spatial variation. Mellor points out that change is something that a single persisting thing undergoes. Events cannot change in this way. Suppose a performance of a symphony is loud at one time and quiet at another. What we have here are two distinct entities—the two temporal parts of the symphony—possessing different properties. Likewise, Jim's being happy yesterday and Alice's being sad today doesn't amount to a change; it is a case of two distinct objects having different properties. Alice's being happy yesterday and sad today is a change.

Entities such as Alice can change precisely because they do not possess temporal parts. Now consider a typical case of spatial variation. If a stick is red at one end and white at the other, this isn't change; rather than one and the same entity (the whole stick) possessing different properties, we have two distinct parts of the stick possessing different properties—that is, two numerically different entities. If we were to say that the stick as a whole is red and white, we would be saying something false, since there are parts of the stick that aren't red and parts that aren't white. Hence we have a clear difference between change and spatial variation: the latter involves numerically distinct entities possessing different properties, whereas the former involves numerically identical entities possessing different properties. Mellor takes a further step. He argues that persistence involves causation. Alice's present condition is, in part, a causal consequence of her preceding condition. More generally, if an object at one time is numerically identical with the same object at an earlier time, then the later object must be causally dependent upon the earlier one, either directly or indirectly. Since causation operates over time but not space (Mellor argues against the possibility of causes being simultaneous with their effects), we find an additional distinction between genuine change and spatial variation. Change requires persistence; persistence requires causation. Since time alone is the dimension of causation, time alone is the dimension of change. Thus, the allegation that the B-theory obliterates the distinction between time and space (or change and mere variation) is successfully rebutted.

Mellor's account of change aims to be non-revisionary. He effectively asserts something like this: The B-theory of time may strike you as very odd when you first encounter it, but it is not that odd, because you can adopt it without having to undertake a radical overhaul of your current conceptual scheme. Other B-theorists take a quite different line. They start from the assumption that if our ordinary ways of thinking about time are mistaken, it may well be that other parts of our ordinary conceptual scheme are mistaken too. They argue that if the B-theory is true, some natural assumptions about what persistence involves turn out to be false, leading to a revisionary account of change that looks very appealing.

Consider a spatially extended physical object, such as a worm. Suppose this worm has ten segments; although some of these segments may be quite similar to each other, each is nonetheless a distinct object (a worm-segment) in its own right. If we think of a persisting object from the four-dimensional perspective of the B-theory, a similar picture emerges.

The diagram (not shown here) depicts Alice as an extended four-dimensional object composed of parts. Some of these parts are of the familiar spatial sort: at one time, Alice can be divided into a head, two arms, two legs, and a torso. However, it is clear that Alice also has other parts too. While Alice exists at one time, she also exists at another time (and other times as well); it is surely wrong to say that Alice-in-her-entirety exists at just one time. If you see Alice at that time, you are not seeing the whole Alice; you are merely seeing the part of her that exists at that time (and place). She has many other parts that exist at other times, which you do not see. The common-sense assumption that when you see a person on a given occasion, you are seeing the whole person—and hence that the person is wholly and entirely present before you—would be true only if the present time were real, and many of us do commonly assume this. But if we adopt the B-theory, this assumption is false. Since all times are equally real, people and other persisting things do not exist only in the present; they also extend into the past and future.

If persisting objects do have temporal parts, the difference between change and spatial variation, which Mellor stresses, evaporates. If Alice-at-one-time and Alice-at-another-time have different properties, these properties are not possessed by one and the same object but by two numerically distinct objects called person-stages. These objects are indeed parts of a single thing, the whole temporally extended Alice, but so too are the red and white ends of a stick, or the front-end and the rear-end of a worm. This reasoning can be contested, but I will not delve further into the intricacies of this particular debate, which is primarily important in the metaphysics of identity and objecthood. Instead, I want to mention a different and arguably more profound way in which the B-theory can be revisionary to varying degrees.

Emergent time

If we choose to think of ordinary objects as four-dimensional entities possessing temporal parts, change becomes more akin to spatial variation. But does it become spatial variation? Not if the temporal dimension of the four-dimensional block universe differs in some way from the three spatial dimensions, in a way that renders the distinction between time and space well-founded. Many B-theorists assume that there is such a difference. Some, like Mellor, believe that causality runs only along the temporal axis. Others, who are skeptical of causality as a time-distinguishing feature, look instead to physics. Although relativity theory posits a four-dimensional manifold or continuum of points, this manifold is not simply a four-dimensional space, and the distinction between space and time is maintained, albeit in an altered form.

However, this may change. Relativistic gravitational theory and quantum theory have yet to be combined in an entirely adequate way, and the search for a satisfactory theory of quantum gravity continues. Some physicists believe that the distinction between space and time will not survive in quantum gravity theory, for the simple reason that this theory will not mention time at all. Rovelli writes that in quantum gravity, there is no reason to expect a fundamental notion of time to play any role. The time along which things happen is a notion that makes sense only for describing a limited regime of reality. At the fundamental level, we should simply forget time. This is, of course, well-informed speculation—there are plenty of physicists who take a different view—but suppose Rovelli turns out to be correct. Belot and Earman suggest that the metaphysical consequences would be considerable.

They argue that there is no change in such a world, since no physically real quantity evolves in time. Change is illusory. This is a very radical thesis—it is much stronger than the doctrine that there is no room for temporal becoming in a relativistic world, since proponents of the tenseless theory of time are confident that they can account for the existence of change.

If change is illusory, then so too is time, or so McTaggart would argue, and it would be hard to disagree.

But does it follow from the hypothetical disappearance of time as an independent variable from the equations of fundamental physics that change is unreal? Only if we take fundamental physics to be our sole guide in deciding which, if any, dimension of the universe can legitimately be called time. Not all B-theorists would want to take this step, as this passage from D. C. Williams’s well-known exposition of the Block theory clearly displays:

It is plain that things, persons, and events, as a matter of natural fact, are strung along with respect to the time axis in patterns notably different from those in which they are deployed in space. The very concept of things or individual substances derives from a peculiar kind of coherence and elongation of clumps of events in the time direction. Living bodies in particular have a special organized trend timewise, a conatus sese conservandi, which nothing has in spatial section.There are distinctive patterns of development and organization found only along the temporal axis. When asked to envisage one of these patterns—a whole human life, from birth to death—being distributed across space as it is usually across time, we simply don't know where to start.

There may be universes where this does not hold—where people's streams of consciousness run alongside roads and garden paths. In such universes, there might be no rationale for maintaining a general distinction between time and space. However, our universe is not like this. Given the asymmetrical ways in which the contents of our universe are distributed, the distinction between temporal and spatial dimensions is well-founded. This distinction, even if not "primitive" or "aboriginal," depends entirely on the actual order of the world's contents.

By saying the distinction is "well founded," I mean it is grounded in objective facts concerning the asymmetrical patterning and distribution of events. This is undeniably true at the macroscopic, introspectible levels in this region of our universe, and future developments in physics are irrelevant to this distinction. To this extent, time is real rather than illusory, and the same applies to change.

### The Block Universe

If it turns out that time is absent from the fundamental equations of physics, this will be significant. There would be a clear sense in which time is not a fundamental feature of the universe, especially if the basic ingredients of the physical world can exist without producing the asymmetrical patterns that distinguish space and time. In that case, time would be an emergent phenomenon, much like macroscopic objects such as planets and animals, which "emerge" when elementary particles are arranged in certain ways. But even if time is emergent, its existence is hardly threatened at the macroscopic level, where the spatial-temporal distinction remains real. After all, cats are emergent entities; are they illusory?

According to Belot and Earman, the idea that time is not physically fundamental is stronger than the idea that "temporal becoming" is unreal. This is debatable. The truly astonishing claim is that time does not pass and all moments are equally real—this challenges our ordinary intuition. The notion that the space-time distinction is emergent relative to fundamental physics is less radical. If time can survive in the metaphysical framework of the block universe, it can certainly survive discoveries from fundamental physics.

### 4 Asymmetries Within Time

#### 4.1 The Direction of Time

A French nursery rhyme says: "L'eau est comme le temps: il coule, il coule, il coule." If time really flowed, like a river, it would have a direction. But if the B-theory is true, time does not go anywhere: there is no moving "now," no passage into the past, no crystallization from possibility into actuality. Every moment and event is equally real. So why does time seem to have a direction?

B-theorists explain that time does not flow or pass, but asymmetries among the contents of time—the material and mental events—give time its apparent direction. These asymmetries are mistaken for temporal flow.

Whether these asymmetries suffice to give time itself direction depends on how time is conceived. If time is an entity over and above its contents, it could be perfectly symmetrical and lack directedness, even if its contents do not. For example, consider a clear glass cylinder that changes color due to an asymmetric colored sheet inside. The glass itself is symmetrical, but the embedded sheet is not. If we identify times with collections of events, then asymmetries in content amount to asymmetries in time itself.

There are further complexities. Time might be asymmetrical even if its contents are not—for example, time could be infinite in one direction or branch differently in each direction. Alternatively, content-asymmetries might be local and not define a direction to time as a whole. Imagine the cylinder is blue at both ends and red in the middle, symmetrically; then time would lack a global direction though local time might seem directed.

We will consider such a universe later. For now, however, our focus is on observable content-asymmetries in everyday life. So far, we have only briefly touched on one family of these asymmetries: those linked to the psychological arrow of time. We feel that we live in the present moment, and that the present advances into the future. B-theorists explain this impression via factors like changing beliefs and memory accumulation. But other aspects of the psychological arrow and further time asymmetries remain to be explained.

If all conscious beings vanished, nobody would have the subjective impression that time passes. Yet many asymmetries within time would remain unnoticed. For example, using time-lapse photography compresses weeks or years into minutes. Suppose someone made a time-lapse film of your garden over the past months. Would you be able to tell whether the film was running forwards or backwards? Of course you would.If the film were running backwards, you would see large plants shrinking rapidly down to small seedlings, smudges of slush concentrating into ripe plums that suddenly leap up and attach themselves to a tree branch, paw-prints gradually appearing in mud before a cat walks backwards, rings of water converging towards the center of a pond from which a ball rises and flies over a fence into the next garden, and so forth. A film running backwards shows events in the wrong order; later events occur before earlier events.

Given that we can easily tell whether a film depicting everyday sequences is running backwards, there must be certain sequences of events that are always or typically ordered the same way with respect to earlier and later moments. This suggests there is an objective difference between earlier and later times—one that does not depend on our psychological perspective. In this chapter, we examine what B-theorists say about such asymmetries. While all B-theorists agree there are some objective asymmetries, they differ on exactly what these are. Some argue that many content-asymmetries we think are objective are actually products of our distinctive perspectives on a more symmetrical temporal world.

### 4.2 Content-Asymmetries: A Fuller Picture

Before considering the B-theorist’s view of various content-asymmetries, it will help to list some of them. These brief descriptions are intended to provide a rough idea of each asymmetry:

1. Entropic Asymmetry: The second law of thermodynamics states that entropy, or disorder, increases over time.

2. Causal Asymmetry: Some events cause others; causes generally occur earlier than their effects.

3. Fork Asymmetry: Many later events often correlate with or result from a single earlier event (e.g., everyone who ate fish at lunch later falls ill). The reverse, where one later event correlates with many earlier events, is much rarer.

4. Explanatory Asymmetry: We explain later events in terms of earlier events (e.g., "Jim died because he drank poison," not the other way around).

5. Knowledge Asymmetry: We often have detailed and reliable knowledge of past events but much less about the future.

6. Action Asymmetry: Our deliberations focus on the future, not the past; our actions can affect the future but not the past.

7. Experience Asymmetry: We experience life as unfolding in a present that moves inexorably forward toward the future.

These asymmetries are interconnected, though exactly how is debated. Different B-theorists have varying views about which are fundamental or dependent on others.

What exactly must the B-theorist explain? A B-theorist might say: "I don't need to explain these asymmetries; they may be brute facts about time’s content distribution." But what does the A-theorist offer as an explanation? If temporal passage exists, it could exist in worlds quite different from ours, with no content-asymmetries or with asymmetries differently oriented. Positing an objective difference between past, present, and future does not explain anything; nor does temporal passage itself.

This is a plausible position, but the B-theorist should adopt it only as a last resort. Explaining some or all content-asymmetries would make B-theory more credible. Moreover, A-theorists can roughly explain some important asymmetries.

For example, discovering last week’s lottery result is easy—just check a newspaper—while finding out next week’s results is not. Past events leave traces on the present; future events do not. If the future is unreal, as some A-theorists claim, this asymmetry is explained: future events cannot influence or leave traces on earlier times; past events can. This explains why there is no backward causation or inverse forks, why our actions influence the future and not the past, and why explanations typically refer to earlier rather than later events. When an event is present, future events cannot influence it.

Can the B-theorist offer similarly plausible explanations?

### 4.3 Entropy

It has often been claimed that understanding time’s direction depends on thermodynamics, specifically the second law stating that entropy tends to increase over time. Some argue entropy asymmetry is the ultimate key, responsible for all other content-asymmetries. Atkins writes:

"We have looked through the window onto the world provided by the Second Law, and have seen the naked purposelessness of nature. The deep structure of change is decay; the spring of change in all its forms is the corruption of the quality of energy as it spreads chaotically, irreversibly, and purposelessly in time. All change, and time's arrow, point in the direction of corruption."

To evaluate this claim, we need to understand what the second law of thermodynamics means. Entropy is a technical concept defined in various ways, but generally it measures how much energy in a system has spread out disorderly through the available space, indicating how close the system is to equilibrium. A system with energy concentrated in a few places is low in entropy; one where energy is evenly spread out is high in entropy. The second law states that in any closed system, energy tends to spread until evenly distributed, reaching equilibrium where it remains.

Currently, the universe has energy concentrated in stars separated by vast cold space, so overall entropy is low. As stars emit energy into space, entropy increases in line with the second law.

The entropic account of the earlier-later distinction is similar, in some respects, to the gravitational account of up and down. Aristotle viewed down as a primitive direction, the same everywhere. Now we understand down as the direction of local gravitational force. There are places with no up or down, and these directions vary by location. Similarly, Boltzmann argues that without local entropic asymmetry, there is no past or future.

Sklar notes the gravitational account is plausible because it explains facts like eggs falling down, balloons floating up, and our ability to feel up and down. Does the entropic account similarly explain the relevant facts?

It can explain why some processes that let us tell if a film is running backwards unfold as they do.If you see a film showing leaves blowing about in the wind and ending up in a neat pile in the middle of the lawn, you can be reasonably certain that the film is running backwards: since this course of events is highly improbable, it is much more likely that the leaves were initially piled up by a human gardener, as human gardeners commonly do such things. What is far less clear is whether any of the other asymmetries listed in 4.2 can be explained in the same sort of way. Would a reversal in entropy also reverse the direction of causation and explanation? Would machines such as clocks run in the opposite direction? Would we have detailed knowledge of what are now future happenings? Would the various components of the psychological arrow reverse their direction? It is certainly not obvious that they would; far from it.

The first point to note is that thermodynamic processes are not, in fact, irreversible. In the nineteenth century, Boltzmann attempted to reconcile the time-asymmetric second law of thermodynamics with the time-symmetric laws of classical mechanics. He showed that the tendency for entropy to rise could be explained in terms of the statistics governing particle interactions. However, as critics pointed out, his explanation also applies in reverse: even if it is overwhelmingly probable that the entropy of a typical system of particles will always increase, there is some small probability that entropy will sometimes decrease. This objection was reinforced in 1889 by Poincaré's "recurrence theorem," which states that particles in any isolated system will, given enough time, return infinitely often to states arbitrarily similar to their initial ones. Boltzmann accepted that so-called irreversible processes are not truly irreversible after all.

Energy in a system tends to spread out; this is by far the most probable way for a low-entropy system to evolve. However, very rarely, temporary decreases in entropy occur through improbable concentrations of matter and energy. These unusual concentrations quickly break down, and equilibrium is restored. More generally, although disorder tends to increase, exceptions can occur; occasionally, bizarre phenomena manifest, such as stirred milk separating again or leaves being blown into neat piles.

Furthermore, any system in a highly ordered, low-entropy state at a given time is likely to be in a more disordered, higher-entropy condition at both earlier and later times. These considerations complicate defining the direction of time solely in terms of entropy. Boltzmann argued that the universe is likely only a tiny part of a much larger whole, most of which is at or near thermodynamic equilibrium. From the perspective of the universe as a whole, stars are localized eruptions of improbable order in a vast sea of disorder. As stars emit energy into space, our region returns to equilibrium; thus, entropy increases overall despite occasional small-scale reversals.

Boltzmann suggested that this regional increase in entropy gives time its direction: earlier states correspond to lower entropy, while later states correspond to higher entropy. Since the universe as a whole is close to equilibrium, its average entropy neither increases nor decreases, and thus, time as a whole may lack direction. Time has direction only in regions where significant entropy gradients exist, such as clusters of galaxies. With the acceptance of Big Bang cosmology, most scientists view the universe as starting in a low-entropy state, with entropy increasing ever since, apart from occasional local reversals.

Setting aside these complications, the entropic account faces another problem. What is the exact connection between the rise of entropy and other observable asymmetries, such as causation, knowledge, and explanation? Extending thermodynamic concepts to systems like playing cards, humans, or printed records involves a significant leap beyond their established domain, and there is no guarantee this extension is valid.

If all content asymmetries were directly caused by local entropy increase, a local decrease in entropy should reverse these asymmetries. However, there is no reason to believe this happens. Many ways exist for entropy in a region to decrease, but most do not seem to affect macroscopic structures, such as people or mountains.

For example, suppose a freak sequence of molecular motions warms the water in the eastern Atlantic Ocean by 20 degrees Celsius above the western half for two weeks, causing entropy in that area to decrease. While this would impact plant and animal life and cause serious weather changes, it would not reverse time. Radio and video transmissions would seem normal, and clocks would continue running forward.

Similarly, if entropy decreases in a region of outer space as particles concentrate unevenly, is there any reason to think time would be experienced in reverse? Since such a region may be almost empty, and you might not interact with anything, there is no reason for changes in the flow of time on your spaceship.

Considering the knowledge asymmetry: a footprint on a sandy shore usually indicates a prior walker rather than a future one. Can this be explained by entropy? Reichenbach argued yes, noting a high-entropy beach with no distinct imprints is more probable than a low-entropy beach with footprints. Therefore, footprints are more likely caused by past interactions than random fluctuations. However, this assumes external influences occurred in the past rather than the future, an assumption based on experience, not explained by entropy.

Thus, there is no direct or obvious connection between entropy increase and many asymmetries typically linked to the direction of time. If defenders of the entropic view could explain how local entropy increase accounts for these asymmetries, the situation would differ. Some B-theorists are optimistic about this; others adopt different approaches.

### The Causal Route

One straightforward way B-theorists explain content asymmetries is the causal approach. Consider the footprint on the beach again. Why is it evidence of an earlier event? Because the footprint likely did not occur spontaneously; it was caused by something else. Since causes precede effects, the event creating the footprint must have happened earlier.

The same reasoning applies to all traces and records that provide knowledge of past events: these are all effects. Since effects come after their causes, there can be no records or traces of future events.By appealing to causation, we can easily explain an asymmetry that seems highly problematic for the entropic account.

We can explain much more. Why do we tend to explain later events in terms of earlier ones? Because explaining an event often consists of citing its cause, and causes come before their effects. We can perceive past occurrences but not future ones because to perceive something is to be causally affected by it, and causes occur before their effects. Memories can also be viewed as causal imprints, so the fact that causes precede their effects explains why we have memories of the past but not of the future. Actions have consequences; effective actions yield desired consequences. If actions are the causes of these consequences and their effects must occur after the actions themselves, there is no mystery as to why our actions can influence the future but not the past, nor why our deliberations tend to be future-directed rather than the reverse.

So far, it seems most content asymmetries can be easily explained in causal terms. We can now take an additional step and define the direction of time explicitly in causal terms. Roughly speaking, if one event causes another, the caused event occurs later than its cause. However, we cannot say that event Ex precedes Ey if and only if Ex causes Ey, since many events are not directly causally related. All events are ordered in time, but relatively few are causally related. Mellor, who defends a causal account of the direction of time, suggests a solution along these lines: take two times, t1 and t2. Many events at t1 are not causally related to events at t2, but some are. We can say t1 is earlier than t2 if some events at t1 cause events at t2. Given the universe's complexity, this will always be the case. In short, the temporal order of all events can follow from the causal order of only some.

### 4.5 Causation in Question

Despite these appealing ideas, many B-theorists find the causal account untenable. To understand why, we need to examine what defenders of the causal account must establish for their view to work.

The claim that event E1 is earlier than E2 because E1 causes E2 is undermined if backward causation is possible—cases where the effect occurs earlier than the cause. This leads to contradictions, where one event would be both earlier and later than another. While backward causation is controversial, many B-theorists hesitate to dismiss it because the usual objection—the future does not exist and thus cannot cause anything—is not available to them.

A second serious problem is distinguishing causes from effects without relying on the earlier-later distinction. If causal theorists define causes as earlier events and effects as later events, their account becomes circular and trivial. For example, saying causes and effects are regularly paired events with causes earlier than effects does not explain causal priority; it merely restates the temporal order.

According to Hume, causes precede effects due to linguistic convention, rather than any real difference. To avoid triviality, causal theorists must provide a substantive account of causal priority that does not appeal to temporal priority.

It might seem easy: causes make their effects happen, but effects don’t make their causes happen, so there is an asymmetrical dependency. However, this asymmetry is not obvious. The laws of physics may be time-symmetrical, meaning an event that can be a cause can also be an effect. Moreover, the relationship of necessity and sufficiency between causes and effects is symmetrical.

For example, consider a spark igniting a fire. In given conditions, the spark is necessary and sufficient for the fire. But the fire also guarantees the spark occurred—without the fire, the spark would not have had its effect—making the relationship symmetrical. If this applies generally, what distinguishes cause from effect other than temporal order?

Causation is complex, possibly involving different forms, and several competing theories exist. It is clear that finding an objective, non-temporal difference between cause and effect is a harder task than expected. Many philosophers conclude there is no such difference; causal asymmetry may be imposed by our way of thinking, much like color is projected onto objects that do not inherently possess it.

If the causal relation is inherently symmetrical, causal accounts of the direction of time cannot succeed or are at best circular. Nevertheless, causal theorists can still provide simple explanations for other asymmetries, such as those involving knowledge. However, even if causation is inherently asymmetrical, its explanatory power is questionable.

### 4.6 Time in Reverse

The universe is currently expanding and may continue to do so forever, but it might not. If there is enough mass to halt expansion, gravity could cause the universe to contract, leading to a "Big Crunch" following the Big Bang.

Big Bang-Big Crunch models come in two types. In one, entropy is low at the Big Bang and increases until the Big Crunch, which is less ordered. In another, entropy rises during expansion but decreases during contraction, making the universe more orderly as it contracts. The latter type may be highly symmetrical, with similar sequences of events running from each singularity to the midpoint. Such models are called "Gold Universes," named after physicist Thomas Gold.

Imagine living in a Gold Universe. Observing the contracting phase would seem like watching a film run backwards: light and energy flow into stars rather than out, people die before being born, and trees start as rotting logs and grow into seedlings. This seems impossible, but the laws of physics may permit time-reversal, as many fundamental physical laws are time-reversible.

We must also avoid assuming our perspective is privileged. Inhabitants of the contracting half would see their universe as expanding and ours as contracting. What we call the end, they would see as the beginning; what we see as expansion, they see as contraction. To them, watching our side would appear as a film running backwards, with time running in reverse.They, too, would wonder how such a thing could be possible, and their perspective is just as valid as our own.

This scenario opens up some intriguing topics. Could we causally interact with our (seemingly) time-reversed neighbors? Could we communicate with them? It might seem not. Suppose we transmitted a sequence of messages M1, M2, M3, and M4. From the perspective of our neighbors, M4 would be the earliest message and M1 the latest; consequently, if they respond to M4, depending on the mode of communication adopted, we may well receive the response before we have transmitted the message itself! However, as MacBeath (1983) shows, with sufficient ingenuity, these hurdles can be overcome. The solution involves programming a computer to transmit one's initial messages at later times.

Then the time-reversal of the sort we have been considering actually involves time itself possessing two dimensions rather than one. But, interesting as they are, these issues cannot be pursued here.

Suppose that the causal account of the direction of time were true. Would the time in the "other half" of a Gold universe have a direction opposite to what it has in ours? It would if the arrow of causation pointed in a different direction in the other half, but there is no reason to believe that this would be the case. If, as we are currently assuming, causation is an intrinsically asymmetrical relation between events, why should its direction be reversed just because the universe starts to contract? So, let us assume that causation runs in just one direction, from one end of the universe to the other: from our big bang to the big crunch.

Something rather disturbing now emerges. It is possible for the apparent direction of time to become completely disconnected from its real direction. Not only are the inhabitants of the "other half" of our universe very similar to us in all respects, but so, too, is the world they inhabit. The various content-asymmetries (with the exception of causation) all run in the same direction: away from their big bang, in the direction of increasing entropy.

I will call the apparent direction of time the "world-arrow." Our time-reversed counterparts exist in a situation where their world-arrow points in the opposite direction to the causal-arrow. Of course, they would be completely oblivious to this fact, since, as is now very obvious, the direction of the causal-arrow has no observable consequences whatsoever. More disturbing still, for all we know, our world-arrow might be pointing in the opposite direction to the causal-arrow! If it were, we would be none the wiser, nor any the worse off.

This thought experiment does not refute the causal theory; it does not prove that events are not, in fact, related by an asymmetrical dependency relationship. But it does a good deal to undermine whatever intuitive support the causal theory in this form has, for it brings into sharp focus how little the theory explains: the apparent asymmetries within time, the asymmetries that determine the direction of our world-time, may well be entirely independent of the causal-arrow. If so, we must look elsewhere for an explanation of the temporal asymmetries that we find in the observable world.

We can draw a further lesson from the thought experiment. The causal theorist's explanations of the content-asymmetries may be suspect, but the fact that these explanations are plausible (at least until examined more closely) suggests that there is indeed an intimate connection between our ordinary concept of causation and these other asymmetries. The causal-arrow and the world-arrow point in the same directions, and it is hard to believe that this alignment is contingent. Wouldn't our time-reversed counterparts be perfectly justified in thinking that the direction of causation in their part of the universe runs in the direction they call "earlier-to-later"? If this is right, if the causal and the world-arrow must point in the same direction, we can ask: why? One answer would be that the causal-arrow determines the direction of the world-arrow, but since this answer is suspect, we must consider the other option: perhaps it is the world-arrow, or some ingredient of it, that determines the causal-arrow.

4.7 Fundamental Forks

In his "Asymmetries in Time" (1987), Paul Horwich embarks on the ambitious task of providing an account of the relationships between all the familiar temporal asymmetries within the context of a block universe. What is striking about the account he defends is the very lowly status causation occupies within it. Rather than being the asymmetry that explains all others, causation comes towards the very end of the hierarchy of explanatory priority.

For Horwich, the knowledge asymmetry is the key to understanding all those other asymmetries. He argues that the knowledge asymmetry itself is largely a product of the fork asymmetry, which exists because of the special conditions that obtained in the early universe. To bring this chapter to a close, here is an outline of some of the main elements in Horwich's explanatory scheme. The account, as Horwich himself concedes, is not entirely adequate as it stands. Some parts are more persuasive than others. Nonetheless, by virtue of its treatment of causation, Horwich's approach to the content-asymmetries is quite different from those that we have considered thus far, and this alone makes it worth examining.

Forks

Since the fork asymmetry plays such an important role in Horwich's scheme, we start here. He describes the asymmetry thus: "Highly correlated event types are invariably preceded by some unified common cause, but they need not have joint effects. We never find a pattern in which the correlated events are linked only by a joint effect."

If X and Y are types of events that frequently occur together, we usually find that there is a certain type of prior event Z that causes both. The reverse—two highly correlated events having a typical subsequent effect but no typical prior cause—never occurs or only rarely. For example, thunder and lightning often occur together and have a common prior cause, high atmospheric static charge, but no single effect invariably follows a lightning discharge.

While this sounds plausible, we cannot characterize this fork asymmetry purely in terms of causes and effects if we hope to use it to explain causation. Horwich provides an alternative cause-free formulation: it is common to find strongly correlated events at a given time statistically associated with a single earlier event; it is rare to find events similarly associated with a single later event.

There is a more general kind of fork asymmetry, not restricted to strongly correlated events. For example, detonating a bomb can have very different consequences in different environments. In each particular case, the effects trace back to a single cause. Here, there is an asymmetry of overdetermination. Owens characterizes it:

"For any event, the number of events at any given point in the future for which it is necessary is greater than the number of events at any given point in the past for which it is necessary."

Any given detonation typically has a single sufficient prior condition, such as pushing a switch. The detonation is necessary for just one immediately preceding event but is necessary for many subsequent effects, which would not have occurred otherwise. These overdetermining later events may be strongly correlated, but they need not be. Bearing this in mind, let us move on.

Causation

Horwich agrees with Hume that causation is itself a symmetrical relationship and that causes precede effects only because of a convention about word usage. However, he maintains that this convention is not arbitrary. The stipulation that causes come before effects is supported by a cluster of beliefs about causation, namely:

1. Causes explain their effects.  
2. Causes are, in some sense, ontologically more basic than their effects.  
3. Correlated events are causally connected.  
4. Causes are the means by which we bring about the effects that are our ends.Given these beliefs about what a cause is, and the temporal asymmetry of knowledge, explanation, and so on, the idea that causes occur earlier than their effects is entirely natural. Or so Horwich goes on to argue. Both 2 and 4 are linked to the knowledge asymmetry. The vague idea that causes are "more real" than their effects is rooted in the widespread belief that the past is more real than the future, and this belief is in turn rooted in the fact that we have detailed knowledge of the past but not of the future. As for 4, Horwich suggests that causation is, in part, defined by its association with our experience of deliberation and control: "We define causation as that general relation between events that is exemplified when an event is deliberately brought about by free choice" (1987: 143).

If, after due deliberation, I decide to put poison in Jim's drink, and Jim subsequently dies, I have caused Jim's death. More generally, we would all agree that if C is used by someone to produce a desired end E, then C causes E. As for why our deliberation and intentions are future-oriented, consider this: Could you seriously deliberate about performing a certain action A if you know for certain that you will not do A? Of course not. We can only deliberate and form genuine intentions to perform actions that we believe we are free to perform. Horwich argues that the feeling of freedom is associated with our ignorance, prior to deliberation, of what we are going to do. If we knew as much about the future as we do about the past (and with the same certainty that is provided by memory), this feeling could not be sustained. We can then explain, in terms of the sense of free choice, why rational decisions are not made for the sake of past events and why we care about the future more than the past.

The knowledge asymmetry also plays a role in the temporal orientation of explanation. The belief that the past is real and fixed, whereas the future is not, is, according to Horwich, grounded in the knowledge asymmetry. This belief helps sustain the practice of explaining later events in terms of earlier ones. For anyone who holds this belief, it will seem natural to suppose that later events depend on earlier events in a way that earlier events cannot depend on later events. However, the contribution of the fork asymmetries to the asymmetry of explanation is even more marked. A genuine coincidence consists of a conjunction of events that has no explanation. Such a conjunction has no explanation if its component events each have necessary and/or sufficient conditions that are independent of one another. If there is some single event that is necessary and/or sufficient for each component of the conjunction, we have an explanation, and the conjunction is no coincidence. For example, ten people on a coach party falling ill is a coincidence if they all ate different meals; it isn't if they all ate the fish for lunch.

Because future-directed forks predominate, and given the asymmetry of overdetermination, non-coincidental conjunctions of events are nearly always explained by reference to earlier events. It is common to find a single earlier event that is both necessary and sufficient for several subsequent events, revealing the later conjunction to be non-coincidental. However, it is rare to find the same in reverse. Generally speaking, when a given earlier event is necessary for several subsequent events, none of the latter is individually necessary for the earlier event, since each is individually sufficient for it. Since the direction of explanation runs from earlier to later, and since causes are events that explain their effects, so too does the direction of causation.

Horwich argues that the knowledge asymmetry can be explained in terms of the fork asymmetry, a conclusion that emerges from a highly abstract discussion of the nature of recording devices. To convey the basic idea informally:

An expanse of wet concrete acts as an efficient recording system: if someone steps in it, an accurate imprint of their footwear (or foot) will be made and likely last for a long time, since once concrete is set, it is physically robust. Anyone who later sees this imprint has good reason to believe that someone with just that shape and size of foot stepped in the concrete shortly before it hardened. Any foot-shaped imprint in concrete is a particular macro-state consisting of trillions of particles in a particular configuration. These particles could exist in countless different configurations, and since concrete slabs generally begin in a randomly mixed condition, it is highly unlikely that any given foot-shaped pattern would develop through chance alone. Moreover, it is even more unlikely that many different foot-shaped imprints found in various concrete surfaces would arise by chance. At the microscopic level, the creation of these imprints involves strongly correlated events: particles in many different concrete surfaces all arranging into highly improbable foot-shaped configurations. These correlated events require an antecedent event of a highly specific sort—for instance, a foot pressing downwards. We can be reasonably certain that the event that explains the correlation occurred earlier than the correlation itself because future-directed forks of this kind are common, while inverse forks are rare.

Records come in diverse forms, such as fossils, newspaper reports, and memories. Despite this diversity, they share similarities:
- Each is a macroscopic system whose parts tend to enter into highly specific and improbable configurations.
- These configurations are strongly correlated with some other type of event (like foot-shaped imprints correlated with feet pressing downwards).
- This other type of event almost always occurs earlier than the configuration.

This last fact holds due to the fork asymmetry. If regularly occurring improbable conjunctions of event-types were commonly associated statistically with a single type of subsequent event, reliable records of future events might exist; but since they do not, such records do not occur.

The Source of the Fork Asymmetry  
Since all other asymmetries lead back to the fork asymmetry, directly or indirectly, one pressing question remains: Why do normal forks predominate over inverse forks? Why does the fork asymmetry exist at all in a universe governed by time-symmetrical laws?

This is a crucial question, and Horwich offers only a speculative cosmological conjecture. He assumes that the early universe combined order with disorder. Disorder existed at the micro-level: elementary particles were in a state of random flux. Within this micro-chaos, there was a limited degree of macro-order: mass-energy was not distributed uniformly but concentrated in structures that eventually formed galaxies, stars, and planets. These assumptions align with many current theories of the early universe.

Suppose events of types X and Y are strongly correlated—they co-occur frequently (such as heat and smoke). It is unlikely that the sequences of events leading up to these conjunctions consist of two independent processes, because that would require a vast and repeated conspiracy at the microscopic level, ruled out by the assumption of initial micro-chaos. Therefore, we would expect to find a single type of event Z (for example, fire) whose occurrence is strongly correlated with, or sufficient for, the subsequent occurrence of X- and Y-type events. This is exactly what we tend to observe: the normal fork pattern.

The question needing an answer is: Why do inverse forks not occur? Why are strongly correlated simultaneous events not regularly followed by a single later event of a specific type? Horwich suggests the answer lies with the implicit microscopic order present at later times.

Suppose the universe does not collapse under its own gravity but that its expansion gradually slows. From the second law of thermodynamics, we know that the energy currently concentrated in stars will eventually disperse, and later stages of the universe will contain no recognizable order—only a thin, homogeneous spread of low-energy particles and fields. Considering the universe's evolution from earlier to later times, we see progression from an initial state combining macro-order and micro-chaos to later states consisting only of micro-chaos—an asymmetric progression governed at the micro-scale by time-symmetric laws. 

Now, imagine viewing this process unfolding in reverse, from later to earlier times. On a large scale, you would see the cold, thin soup of particles gradually coming together to form dim stars, which grow brighter as more energy flows into them; occasionally, vast numbers of particles converge into a single star, resembling a supernova in reverse.On the much smaller scale of our Earth, you would see numerous astonishing coincidences, such as wind blowing leaves into neat heaps in the middle of lawns and dirty clothes scattered around a room ending up washed and neatly folded in cupboards and drawers.

Adopting the time-reversed view makes one thing very clear: although the thin cold particle soup that exists at the later stages of the universe might look like a random distribution, it isn't. Given the distributions of particles at these times and the time-symmetrical laws governing them, they will evolve into the highly organized systems that exist at the present time. The apparently random distribution is, in fact, highly ordered; this order is nearly impossible to discern. It is implicit but present nonetheless.

The presence of this implicit order explains why inverse forks are rare. Even if the scenario sketched above is implausible and the implicit order dissipates over long periods, it certainly exists over the short term—short by cosmological standards.

---

So, given the hypothesis of initial micro-chaos, strongly correlated events generally occur only when there is a single earlier event that is nomologically sufficient for all of them. The implicit order in the events following strongly correlated occurrences means there is no need for correlated events to be followed by some single type of event that is nomologically sufficient for them all. Even if the subsequent events are many, varied, and apparently random, in each specific case these later events are nomologically sufficient for the earlier events. For example, thunder and lightning are always preceded by a buildup of atmospheric static; there is no one succeeding condition of remotely comparable significance.

If we explain the content asymmetries along the general lines sketched out by Horwich, we cannot rule out the possibility of time-reversed universes. Since the initial conditions at each end of a Gold universe are similar—both exhibiting micro-chaos—each half is expected to develop similarly. Not only will both halves be characterized by fork asymmetries, but the forks will point in different directions. If, as Horwich maintains, all other content asymmetries depend on the fork asymmetry, these, too, will be differently oriented, including causation. The expanding and contracting phases of the universe will be time-reversed with respect to one another. Time's arrow may thus be less securely fixed than we tend to think. At least, this would be the case if we lived in a B-world—a world where temporal passage is illusory, and time itself has no inherent direction. Whether this is true remains to be established.

Horwich himself stresses that his account of why the fork asymmetry exists rests on largely speculative premises—such as a low-entropy big bang—and needs further development before it can be considered fully adequate. He notes that all that matters for his account of other content asymmetries is the existence of the fork asymmetry itself, but there are still questions to address, and some doubt whether this asymmetry is sufficient for the role Horwich assigns to it. For our purposes, what matters is this: even if the B-theorist cannot appeal to causation to explain time asymmetries, alternative strategies are available that may prove viable. If Horwich's proposal is problematic, variants may be more successful.

However, without any fully worked-out proposals along these lines, and given difficulties encountered by entropic and causal accounts, B-theorists have yet to provide a fully satisfactory account of the various content asymmetries considered. If it proves impossible to formulate such an account, B-theorists would have no option but to regard these asymmetries, or at least those unexplained, as brute facts—simply the way contents are distributed in a four-dimensional continuum in asymmetrical patterns. This is not ideal, but neither is it necessarily fatal, since every theoretical system has unexplained elements. Adopting this stance would be damaging if viable dynamic conceptions of time with greater explanatory power exist. Whether such alternatives to the eternalist or block conception exist is the issue we will examine next.

### 5 Tensed Time

#### 5.1 Tense versus Dynamism

Earlier, it was noted that we should not assume the eternal-dynamic dispute maps neatly onto the tenseless-tensed dispute. The latter revolves around concepts needed to describe the world metaphysically adequately, while the former concerns the reality or otherwise of temporal passage. The B-theory combines two claims:

1. The world is a four-dimensional ensemble, where all times and events are equally real and coexist.
2. The world can be fully described in tenseless terms.

Later chapters will explore ways to challenge the first claim; this chapter considers the implications of rejecting the second.

E. J. Lowe has defended the A-framework against critics and aimed to establish the viability of the tensed conception of time. Many B-theorists have argued that the tensed conception is incoherent, so Lowe’s arguments are of interest. Previous discussions introduced McTaggart's A-paradox and Mellor's claim about tensed statements having tenseless truth-conditions, but counterarguments remain to be considered. Lowe’s position is particularly noteworthy because, while defending the tensed conception, he does not engage in grand metaphysical speculations. Does this mean such speculations are unnecessary? Can someone rejecting the B-theory find what they need in the tensed conception alone? These questions guide what follows.

#### 5.2 Taking Tense Seriously

Lowe distinguishes the tensed and tenseless views this way: the tensed view takes McTaggart's "A-series" terminology as indispensable for the metaphysics—not just the epistemology and semantics—of time, whereas the tenseless view takes "B-series" terminology alone to be indispensable.

Lowe firmly rejects the idea that properties can be tenselessly predicated of concrete historical events. Only abstract entities, such as numbers, which exist outside space and time, can tenselessly possess properties. Anything that exists in time possesses properties in a tensed way: to exist in time is to be a potential subject of tensed predications. That is, for something to exist in time is for some property to be now exemplified by it, or to have been exemplified, or to be going to be exemplified.
