If we read PSR in this way, how can the substantivalist respond to the question "Why is the world as a whole located where it is?" Sklar suggests that the substantivalist can answer thus:

"Because yesterday it was, as a whole, in this position and no forces arose to move it. So there is a sufficient reason for the present place of the material world in substantival space—its previous position and the forces that have acted in the meantime. After all, why is Jupiter where it is today relative to the Earth? Answer: Because of the positions and velocities they had yesterday and the forces acting upon them in the intermediate period." (1977: 180)

Explaining the way things are at present in terms of causal laws and the way things were earlier is what counts as providing a "sufficient reason" in contemporary science. Leibniz would have refused to accept this sort of answer as providing a genuinely sufficient reason, since it explains one contingent state of affairs in terms of another contingent state of affairs, and he believed that ultimately, nothing was contingent; everything is as it is because it had to be this way, as a matter of necessity. His argument thus rests on a particular version of the Principle of Sufficient Reason (PSR) that, as Sklar notes, "most present-day philosophers and scientists would reject out of hand" (Sklar 1977: 181).

### 10.7 The Methodological Argument

There is a further argument to be found in Leibniz, one that he did not greatly emphasize himself but that has proved by far the most influential subsequently. In his "Fifth Paper," he writes:

"The author replies now that the reality of motion does not depend upon being observed; and that a ship may go forward, and yet a man who is in the ship may not perceive it. I answer, motion does not indeed depend upon being observed; but it does depend upon being possible to be observed. There is no motion when there is no change that can be observed. And when there is no change that can be observed, there is no change at all" (Alexander 1956: 74).

I noted earlier, in connection with the argument from PII, that Leibniz had failed to defend the claim that only empirically detectable differences are real differences. He is now claiming that although real changes can occur without being observed, any real change is such that it must be possible for someone to observe it.

Is this a plausible position? There are those who have found it so. The logical positivists subscribed to the "verification principle": for a claim about the world to be meaningful, it must be empirically verifiable. This principle yields the result that claims about uniform absolute motion are meaningless, since none can be empirically verified. It is not at all clear that this is what Leibniz had in mind, and since few now are convinced that the verification principle is true, perhaps this is just as well. Scientists these days are quite prepared to posit unobservable entities, and thus unobservable changes, provided these unobservable goings-on are among the postulates of a theory that explains and predicts what can be observed.

This last point takes us to the heart of the matter. Many of the entities postulated by contemporary scientists may not themselves be directly observable, but they do have effects that are observable. Subatomic particles are not visible to the naked eye, but not only are they the building blocks of the macroscopic objects that we can perceive; their movements can be briefly tracked by the detectors in particle accelerators. No scientist would be happy accepting a theory that posits entities that have no discernible empirical consequences.

By way of an example, suppose that two competing theories of particle physics are proposed, P and Q. Both explain the behavior of all known particles and forces in equally elegant ways, but theory P posits an additional type of particle, called the squillion, which is said to be everywhere all the time. Theory Q does not posit squillions. Moreover, according to P, squillions are in principle unobservable; they never interact with any other particles. Clearly, we would have no reason to believe theory P and would reject the claim that squillions exist.According to Leibniz, Newton's absolute space is akin to the squillion. Not only is it unobservable, directly or indirectly, but we also have an equally powerful theory of space, relationism, which is just as good as Newton's but does not posit any unobservable entities or empirically indiscernible states of affairs.

This is a potentially compelling argument. I say potentially because Newton disputes Leibniz's claim that motion with respect to absolute space has no empirical consequences. Newton claims that dynamic (forced, accelerated) shifts are detectable.

## 11 Absolute Motion

### 11.1 Inertial Motion

Newton deploys two basic arguments against relationism. The best known is the argument from inertial effects—the notorious bucket argument. The second is the argument for real inertial motion. Both are connected with Newton's first law of motion, namely that any object will continue to move at a constant velocity (or remain at rest) unless acted on by a force. Before proceeding, it will help to review some of the relevant terminology.

Newton's first law is often called the "law of inertia." Inertial motion is motion in the absence of impinging forces: a moving body will continue to move at the same speed in a straight line forever if no forces act on it. Inertial forces are the forces experienced by a body that is undergoing acceleration. In Newton's physics, a body is at absolute rest if it is stationary with respect to absolute space, and in absolute motion if it is moving relative to absolute space. An object has relative velocity if it is moving at a certain speed and direction with respect to another object. Acceleration is the rate of change of velocity. An object undergoes absolute acceleration when it changes its velocity with respect to absolute space, whereas relative acceleration involves a change in velocity with respect to other bodies.

We can establish a frame of reference by centering a coordinate system on a particular body assumed to be at rest. Motions of other bodies can then be specified relative to this reference body. These bodies may be at rest, in constant motion, or accelerating relative to this frame. "Inertial frames of reference" are centered on bodies not undergoing absolute acceleration; these are the Galilean frames discussed previously.

### 11.2 The Argument for Real Inertial Motions

We start with *De Gravitatione*, a work that was unpublished until recently but contains important arguments not found in the *Scholium*. Newton's explicit target is Cartesian physics, as formulated in Descartes's *Principles*.

Descartes combined a commitment to the law of inertia with a relational conception of motion. Newton points out that these two are incompatible. The law of inertia states that a body will continue to move in a straight line at constant speed unless acted on by a force. This means a body not moving in a straight line is acted on by a force. Since the existence of forces is not conventional, the law of inertia presupposes a real and objective difference between straight and curved paths. However, Descartes held that all motion is relative—to move is to have different distance relations to other bodies at different times—so there is no such difference. Each body can be regarded as stationary and used to define a reference frame in which other bodies' motions are measured.

Consider two spaceships, A and B. A regards itself as moving in a straight line and B as moving along a curved path, while B regards itself as moving straight and A as moving curved. For the relationist, neither is privileged; both views are equally valid. Thus, every body can be considered to have many different motions, and there is no objective fact about a body's "real" motion or whether it moves in a straight line.

Another example involves competing reference frames. According to frame F1, Earth orbits the stationary Sun; according to frame F2, the Sun orbits the stationary Earth. If the whole solar system moves uniformly in a straight line, these frames describe Earth's motion differently: in F1, Earth moves in a spiral, implying a force acts on it; in F2, Earth moves in a straight line and the Sun moves in a spiral, implying the force acts on the Sun. These accounts ascribe different forces to the same bodies, which cannot both be true because the existence of a real force cannot depend on an arbitrary reference frame. Therefore, a consistent physics requires a notion of real inertial motion—an objective fact about which motions are inertial. Newton's concept of absolute velocity addresses this.

Newton further challenges relationism by arguing that if an object's location depends only on distances from other objects, and if those objects continually change positions, then there are no facts about past locations. He explains that according to Cartesian doctrine, because places depend on positions relative to surrounding bodies, once those bodies move, past positions no longer exist. This means motion cannot have definite velocity or distance traversed, undermining the concept of motion itself. Hence, Newton concludes that definitions of place and local motion must refer to something motionless, such as absolute space.

Newton argues that for objective facts about a body's velocity and direction at any moment to exist, there must be objective facts about its past locations. If spatial locations are only defined relative to changing material bodies, these facts are unavailable. It would help to take one body as motionless, like the Sun, and measure other bodies’ movements relative to it, but this is not an option for the relationist. Recognizing absolute space and motion allows for objective facts about directions and distances between objects.

This argument is intriguing but not decisive. There may be ways for relationists to address Newton's challenge, but he correctly identifies a difficulty that absolute space solves.

In light of these arguments, we can provisionally grant Newton this: the law of inertia presupposes a real difference between bodies moving uniformly in straight lines and bodies moving differently (like in circles), a difference relationism based only on distance relations cannot recognize.However, what is to prevent the relationist from rejecting the distinction in question? Is there anything to prevent Leibniz from holding that claims such as "the Sun is moving in a straight line and the Earth is rotating about it" and "the Earth is moving in a straight line and the Sun is rotating about it" are equally true? It is here that Newton's arguments in the Scholium come into play.

While Newton fully accepted that absolute velocity has no detectable consequences—there are no experiments that will reveal how fast you are moving with respect to absolute space—he took pains to point out that the same does not apply to changes in velocity; that is, accelerations. We all know that forces are associated with acceleration. If you are in a quickly accelerating car, you feel yourself being pulled back into your seat. This is a typical instance of so-called "inertial forces" making themselves felt: these are the forces "experienced" by a body that is not following its inertial path through space due to some applied force, such as that supplied by a car's engine. The trouble is, just as velocity is frame-relative for the relationist, so is acceleration. If A and B are accelerating apart, then for the relationist there is no fact of the matter as to who is really accelerating: each of A and B can regard themselves as being at rest and the other as accelerating with respect to them. If I accelerate away from your car, which remains stationary on the pavement, then, relatively speaking, it is as true to say that I am accelerating away from you as it is to say that you are accelerating away from me. But it is clear that if we describe the situation in terms of relative accelerations, we are omitting a crucial factor: it is only I who feels inertial forces; you do not. I am the one pulled back into my seat, not you. The obvious explanation for this is that only one of us is really accelerating. Hence, it seems that an adequate dynamics requires a distinction between real and relative acceleration, for without it we cannot account for the presence or absence of inertial forces—that is, those experienced by the accelerating body. Since the relationist has no means of grounding this distinction, his account of (accelerated) motion is inadequate. If we assume, as most do, that acceleration is a change of motion with respect to something, then what alternative is there to Newton's view that real acceleration is a change of motion relative to absolute space? Once the existence of absolute space is established in this manner, we can deduce that material objects that are not accelerating must be either in uniform motion or at rest with respect to it, even though there is no observational evidence that will tell us which state a particular body is in.

In making these points in the Scholium, Newton employed two thought experiments. Neither involves linear accelerations of the sort that we have just been considering; rather, they feature rotations. Newton claims, "The effects which distinguish absolute from relative motion are the forces of receding from the axis of circular motion. For there are no such forces in a circular motion purely relative, but in a true and absolute circular motion, they are greater or less according..."

And, of course, rotation is itself a form of acceleration. Since acceleration is change in velocity, and velocity involves moving in a particular direction, a rotating object is continually changing its direction of movement and hence is continually accelerating. In the case of a stone fixed to a string and whirled around one's head, at any instant the velocity of the stone is in a direction tangential to the circle it is moving around—the direction it would fly off in if released. The string must exert a continual force on the stone to prevent it from pursuing this natural inertial motion. In the case of a body that follows a curved (but not circular) path, such as a rocket ship, forces supplied by small rocket motors must be applied to push the body off its inertial path. Since these motors apply force to the body of the ship, not to its occupants, it is left for the ship itself to force its occupants off their inertial path: unless strapped down, those on board would find themselves pushed by the walls of the ship, just as people in a car turning a corner find themselves pushed against the car doors. Similar effects, albeit in one case more complex, underpin Newton's examples.

The bucket

Suppose a bucket full of water is suspended by a rope from the ceiling. Twist the bucket so the rope winds up, then release it. The following sequence unfolds:

1. The bucket and water are initially at rest with respect to one another, and the surface of the water is flat.
2. After a short while, as the twisted rope unwinds, the bucket begins to rotate, but the water remains stationary and flat.
3. Then, as the rotation of the bucket is gradually communicated to the water via friction, the water starts to turn and soon rotates at exactly the same rate as the bucket; the water surface is no longer flat but concave, rising up the sides.

We are all familiar with this phenomenon: fluids rotating in containers rise up the sides—think of spilling tea when stirring in milk. Newton talks of a tendency to "recede" from the axis of rotation, but this is not entirely accurate. The particles in a rotating fluid tend to move tangentially to their curvilinear path, as they would if unconstrained, like a stone released from a sling. But since their natural inertial movement is constrained—by other particles and the container walls—their actual movement takes the form of an outwardly expanding spiral. As all particles move this way, the fluid builds up around the container’s sides.

This build-up generates a marked increase in downward fluid pressure, which often prevents the liquid from overflowing, depending on the rate of rotation. The behavior of the water in this simple, familiar case poses a serious problem for the relationist. Newton explains the curvature of the water in the third stage as its motion with respect to absolute space. The relationist cannot use this explanation and must say the curvature is due to motion relative to some other material thing. But what? Descartes said that "real motion" takes place when a body moves relative to its immediate surroundings. If this were correct, then curvature should be at a maximum when the relative motion of the water with respect to the bucket is greatest. But at this point—the second stage—the water is flat. The curvature is greatest in the third stage, when there is no relative motion between the two, as both rotate at the same rate.

If we cannot explain the inertial forces by appealing to the relative motion of the water with respect to the bucket, can we say the water is rotating with respect to itself? No—for the same effect would occur even if the particles making up the water did not rotate relative to one another. Could the curvature be due to motion with respect to us, the observers? Again, no. The curvature certainly occurs when the water is rotating relative to an observer, but it is not caused by this relative motion. If the observer ran around the bucket quickly, the water would remain perfectly flat. Similarly, if the surrounding buildings were placed on a giant turntable and spun around the stationary bucket, the water would not be disturbed. Newton's explanation—that the inertial effect occurs when the water is rotating relative to absolute space—has strong merit.

Newton does not explicitly consider whether the curvature of the water could be due to relative motion to more distant bodies, such as stars. Instead, he offers another example, likely in part to refute that idea: if two globes, kept a given distance apart by a cord, are revolved about their common center of gravity, the tension in the cord reveals the globes’ tendency to recede from their axis of motion, allowing us to compute their circular motions.

If the cord contains a tension-measuring device, like a spring balance, we can tell if the globes are rotating simply by looking at it: tension indicates rotation. Newton then points out that the same would apply if these globes were in an immense vacuum—no stars, no planets—nothing external to compare with.

Imagine two possible worlds, each containing just a pair of globes connected by a tension-measuring cord and nothing else. In one world, the meter registers zero tension; in the other, it registers significant tension. Newton explains the difference: in the second world, the globes are rotating in absolute space; in the first, they are not.What can the relationist offer? Since there are no objects other than the globes and cord in either world, and the distance between the globes remains constant, from a relational standpoint there is no relative motion in either world, and so the globes in both W1 and W2 are stationary. It seems that the only difference between these worlds is the existence of the tension in the cord in W2. The relationist is thus pushed into the uncomfortable position of having to view the tension in W2 as an entirely inexplicable occurrence. Given the availability of an alternative explanation—Newton's—this position seems quite untenable.

Taken together, the bucket and globes amount to a formidable challenge to relationism. In both cases, accelerations give rise to observable forces; these accelerations are not, it seems, accelerations relative to ordinary physical bodies. But if acceleration is a change of velocity relative to something, what can this something be if not absolute space? As Earman suggests (1989: 64), Newton's argument is best construed as (in part at least) an inference to the best explanation of the mechanical phenomena:

P1: The best explanation of mechanical phenomena in general (and rotating phenomena in particular) utilizes absolute acceleration (and absolute rotation in particular).  
P2: Absolute acceleration (and absolute rotation in particular) must be understood as acceleration (and rotation) relative to absolute space.

If we accept the Newtonian account, we have an explanation for why the connection between acceleration and force is so ubiquitous; why it exists at all places and all times. If all acceleration is a change in velocity with respect to absolute space, and absolute space is the same everywhere, it is to be expected that similar accelerations give rise to the same forces everywhere and everywhen, which, as far as we know, they do. For, as we have already seen, and as Newton was to go on to demonstrate in the rest of the Principia, inertial forces are ubiquitous and are by no means confined to rotating bodies: every time you feel yourself pushing into the wall of a turning bus or car, you are experiencing them.

11.4 Stalemate?  
Although Newton's bucket amounts to a weighty argument, relationists did not simply surrender. This is not surprising, for although the argument from inertial effects is powerful, the relationist's stance is far from absurd. Indeed, the positions might seem perfectly balanced:

- Leibniz argued (and Newton accepted) that in Newtonian physics, uniform absolute velocity has no empirical consequences. If you have an absolute velocity, it is impossible to tell what it is. If we assume that uniform absolute velocity is a suspect notion, since it has no empirical significance, then how can absolute acceleration have any empirical significance? After all, absolute acceleration is simply variation in absolute velocity.
  
- Newton argued in the other direction. Having good grounds for believing that absolute acceleration has significant empirical consequences, he concluded that there must be such a thing as absolute velocity, even though the latter is without physical consequences.

From this, we see that Leibniz's kinematic shift continues to pose a problem for the Newtonian: the empirical insignificance of uniform motion with respect to absolute space renders the relevance of acceleration (relative to absolute space) questionable. That said, if relationists are to reject Newton's theory, they must still come up with an alternative explanation of inertial effects. If these are not produced by absolute acceleration, construed as change in velocity with respect to absolute space, what does produce them? In the remainder of this chapter, we will consider three lines of response.

11.5 The Leibnizian Response  
Although Leibniz was well aware of the bucket argument and the general problem posed by acceleration for the relationist, he never got around to publishing a detailed response to it. However, he made some suggestive remarks. In his fifth letter to Clarke, he wrote:

"I find nothing in the Eighth Definition of the Mathematical Principles of Nature, nor in the Scholium belonging to it, that proves, or can prove, the reality of space in itself. However, I grant there is a difference between an absolute true motion of a body and a mere relative change of situation concerning another body. For when the immediate cause of the change is in the body, that body is truly in motion; and then the situation of other bodies, with respect to it, will be changed consequently, though the cause of the change is not in them. Thus, I have left nothing unanswered regarding what has been alleged for the absolute reality of space."  
(Alexander 1956: 74)

Unfortunately, Leibniz died before he could spell out exactly what he had in mind, but it may have been something along these lines. Since Newton's first law of motion tells us that absolute accelerations are correlated with forces, can't we say that inertial effects are produced not by change of motion with respect to absolute space, but rather by the forces that Newton rightly recognizes are inseparably bound up with real as opposed to merely relative accelerations? Real accelerations are those in which the immediate cause of the change is in the body.

This idea works well enough for some cases; for example, if two rockets in outer space are in relative acceleration, it is easy to apply Leibniz's criterion to determine which is really accelerating—it is the one whose rocket motor is firing. However, not all cases of real acceleration are like this. We might extend Leibniz's argument to collisions and say that when contact between moving bodies occurs, the cause of the change is "in the object." But there remain many instances of real accelerations that are not produced by external forces in this sort of way. First, there are the accelerations produced by forces that act at a distance, such as gravitational and magnetic attractions in Newtonian physics.

Secondly, as Friedman stresses, uniform rotation is not correlated with external forces in Newtonian theory. Even if we could replace the reference to absolute acceleration in some cases with a reference to force-generating causal processes, Newtonian theory does not allow us to replace the reference to absolute rotation in a similar manner (1983: 229).

A rotating solid disc will continue to rotate at a constant angular velocity without any need for external force or torque to be applied; it is maintained in motion by the preservation of angular momentum, a basic principle of Newtonian physics confirmed by, for example, the Earth's daily rotation. Yet, if the disc rotates fast enough, the centrifugal forces generated by its rotation will tear it apart.

In earlier Leibnizian writings, a different approach can be discerned. In the "Specimen Dynamicum," he claims that all motion is in straight lines or compounded of straight lines and then says:

"From these considerations, it can be understood why I cannot support some of the philosophical opinions of certain great mathematicians ... on this matter, who admit empty space and seem not to shrink from the theory of attraction, but also hold motion to be an absolute thing and claim to prove this from rotation and the centrifugal force arising from it. But since rotation arises only from a composition of rectilinear motions, it follows that if the equipollence of hypotheses is saved in rectilinear motions, however they are assumed, it will also be saved in curvilinear motions." (Loemker 1970: 449-50)

His idea may have been that although objects look as though they are rotating, in reality, they are composed of microscopic particles that are moving in straight lines and zigzagging back and forth. This is an intriguing speculation, but, as Earman points out (1989: 72), Leibniz's analysis is based on the assumption that there is a fact of the matter as to which trajectories are straight and which are not. In a relational space, a trajectory that is straight relative to one reference body will be curved relative to another, and each perspective is equally valid.

11.6 The Machian Response

The nineteenth-century physicist Ernst Mach criticized Newton for positing absolute space on empiricist grounds. Mach was deeply suspicious of unobservable entities such as absolute space and believed that science should concern itself only with relationships between observable things and events. This general approach, which exerted a profound influence on Einstein, naturally leads to a relationist approach to space.

Like Berkeley, Mach criticized Newton for, as he saw it, going beyond the observational data. The globes argument is particularly vulnerable on this score. Newton claims that globes in an otherwise empty universe would behave just as globes in our universe do; a tension would be created in the joining cord when the globes are rotating. Mach complains that this is an entirely groundless speculation. On the one hand, since the experiment has never been carried out, we have no way of knowing how a pair of rotating globes would react if the rest of the matter in the universe were to disappear. On the other hand, the experiment is impossible to carry out. If the globes are the only objects in the universe, there couldn't possibly be anyone present to observe the results of their rotation. Newton is guilty of assuming the result he purports to be arguing for.Yes, given his theory, the globes would behave as he describes, but his theory is the only reason we have for believing that the globes would behave as he describes; hence the thought-experiment doesn't prove his theory true.

But what of the bucket? What of inertial effects that are observable in the actual universe? Since Mach is convinced that talk of absolute space is nonsense, and that the only motion it makes sense to talk about is relative motion, he argues that inertial effects must be the product of change relative to other material bodies in the universe.

How does this work in practice? Which other bodies? Well, for most practical purposes, from the standpoint of the Earth, the stars can be regarded as providing a fixed frame of reference. Instead of saying that the water in the rotating bucket rises because it is accelerating relative to absolute space, we say that it rises because it is rotating relative to the fixed stars. It is important to note that all the inertial effects we observe—on the Earth at least—do in fact arise when objects accelerate relative to the stars. Since we cannot observe motion relative to space, this relative acceleration is all we can directly detect. Mach is on firm ground here.

There are predictive differences between Mach's account and Newton's that are readily grasped. For Mach, since it is the relative motion between water and the stars that causes the water to rise, it doesn't matter, or even make sense to say, which of the two is really rotating. If there is relative motion between water and stars, the water will curve. For Newton, however, it does make sense to suppose that the water is really stationary, and if it is stationary (i.e., at rest relative to absolute space), it will remain perfectly flat no matter how fast the rest of the universe is rotating about it. Similarly, if there is no relative motion between water and stars, then according to Mach, the water will remain flat. Not so for Newton; if the water and the stars are rotating at the same rate relative to absolute space, the water will rise, even though there is no relative motion between the two.

So far, so good; now for the hard part. Mach has correctly pointed out that for most practical purposes on Earth, we can regard inertial effects as arising when objects move relative to the stars, which we can regard as fixed. But this is only an approximation: in fact, the stars are moving relative to one another; it is just that this motion is comparatively slow (at least when observed from Earth). So, what precise theory of dynamics is Mach offering? If the stars are not fixed and inertial effects are caused by relative motions, we need to specify what the relevant relative motions are. What must an object be accelerating with respect to in order to undergo inertial effects? What are the relevant laws of nature? What is the mechanism?

It is here that Mach is rather vague. The general character of the needed theory is plain. The distinction between inertial and non-inertial (accelerated) motion must be grounded in the overall arrangement of matter in the universe. It is because matter is configured in a certain way that certain paths are inertial and others are not. His one concrete proposal is that inertial forces are produced when a body accelerates with respect to the center of gravity of the entire universe. By what mechanism, though, and why?

Presumably, there must be something akin to a force that links all bodies in the universe and that is responsible for their moving as they do. This is a bold conjecture—one that can seem implausible: would you seriously entertain the possibility that the reason you get seasick is due to your motion relative to the stars? Is this notion any more plausible than astrology? Newton himself recognized the possibility of explaining the bucket in this sort of way but was skeptical: "But who will imagine that the parts of the water endeavor to recede from its center on account of a force impressed only on the heavens?" It is not clear that this scorn is entirely justified: Newton's own theory of gravity, after all, posits a force of attraction acting at a distance that operates instantaneously and links every material body to every other material body! This is just the sort of force that Mach needs, but there is a key difference: Newton had a precise quantitative theory that matches known empirical data and whose predictions are well confirmed. Mach can offer only a sketch of a theory of comparable power. In the absence of a complete physical theory, the Machian approach cannot be judged successful.

### 11.7 The Sklar Response

In responding to Newton, both the Leibnizian and the Machian make the same assumption: they agree with Newton that inertial effects are due to acceleration and that acceleration is change of velocity with respect to something. It is this last assumption—that change of motion has to be relative to something—that Sklar questions. Sklar pointed out that the relationist can simply abandon this assumption.

What follows if we do? Recall the problem. If we adopt a relationist view of acceleration, how can it be that when two bodies are undergoing acceleration relative to one another, only one body experiences inertial effects? What account can the relationist give of this asymmetry? Sklar's suggestion is that there is a primitive monadic property that bodies moving on some trajectories possess, which comes in different quantities, and inertial effects are associated with the possession of this property. If we like, we can call this property "absolute acceleration," but this is only a label; objects possessing this property should not be thought of as undergoing changes in motion relative to Newtonian absolute space or Mach's center of the universe. The property in question is primitive and thus quite independent of absolute space, which we now say does not exist.

While Sklar's proposal on behalf of the relationist is interesting, in that it reveals a previously unsuspected region of logical space, in other respects it is not very appealing. The Sklarian relationist posits exactly what is needed for the relationist to solve the problem of inertial effects in a way that is immune to the problems facing the Leibnizian and the Machian theories. However, the posited property lacks independent motivation. To deliver quantitative predictions, the Sklarian will presumably employ the standard machinery of Newtonian mechanics and then reinterpret the results in his own terms, redescribing trajectories that Newtonians view as absolute accelerations in terms of varying quantities of the monadic property.

The resulting picture is quite odd: there are bodies moving along various paths (construed relationally); only some of these objects possess the monadic property, and only those that do experience inertial effects. There is nothing in the objects or the paths they follow that distinguishes those that possess the monadic property from those that do not. The property appears to be distributed in a rather accidental or unprincipled fashion.

Of course, the Sklarian can respond: "Not at all! The property is correlated with inertial effects. There is nothing unprincipled about its distribution." While this is true, it raises the question of whether it truly helps. The problem facing relationists is to explain how and why inertial effects arise as they do. Sklar addresses this by introducing a primitive property; the possession of this property is proposed as the explanation for inertial effects. Yet, and this is the real issue, all we are told about this property is that it is "that property which is found wherever inertial effects are found." In contrast, the Newtonian offers a richer account: there is a difference between objects that experience inertial effects and those that do not—namely, inertial effects are experienced by objects undergoing absolute acceleration. If we accept the idea that some objects are moving uniformly with respect to absolute space while others are not, we have a principled explanation of what distinguishes those objects that experience inertial effects from those that do not. The Sklarian cannot provide this level of explanation.

At this stage of the discussion, the Newtonian is clearly ahead in terms of theoretical points, but the game is not yet over. We will next explore ways to improve the Newtonian account, but we shall also see that this revision gives rise to new problems and difficulties.

---

### Motion in Spacetime

12.1 Newtonian Spacetime

Newton may be ahead in theoretical points, but his account is still hindered by a serious issue. To account for inertial effects, Newton recognized the need for absolute acceleration; having recognized absolute acceleration, he also had to acknowledge absolute velocity—the change of which constitutes absolute acceleration—despite the fact that absolute velocity has no physical consequences. By positing absolute velocities, Newtonians expose themselves to claims that their theory gives rise to empirically indistinguishable states of affairs. Which of the infinite number of possible universes generated by Leibniz's kinematic shift do we inhabit? Which corresponds to the actual world? If Newton's theory is correct, there is no empirical test that could be performed to reveal the answer.

Since the work of Cartan and others in the 1920s and 1930s, Newtonians have discovered a better way to formulate Newtonian theory—one that eliminates absolute velocities while retaining, in a sense, absolute accelerations. This improved formulation involves two steps: first, Newton's theory is formulated in terms of "spacetime"; second, this Newtonian spacetime is discarded in favor of neo-Newtonian, or Galilean, spacetime.Before we can appreciate the difference between these two approaches, we need to understand what a spacetime is. We will then consider some of the ways that spacetimes can differ. However, there are some misconceptions to address. Upon hearing the term "spacetime," one naturally thinks of Einstein and relativity theory. While Einstein's special and general theories of relativity are indeed formulated in terms of spacetime, they employ a specific form of spacetime—one in which spatial and temporal intervals are relativized to reference frames. However, spacetime can take many forms, including classical spacetimes that do not share this feature.

Another misconception involves the four-dimensional Block view of the universe. It is certainly true that spacetimes are often construed in this way, but it would be a mistake to assume that they must be. There are alternatives. For the time being, to avoid unnecessary complication, we can overlook this point. In what follows, I will assume that all parts of a spacetime coexist in a four-dimensional ensemble.

There are two ways to approach a spacetime: top-down and bottom-up. The top-down approach starts with a complicated spacetime—Newtonian, for example—and then subtracts various elements of structure. The bottom-up approach begins with the elementary components of a spacetime—a collection of distinct individual points—and then adds different types of structure. We will be following the top-down route.

Newton viewed space as an infinite and immutable three-dimensional Euclidean continuum of points, all of which endure through time (which is also infinite). To convert this space + time model into a spacetime, we replace Newton's enduring spatial points with a succession of momentary and numerically distinct spacetime points. This shift in perspective moves from points persisting through time to an array of spacetime points. Newtonian space is divided into distinct segments, and the collection of spacetime points constitutes a four-dimensional continuum—a spacetime.

A spacetime continuum (or manifold) is composed of numerically distinct points-at-times, rather than the enduring points of classical space. While spacetime and the Block view are compatible, it is also possible to combine the spacetime view with a dynamic conception of time. A Growing Block theorist, for example, would hold that spacetime increases in temporal size as layers of spacetime points come into being.

Just as a three-dimensional volume (think of a brick) can be thought of as being composed of a collection of two-dimensional planes, a four-dimensional volume can be regarded as a collection of three-dimensional volumes, or hyperplanes. Newtonian space at different moments in time can be represented by these three-dimensional volumes, and a succession of these composes a four-dimensional spacetime.

In Newtonian spacetime, every point is at a determinate spatial and temporal distance from every other point. Vertical lines through these three-dimensional slices indicate points at different times that share the same spatial location.

Distinguishing absolute velocity from absolute acceleration in Newtonian spacetime is straightforward. Persisting material objects are represented in spacetime diagrams by worldlines. Each worldline represents an object persisting through a succession of different spacetime points; the entire worldline depicts the object's complete history. Objects at absolute rest have vertical worldlines, while objects in uniform motion have straight worldlines that cross the three-dimensional layers at an angle; the greater the deviation from vertical, the faster the velocity. Curved lines represent worldlines of bodies undergoing absolute acceleration; the steeper the curve, the greater the acceleration. Objects in inertial motion are not moving relative to one another; the distance between their worldlines remains constant over time.

Looking at spacetime more generally, we can distinguish between inertial and non-inertial trajectories. These are continuous successions of spacetime points, occupied or unoccupied; straight paths are inertial, while curved paths indicate accelerated motion. Vertical lines represent absolute rest, each corresponding to an enduring spatial point.

Taken together, the collection of straight lines—vertical and non-vertical—represents the inertial structure of spacetime. These lines represent all possible inertial trajectories that bodies could take.

MOTION IN SPACETIME

Neo-Newtonian Spacetime  
To turn a Newtonian spacetime into a neo-Newtonian (or Galilean) spacetime is not difficult. In Newtonian spacetimes, there are distance relations between points in different simultaneity hyperplanes. If we eliminate these distance relations, leaving everything else unchanged, we are left with a neo-Newtonian spacetime.

Once the distance relations between hyperplanes are abolished, it no longer makes sense to talk about the distance separating a point in one hyperplane from a point in a different (earlier or later) hyperplane. Moreover, the concept of "same place" cannot be applied over time since points at different times are at no distance from one another; only points that are simultaneous have spatial distance between them.

Since all other aspects of Newtonian spacetime are preserved, the temporal relations between hyperplanes remain intact, and the points within a given hyperplane are simultaneous. Crucially, the distinction between straight and curved paths is retained despite the elimination of transtemporal spatial distance. This is achieved by first establishing coordinates for each three-dimensional space, allowing points to be assigned numerical values and distances between simultaneous points defined. Next, sequences of points through successive three-dimensional spaces are specified to be continuous. Distinguishing straight from curved sequences is known as specifying the "affine structure" of the spacetime; specifying straight paths is known as supplying the "connection." These straight lines represent inertial paths—those bodies follow if no forces act on them—while curved paths correspond to accelerated motion.

To better understand the difference between Newtonian and neo-Newtonian spacetime, consider two neo-Newtonian inertial frames, P and Q. As in the Newtonian case, P is represented by a collection of parallel vertical worldlines, and Q by a collection of slanting worldlines. An "inertial transformation" realigns the hyperplanes so that the lines of the Q-frame become vertical, making Q the zero-velocity frame. Such transformations preserve inertial structure.

In neo-Newtonian spacetime, every inertial frame can be transformed into the zero-velocity frame by an inertial transformation, which preserves the distinction between straight and curved lines, and hence between uniform and accelerated motion. Given that there are no distance relations between distinct hyperplanes, there is no "right" way to align them, and selecting one frame as "privileged" or motionless is entirely arbitrary.The right is an alternative representation of the same spacetime—created by altering the distance relations between hyperplanes—in which the Q-frame is the zero-velocity state.

Every inertial frame can be transformed into the zero-velocity frame by an inertial transformation (or realignment of hyperplanes). Since there are no distance relations between hyperplanes, there is no fact of the matter as to which of these representations is correct. In a Newtonian spacetime, there are distance relations between hyperplanes, and so one of these representations will reflect reality; the remainder will not.

In Euclidean plane geometry, transformations that leave the structure of the space unchanged are called "symmetries." The symmetries of the plane are rotation about a point (by any angle), reflection about any axis, and translation (or displacement) in any direction by any distance. The geometry of a shape remains the same under these transformations. For the same reason, inertial transformations are symmetries within a neo-Newtonian spacetime, for they leave spacetime structures unchanged. Different spacetimes have different symmetries; for example, the Minkowski spacetime of special relativity has "Lorentz transformations" as symmetries.

Velocity is defined as distance traveled divided by time taken; since the notion of distance over time is undefined in neo-Newtonian spacetimes, there are no absolute velocities in such spacetimes. However, there are absolute accelerations, and these can easily be measured empirically. Suppose that we have an object that we have reason to believe is accelerating (for example, it is being boosted by a rocket motor). Select some part of its trajectory as the "starting point" and choose an inertial frame of reference for which the object is at rest at this time. Now select a later part of its trajectory as the "end point," and another inertial frame with respect to which the object is now at rest. To find the absolute velocity change, ascertain the relative velocity of the second inertial frame with respect to the first. This gives the absolute velocity change, one that observers in all inertial frames can agree on. 

Dividing this absolute velocity change by the time separating the starting and end points gives the average absolute acceleration of the object over this segment of its trajectory. The instantaneous absolute acceleration can be calculated using standard methods.

The following points may help your understanding of neo-Newtonian spacetime:

- "This particle is moving at constant velocity" makes sense; it means that the worldline is straight.  
- "This particle has a speed of 10 cm/s" does not make sense, since a definite time and spatial distance between events are required, and in neo-Newtonian spacetime, the spatial distance is not well defined.
- "This particle is at rest" does not make sense. 
- "This particle collided with that one" makes sense; two particles collide if their worldlines intersect. 
- "This rope is straight" makes sense; the claim is true if every temporal stage of the rope is straight in every three-dimensional space (or hyperplane). 
- "This particle has traveled 10 cm" does not make sense; spatial distances over time have no well-defined meaning here. 
- "Particle A is moving faster than particle B" does not make sense; in some frames, A is at rest and B is moving, and vice versa, with all frames equally legitimate. 
- "Particle A is moving at 10 cm/s relative to particle B" makes sense.

Neo-Newtonian spacetime seems very odd initially, since we are accustomed to assuming that where we are now is spatially related to where we were a little while ago. But some writers suggest it is not so strange. Geroch says that the neo-Newtonian view, while it takes some getting used to, is a simple and natural attitude about how space and time operate, perhaps even the only reasonable attitude to take. Similarly, Huggett notes that it seems plausible that spacetime really is this way: the question of how far in space you are now from where you were a minute ago may have no answer.

Consider this: if the past is unreal, completely gone from existence, then it makes no sense to think of yourself as now being some distance away from where you were.

There is an amusing twist here, for one might think there is no metaphysic more hostile to this view of the past than the four-dimensionalist way of thinking about space and time. However, a neo-Newtonian spacetime, equipped with a well-defined simultaneity relation, is not incompatible with the view that only the present is real.

It is also interesting that the Minkowski spacetime of special relativity has no place for absolute velocity, though it retains absolute acceleration. But the reasons differ: neo-Newtonian spacetime maintains rigid temporal relations but abandons spatial distance over time, whereas Minkowski spacetime abandons absolute temporal intervals. Since velocity measures how much space is traversed in a given time interval, the notion can be undermined either by losing spatial distance over time or by losing absolute temporal intervals.

Geroch observes that the transitions from Aristotelian to Galilean views, and beyond in physics, mean fewer things make sense, noting that notions like "position" and "speed" lose their usual meaning in quantum mechanics. He suggests that one measure of the importance of new ideas in physics is how many familiar notions we are forced to give up.

The claim that advances in physics produce "fewer things making sense" can mean either that worldviews become utterly unintelligible, or that we learn to make sense of the world despite losing basic assumptions. It is up to the reader to judge which applies to neo-Newtonian spacetime.

---

Assuming these points are reasonably clear, we can proceed.

The move to neo-Newtonian spacetime solves the problem posed by the undetectability of absolute velocity. Velocity measures distance over time; since points in successive hyperplanes are not at any distance from each other, it no longer makes sense to talk about absolute velocity or how fast a body is moving relative to absolute space.

In practice, we set up reference frames of bodies at rest with respect to one another and measure velocities relative to these. Yet other equally valid reference frames exist, each with its own rest state and relative velocities. Since absolute motion or rest no longer exist, no inertial frame is privileged, and there is no frame-independent fact about what our velocity really is. The only facts about velocity are relative velocities.

Thus, by situating Newtonian dynamics within neo-Newtonian spacetime, the serious Leibnizian objection is overcome. Since absolute velocities are no longer meaningful, the Newtonian is no longer guilty of positing innumerable empirically undetectable physical states.However, the move to neo-Newtonian spacetime does not solve the problem of the static shift. The Leibnizian could argue that, provided inter-object spatial relations are retained, the whole system of material bodies could, in each hyperplane, be located at a different and distant collection of spacetime locations. However, as we saw earlier, the static shift argument only has bite for those in thrall to the principle of sufficient reason, and these days, few are.

We should pause before drawing the conclusion that Newton has been fully vindicated by the move from Newtonian to neo-Newtonian spacetime. The debate between the substantivalist (or absolutist) and the relationist takes on a somewhat different complexion in the neo-Newtonian context, as we shall now see.

### The Charge of Explanatory Impotence

Teller (1991) questions the extent to which the substantivalist neo-Newtonian actually explains inertial effects. Teller suggests that in the context of standard Newtonian space, the substantivalist's complaint against the relationist is best construed thus:

“We know that there are systematic connections between acceleration and inertial effects. With only relative accelerations to which to appeal, relationists have no way of distinguishing between the relative accelerations which do and those which do not experience inertial effects. Thus, they have no way of giving a systematic description of the relation between acceleration and inertial effects or of providing an explanation which such a systematic description might constitute.” (1991: 370)

In response, the relationist can make Sklar's move and bite the bullet by maintaining that there is a primitive unexplained property (which we can call "absolute acceleration," if we like), which is systematically associated with inertial effects. This meets the challenge of the substantivalist. The relationist can argue that the difference between relative accelerations that are, and are not, associated with inertial effects is that the former possess this primitive property while the latter do not. But this seems an unappealing move. The substantivalist will argue:

“Sklar's move is ad hoc. To say that inertial effects are always accompanied by a primitive property, a property that is characterized solely as the property that goes with inertial effects, amounts to nothing more than saying that inertial effects are found where they are found. The substantivalist isn't restricted to this truism, for the substantivalist can say that absolute acceleration can be characterized independently of inertial effects, in terms of motion with respect to space (or spacetime). It is this independently characterized absolute acceleration that is connected with inertial effects.”

Teller now states his claim. This plausible sketch of an argument relies on a slide from characterizing absolute acceleration relative to space, to characterizing absolute acceleration relative to spacetime. The argument is compelling in the context of absolute Newtonian space, but not in the context of spacetime, particularly neo-Newtonian spacetime. Since there is good reason for rejecting Newtonian space in favor of neo-Newtonian spacetime, the substantivalist's argument against Sklar has no force, for it turns out that the neo-Newtonian substantivalist is in a position that is precisely analogous to that of Sklar's primitive property theorist.

The justification for this claim runs as follows. In Newtonian space, the distinction between absolute and merely relative acceleration is real and clear: if an object is located at the same point as time passes, it is at absolute rest; if an object is located at different points at different times, such that equal times are accompanied by equal changes in distance, and the changes in distance are always in the same direction, then the object is in uniform absolute motion; and all other motions constitute absolute accelerations. In neo-Newtonian spacetime, the situation is quite different: absolute velocity is not well defined, and absolute acceleration consists in having a curved worldline, rather than in change of absolute velocity. So the notion of worldline curvature is key. 

Neo-Newtonians construct their spacetime starting with a collection of spacetime points, organized into distinct Euclidean three-dimensional spaces—one for each moment of time—and impose additional structure. They specify which points are very close together, count these as "next to one another," and further specify continuous sequences of points, which represent possible trajectories through spacetime. They also specify a "connection" on the space, which determines which trajectories are straight and which are curved.

Straight trajectories are identified with inertial paths. Once this connection is specified, a distinction arises between absolute or true acceleration and merely relative or apparent acceleration. However, a problem emerges: which trajectories are the ones to be called straight, representing unaccelerated motion? There is nothing in the collection of bare spacetime points and their "next to" relations to answer this question.

The facts presented so far do not enable a substantivalist to say more than that the trajectories labeled "straight" are just those that exhibit no inertial effects—ones which, if followed by an object, describe motion free of inertial effects. Such a substantivalist has no more of an account of inertial effects than Sklar's relationist. The only guide the neo-Newtonian has as to which trajectories are to be construed as straight, and which are curved, is the production of inertial effects. So, just as the Sklarist relationist can do no more than state that trajectories associated with inertial effects are the trajectories associated with inertial effects, so too for the substantivalist. Consequently, it seems plain that the neo-Newtonian substantivalist provides only a trivial explanation of the origin of inertial effects.

### 12.6 A Rebuttal

The underlying worry is that the move to neo-Newtonian spacetime seems to have fundamentally altered what absolute acceleration consists of. Since absolute velocity is no longer well defined, it can no longer be understood as a change of rate of motion relative to an underlying substructure. As a consequence, our intuitive grasp of what absolute acceleration consists of has largely vanished, making it difficult to see what role substantival spacetime plays in the new scheme.

However, while there is some truth in this, Teller's argument is suspect. According to Teller, the substantivalist constructs a spacetime to fit observed interactions between bodies and decides which point sequences to count as straight by looking to inertial forces. He apparently has no other way of distinguishing between straight and curved spacetime point sequences. But this view is potentially misleading: for the substantivalist, spacetime is a real entity with its own distinctive structure.

Constructing a neo-Newtonian spacetime is a mathematical modeling of this independently existing entity, not constituting spacetime itself. From the substantivalist perspective, inertial structure is not introduced into spacetime by the physicist; rather, it is a real feature of an autonomous physical entity. The substantivalist can claim that there is a real distinction in spacetime between straight and curved trajectories, and inertial effects occur when objects follow curved trajectories. So, unlike Sklar, who can do no more than assert that inertial effects accompany a primitive property characterized solely by their occurrence, the substantivalist can characterize absolute acceleration independently of inertial effects, in terms of curved trajectories through neo-Newtonian spacetime.

While this reply may partly dispel Teller’s doubts, the question remains: what exactly is the substantivalist offering by way of an explanation of inertial effects? One answer is that an object suffering inertial effects possesses a distinctive physical property, and there is a law of nature linking this property’s occurrence and magnitude to curved spatiotemporal paths.

There is nothing in principle wrong with such an answer: it is conceivable that laws exist causing objects following certain trajectories through spacetime to possess intrinsic properties of a certain kind. However, it would be a mistake to view inertial effects as involving intrinsic properties of a distinctive kind.Once this point is appreciated, it becomes clearer just what the neo-Newtonian is offering by way of an explanation of inertial forces.

What do inertial effects consist of? Typically, they consist of internal stresses between the component parts of a body. Objects follow inertial paths unless acted upon by a force, which is generated by gravity, magnetism, or an expenditure of energy. When objects are forced off their inertial path, this is typically achieved by applying force to one part of the object only, which sets up tensions within the object: that is, some parts of the object start exerting forces on other parts. In the case of rotating globes, the tension is registered in the cord, which exerts a force on the globes whose inertial motions are tangential to their circular motion. When a jet plane deviates from its inertial motion, it pushes those parts of your body that are in contact with it off their inertial paths, and you feel this as pressure on your skin. Since a similar motion is not imparted to the other parts of your body, a tension is created as your rear parts push against your middle parts, often accompanied by a sensation of queasiness. All this can be explained without positing any special properties. The neo-Newtonian's explanation of inertial effects is thus simple: the internal stresses arise when absolute velocity differences arise. The absolute differences are those that observers in all inertial reference frames will agree upon despite differing in their views as to who is moving and who is stationary.

The notion of inertial paths lies at the heart of this account. The neo-Newtonian says something like this: certain paths through spacetime are privileged by the natural laws governing the relationship between material bodies and spacetime; the relevant natural law is that bodies continue to move along these paths unless acted upon by a force exerted by another moving body, a locally generated impulse such as a jet engine, or a distant attractive or repulsive impulse like gravitation or magnetism; and these nomologically privileged paths are the inertial paths. This explanation seems non-trivial and eminently reasonable.

### 12.7 Newtonian Spacetime Relationism

Another way to see how the debate between relationists and substantivalists may be affected by adopting the spacetime perspective comes from Maudlin’s recent article. Maudlin defends the claims that, in Newtonian spacetime, the relationist is in a very strong position because the argument from inertial effects is without force; however, this changes once we move to neo-Newtonian spacetime, which is "an extraordinarily hostile environment for the relationist."

Returning to Newton's buckets and globes and the argument from inertial effects, Maudlin poses this question: exactly which sorts of relationism does this argument work against? Suppose a relationist were to stipulate that the only spatiotemporal relations are simultaneity, temporal succession, and spatial distance relations between simultaneous events. For such a relationist—a Leibnizian relationist as Maudlin calls it—the situations that the substantivalist describes as involving different rotations are all exactly alike physically. They must be, since there is no spatial distance relation between states of affairs at different times. Hence, there is no way of distinguishing a pair of globes at rest from a pair that are rotating. In a case where the substantivalist explains different observed tensions by reference to different rates of rotation, the relationist can say nothing: there are simply the different tensions, which are just found to occur.

Introducing Newtonian absolute space solves this problem. Absolute space consists of points that endure through time, providing a reference frame that allows defining distances between simultaneous and non-simultaneous events. Newton's ability to account for inertial effects depends not directly on positing absolute space, but on extending the domain of the spatial distance relation to include non-simultaneous occurrences. This suggests a new form of relationism called Newtonian (spacetime) relationism.

The advocate of this doctrine rejects absolute space and maintains that all spatiotemporal facts are about relations between material bodies, but these relations now include distance relations between non-simultaneous events. Material objects are conceived in a four-dimensional spacetime way, consisting of a succession of temporal parts. This change gives the relationist additional resources. The Newtonian relationist will say that a body is at absolute rest if the spatial distance between its successive temporal stages at different times is zero, and that an object is in absolute uniform motion if its successive temporal stages are separated by the same spatial distance per unit time. The stages of an object undergoing linear acceleration are separated by different distances per unit time.

Rotation poses no problem: the particles in a rotating sphere have different distance relations to one another at different times, even though they retain the same distance relations at any given time. With these augmentations, the Newtonian relationist can accommodate the bucket example. In the stationary case, the successive bucket stages are aligned on top of one another in spacetime. In the rotating case, they are not; each stage is rotated to some degree with respect to its immediate predecessor.

Once all distance and direction relations between material objects are specified, the entire system can be regarded as situated or "embedded" in a full Newtonian spacetime: a four-dimensional continuum of points, some occupied and many not. The relationist does not regard this spacetime as a real entity but as a fictional representation useful for calculation and prediction. The only real spacetime locations recognized are those occupied by material things, along with their spatial relations. The substantivalist, on the other hand, regards the material bodies and their spatial relations as just one part of the totality of real spatiotemporal facts. 

This restriction provides the relationist a solution to the static shift problem, or the problem of initial location. For any given Newtonian spacetime regarded as an infinite four-dimensional continuum, there are indefinitely many ways to embed a given system of material bodies into it that preserve all spatiotemporal relations between the bodies. For instance, the system could be embedded at different locations, or set at rest relative to the spacetime, or given a uniform velocity of any magnitude. Since the embedding space is fictional, the relationist does not regard these embeddings as physically distinct states; each embedding simply provides a different way of representing the same physical state. Maudlin suggests this option has been overlooked historically because it requires a four-dimensional perspective and an event ontology, but it shows Newton's bucket argument is not effective against relationism per se. Maudlin concludes, "Relations that are sufficiently rich can provide the means for explaining inertial effects."

### MOTION IN SPACETIME

12.8 Neo-Newtonian spacetime relationism

Can the same approach work in neo-Newtonian spacetime? Can the relationist devise a satisfactory theory of dynamics if all that exists are material objects whose successive temporal parts are linked by the spatiotemporal relations recognized by the neo-Newtonian? Maudlin suggests not. Neo-Newtonian spacetime lacks the complete four-dimensional metric provided by absolute space. It results from adding to Leibnizian spacetime an affine connection, which roughly specifies which spacetime trajectories are straight, and hence inertial.

A reasonable version of neo-Newtonian relationism would add to the Leibnizian relations a three-place relation describing collinearity of events along inertial trajectories. Specifying these collinearity relations completely would fix the affine connection. However, the relationist is given only the collinearity relations among occupied points. This information is usually too limited to specify an embedding of the occupied trajectories into full spacetime, up to the freedom associated with choosing a reference frame.

For example, consider two particles rotating about their common center of mass. Until the first rotation is complete, no triple of occupied event locations are collinear. Even after many rotations, the collinearity relations among occupied points are consistent with any periodic rotation, uniform or not.So, while the Newtonian relationist could make use of the full power of Newtonian absolute space, starting only with the relations between occupied points, embedding them in a fictional space, and deriving via Newtonian mechanics exhaustive predictions about the future relations between occupied points, the neo-Newtonian cannot perform the same trick. Knowing, for example, that two particles remain at a constant distance through some period of time and that no three points in their world-lines are collinear, the neo-Newtonian relationist could not predict when, if ever, a triple of points would be collinear. Nor could inertial effects be predicted or explained since the absolute acceleration cannot be inferred from the data.

To simplify, suppose that we are dealing with two rotating particles in an otherwise empty universe. Imagine the two particles as black dots on a thin sheet of glass. In describing the evolution of this system, the relationist has to do two things: put the sheets into a particular order and provide them with an orientation with respect to one another. The temporal ordering is unproblematic (it is a given in neo-Newtonian contexts), but the orientation is not. If we draw an imaginary line between the particles, the relationist can align the successive sheets in any number of ways. If, say, the lines in successive sheets coincide vertically, the particles are at rest. If just 1 degree separates successive sheets, they are rotating but slowly; if the separation is 30 degrees, they are rotating more quickly, and so on.

Now, since there are no distance relations between non-simultaneous spacetime locations, it seems that the relationist not only has no means of specifying what the correct orientations are, but he cannot even recognize that the different orientations are physically distinct states of affairs. The only fact about the relative orientations of the slices available to the relationist is collinearity: the relation that holds between particles that lie on the same inertial path. Given this, the relationist can tell when a rotation is complete, but there are no facts about how fast the particles have been moving in the intervening period. The Newtonian relationist is in a very different position. Since there are distance relations between particles at different times, there are facts about how fast the particles are moving from moment to moment, and the behavior of the system through the relevant period of time is well defined. This is clearly not the case for the neo-Newtonian relationist.

The significance of this underdetermination is clearer for slightly more complex situations. When astronomers observe the behavior of triple star systems, they see the stars moving about in seemingly bizarre ways; the orbits are highly irregular, with stars quickly moving together and then shooting off again. Although the behavior of the three bodies seems random, it is in fact determined, and so, in principle, predictable using Newtonian theory, provided one is given certain initial information.

As Poincaré showed, if we are given two snapshots of the system showing the relative distances between the bodies, then, provided we know both the time interval between the shots and the relative orientations of the snapshots, the future behavior of the system is determined. However, if we lack information about the orientations, the future evolution of the system is not determined. These different "orientations" correspond to different initial absolute accelerations. Facts about absolute acceleration play a crucial role in how a system of three mutually gravitating bodies behaves over time.

The Newtonian relationist can view such systems in just the same way as the Newtonian substantivalist, since all the relevant distinctions are available to both. Not so for the neo-Newtonian relationist: in the absence of spatial distance relations between successive object stages, the absolute accelerations of objects are drastically underdefined.

In response to this problem, the relationist could adopt an instrumentalist strategy. Is there anything to prevent the relationist from using the substantival neo-Newtonian apparatus for predicting the behavior of moving bodies, while regarding this as nothing more than a fictional device useful for predictive purposes? Suppose that, when mapping his spacetime, the relationist "pretends" to be a substantivalist and uses maps that display unoccupied locations. When the map is complete, what remains after deleting all the unoccupied points and trajectories are world lines: straight and curved four-dimensional material objects. At any given time, the spatial distances between the relevant temporal stages of these objects are well defined.

So, too, are the intrinsic curvatures: either zero for straight worldlines, or non-zero for curved worldlines, with greater or lesser intrinsic curvatures for greater or lesser absolute accelerations. In trying to measure absolute accelerations, the neo-Newtonian relationist has exactly the same observational data as the neo-Newtonian substantivalist: inertial effects found in accelerating bodies, along with at-a-time distance relations. Moreover, the relationist can explain alterations in curvature in just the same ways as the substantivalist: in terms of collisions—worldlines that meet—and action-at-a-distance forces, such as gravity inducing worldlines to curve toward one another. Hence, it is not clear that the unoccupied positions and paths that the substantivalist posits are doing any real work.

If we suppose that the relationist's world is accurately represented by one of these spacetime diagrams, given the data he has, it is plausible that he could tell which one he lives in. More generally, given equal amounts of data, the relationist and substantivalist could work out equally accurate "maps" of their world.

This instrumentalist maneuver may seem harmless enough, but closer scrutiny reveals it to be dubious. The substantivalist provides a precise explanation of the system's behavior in terms of Newtonian dynamics; according to this theory, every particle at every moment has a definite absolute acceleration, a feature that plays an indispensable role in determining how the dynamic system evolves. Since the relationist does not recognize the same range of dynamic features as the substantivalist—particularly, the absolute accelerations of particles are often undefined—he cannot regard the Newtonian account as true. However, since the relationist has nothing to offer by way of an alternative theory of comparable empirical power, he has failed to show that the dynamical features he rejects are irrelevant to the physics of moving bodies. Consequently, although the relationist offers a more ontologically economical account, we have no reason to accept his account as true, nor to suppose that the discarded dynamical features do not exist.

12.9 Relationism Redux  
Is this the end of the story? Not necessarily. As Maudlin notes in connection with Newtonian relationism, "To defeat one tribe of relationists is not to vanquish the whole nation," because "the predictive and explanatory power of a relationist theory depends both on the nature of the spatiotemporal relations admitted and on the domain over which the relations are defined." Newtonian relationism emerged as a serious rival to Newtonian substantivalism when the domain of the distance relation was extended to include temporal parts of the same objects at different times. Might the objections to neo-Newtonian relationism be overcome if the relationist embraces a richer collection of spatiotemporal relations?

Consider again the system consisting of two rotating particles in an otherwise empty universe. It is true that in the absence of across-time spatial distances, as in a neo-Newtonian context, the relationist cannot distinguish between uniform and non-uniform periodic rotations, and so cannot discriminate physically distinct states of affairs. However, the relationist can solve this problem by introducing distance relations among simultaneous objects that have a specific direction as well as a size. There are two options: we can suppose either that distance relations have a relative orientation with respect to one another over time, or that distance relations have an absolute orientation. Since the latter avoids across-time comparisons, it remains more faithful to neo-Newtonian principles, and so this option will be considered here.

For an intuitive picture, imagine a motionless sphere in Newtonian spacetime. We now stipulate that every straight line connecting a point on the surface of the sphere to its center both possesses and defines a different intrinsic direction.Focus on just one of these lines and imagine many more with the same alignment, all originating at the center of the sphere but extending outwards for different distances, up to infinity; this collection of lines corresponds to all the potential spatial relations with the same intrinsic direction. A similar operation on the other radii generates the remaining spatial relations. Building a world is a matter of choosing different sorts of particles, plucking distance relations from the sphere, and fitting them all together. When a distance relation is removed from the sphere, it retains its original direction and resists reorientation. Since we are operating in a spacetime framework, strictly speaking, you are not connecting particles but rather particle-stages (momentary or very short-lived), and each configuration of particle-stages constitutes a temporal slice of your world.

Equipped with these newfangled distance relations, we can reconsider the two-particle system. If the particles are not rotating, their neighboring (but not simultaneous) temporal parts will be connected by a distance relation of the same size and the same intrinsic direction. If they are rotating, neighboring temporal stages of the particles will be connected by distance relations of different intrinsic directions. Should the rate of rotation be uniform, the angles between the distance relations will be the same for unit intervals of time; in cases of non-uniform rotations, the angular variation will be different for unit times.

Intrinsically directed distance relations solve some problems but not all.
