Unfortunately, despite its interest, Foster's argument is complex, multi-faceted, and often difficult; to do it full justice would require a lengthy exposition, and this is not the place for it. Consequently, I will be selective in my approach and confine my attentions to what he has to say about spaces that are substantival in character and taken to be ontologically basic components of the physical world. Foster develops similar (and equally potent) arguments against relationism, but since these depend more heavily on the sometimes exotic and unfamiliar framework employed in his book, I will leave them for interested readers to explore for themselves. In his more recent *The Succinct Case for Idealism* (1993), Foster himself employs just this strategy; in his still more recent *A World for Us* (2008), his approach is somewhat different.

Although the import of Foster's argument is ontological, whereas that of Poincaré's conventionalism is epistemological, in certain respects the routes they take are similar. Both arguments are concerned with spatial geometry, and there are similarities in approach: both employ thought experiments in which natural laws conspire to conceal the real structure of the underlying spatial reality. The similarities do not end there. The following sketch of an argument shows how Poincaré's conventionalism might be taken to have ontological implications:

1. Physical space has the geometry specified by our best physical theory.
2. This geometry is a matter of convention.
3. The character of objective reality is not a matter of convention.
4. Hence, the geometry of physical space does not belong to objective reality.
5. As the bearer of this geometry, physical space does not belong to objective reality either.

Whatever its merits, this reasoning will not convince those who adopt a realist stance towards scientific theories, since they will reject conventionalism and thus point 2. Foster's argument poses a threat even for realists. He begins by assuming that there is a physical world and that our best scientific theories provide accurate guides to its laws and structure. Then, having thus assumed that the scientific picture of the world is broadly correct, he goes on to show that this assumption is incompatible with adopting a realistic stance with respect to physical space.

Although I will be concentrating on Foster's spatial anti-realism rather than his more general physical anti-realism, a brief comment on the transition from the latter to the former is in order. It may not be immediately apparent why an argument that threatens the existence of physical space is a threat to the existence of the physical world per se.Supposing that such an argument were successful, shouldn't we conclude not that the physical world doesn't exist, but rather that it consists solely of material objects? Bearing in mind what was said in Chapter 9 about the constraining and connecting role that space plays, this is not a plausible option. Even if we could make sense of individual physical objects existing in the absence of space, it is surely uncontroversial that for a physical world to exist, these objects must be spatially connected. In the absence of space (whether construed relationally or substantively), this is simply impossible.

### 15.2 The Intrinsic and the Inscrutable

We can start with a doctrine that plays an important role in what is to come. Foster sometimes calls it "the inscrutability of physical content." It can be broken down into a conjunction of claims, one of which has two components:

1. The content thesis: at least some material things possess their own intrinsic natures.
2. The inscrutability thesis: (i) it is impossible for us to know anything about these intrinsic natures; (ii) these intrinsic natures do not fall within the scope of physical science.

If "intrinsic nature" is taken to mean "intrinsic property," 1 is clearly true, but 2 is equally clearly false. Shape, size, mass, color, impenetrability, and charge are commonly taken to be intrinsic properties of material bodies, but all are eminently scrutable. However, in talking of "intrinsic natures," Foster is not referring to "intrinsic properties" in this familiar sense, as the following passage makes plain:

"From our observations, and from the way these support certain kinds of explanatory theory, we may be able to establish the existence of an external space with a certain geometrical structure... we can never find out what ... the space is like in itself... Likewise, while, by the same empirical means, we may be able to establish the existence of external objects located in this space... we can never discover the ultimate nature of their space-filling content... we can never discover, beyond a knowledge of their spatiotemporal properties, what the simplest objects (the elementary particles) are like in themselves..." (Foster 1993: 295)

The idea that there is something that elementary particles "are like in themselves" has to be taken in the right way. The claim is not that "there is something that it is like to be an elementary particle"; Foster is not saying that elementary particles are conscious entities. The claim is simply that the most basic physical things have some intrinsic characteristics over and above their spatiotemporal and causal properties—over and above all the properties we ever actually ascribe to physical things. 

Put in this way, the content thesis may well seem to lack motivation. Why believe that these additional properties exist? In fact, as quickly becomes apparent, the content thesis has a good deal of plausibility. Consider the intrinsic properties we do ascribe to ordinary physical objects. Some properties are spatiotemporal: size, shape, and motion fall into this category. Shape, size, and mode of arrangement are sometimes referred to as "structural" properties, for obvious reasons.

As for the other properties, it may not be immediately obvious, but they are all causal powers; that is, capacities to produce specific types of change in (or to be changed by) other objects. This is often clearly the case. Objects that are rigid have the capacity to retain their shape under pressure, while objects that are brittle tend to shatter when struck. Electric charge is associated with a variety of causal properties, such as the capacity to attract or repel other charged objects and the ability to interact with magnetic fields. Mass is also a causal property: more massive objects are harder to stop and require more pushing to get them moving. Likewise, impenetrability: if we know that two particles are impenetrable with respect to each other, we understand that neither can occupy the space occupied by the other, meaning each has the capacity to repel the other at its borders.

Less obviously, physical color is a causal capacity, too. Or at least, it is if we follow Locke, Foster, and many others in rejecting "naive realism" and subscribing to a "representative" theory of perception. According to this theory, to say that a physical object has a certain color amounts to saying that the object is causally disposed to produce in perceivers a particular kind of visual experience under certain conditions, such as normal daylight. The same applies to all other secondary qualities, such as warmth, texture, and solidity, as these feature in our experience.

The question now arises as to whether all non-structural intrinsic properties could be causal or dispositional. If so, then the physical world as a whole might consist of nothing but "bare powers" or "bare potentialities"; bare because these potentialities are not grounded in anything that is not also a potentiality. Foster maintains that this conception of physical reality is incoherent: the causal potentialities that physical things possess must be grounded in something that is not merely a potentiality. Therefore, physical things must possess some properties that are not mere potentialities. These non-dispositional or "categorical" properties are the "intrinsic natures" referred to in the content thesis.

Foster is certainly right about one thing: the idea of a world consisting of nothing but bare powers is very odd indeed. In a real sense, nothing can ever happen in such a world. The changes that do occur would be only alterations in some of the various potentialities that exist. But since everything that ever exists is a potentiality, any activation of a power only results in an alteration in some other potentiality, which in turn can never result in any actuality. Is a world without anything that is more than a potentiality—a world in which activating any potentiality only brings about changes in other potentialities—really possible? I suspect not.

Consider a simple world consisting of just two atoms moving in space. Atom A1 has the power to repel another atom, A2, should it ever approach, and A2 has the power to repel A1. We can ask: what is atom A1? Given that it is nothing but a spatially located mobile disposition, we can only answer that A1 is the capacity to repel A2. But what is A2 if not the power to repel A1? This seems problematic. To make sense of what A1 is, we need to understand what it is a power to do, but all A1 can do is repel something that is itself no more than a power to repel A1, and the same applies to A2, which is merely a power to repel something that is in turn a power to repel A2. It seems that, no matter where we begin, we are left with nothing.

Before we can consider A1 to be something that exists at all, we need to specify what it is able to do, but we cannot do this, because all it can do is repel A2, and the status of A2 as something that exists is only certain when we state what it can do; but this cannot be done, since all A2 can do is repel A1, whose status as an existing thing we have yet to secure. Thus, it seems impossible for the universe we are envisaging to contain anything at all, contrary to the stipulation that it contains two atoms.

This argument concerned only two atoms and a single causal power. However, adding other atoms—each consisting of nothing but the power to repel others—will not alter the situation. Moreover, adding further causal powers will not improve matters, unless they are powers to affect something intrinsic, that is, non-dispositional, which we have ruled out. Returning to our two-atom universe, suppose each atom has the power to attract the other, in addition to their powers of repulsion. As soon as we try to articulate what each atom's attractive power is—the power to attract A2—we encounter the same problem as before: A1 is the power to attract A2, which is itself no more than the power to attract A1, and so on. Adding that A2 possesses the power to repel A1 achieves nothing, since the power of mutual repulsion alone is insufficient to secure either atom's existence. Once again, adding purely dispositional atoms does nothing to improve matters.

The "bare power" ontology may be incoherent, but does it follow that all physical things must possess an intrinsic nature in addition to their causal powers? Probably not. However, if some physical things are nothing more than mobile clusters of causal potentiality, they can only exist because there are other physical things that do possess intrinsic natures. As for which physical things fall into the latter category, Foster is adamant on one point: a substantival space must possess an intrinsic nature of its own. He is surely correct about this, assuming that a substantival space, as a fundamental ingredient of physical reality, does not depend for its existence on any other type of physical thing.

Let us now turn from the content thesis to the inscrutability thesis. Now that we have a better understanding of what Foster means by "intrinsic nature," it is hard to disagree with the claim that we know nothing about them. We know that some physical things have intrinsic natures, but this is all we know: the precise character of these intrinsic natures remains a complete mystery.Science tells us a great deal about the intrinsic properties that physical things possess, but as we have already seen, since these properties are entirely causal or structural in character, they fail to provide insights about the intrinsic natures of the things that possess these properties.

Foster goes further: the inscrutability thesis also includes claim (ii), according to which intrinsic natures do not even fall within the scope of physical science. Physical concepts—the concepts that it is legitimate to apply to the physical world in scientific theorizing—are, without exception, entirely neutral on the question of intrinsic natures. This "topic neutrality" thesis is a conceptual truth, or so Foster maintains: genuine physical concepts are restricted to those that can feature in an empirically testable theory, and claims about intrinsic natures cannot feature in such theories.

An example will help bring these claims into clearer focus. Imagine a world superficially very similar to our own, but consisting of a substantival space and a single type of atom—a simple and indivisible particle of small but finite size. Physicists have a well-confirmed and seemingly complete theory of atomic interactions and motions, and this theory—let's call it T—is topic neutral. Now, there are any number of competitor theories that are not topic neutral. These theories are identical to T in all respects save one: they attribute intrinsic natures to both atoms and space. For our purposes, we will consider just two of these competitors. According to T1, atoms are spheres of an intrinsic quality Q1, whereas space consists of an infinite expanse of an intrinsic quality Q2. According to T2, space is filled with Q1, and atoms are pervaded by Q2.

Foster does not claim that we can make no sense whatsoever of theories such as T1 and T2; in fact, we can, especially if we give the posited qualities a positive interpretation in phenomenal terms (for example, by thinking of Q1 as red and Q2 as blue, as these colors feature in our experience). However, it is one thing for a claim to be intelligible and another for it to be an intelligible scientific claim. Real-life physicists do not produce theories such as T1 and T2, and any physicist who did would not be taken seriously; intrinsic natures, in Foster's sense, play no role in actual scientific theorizing.

Although the difference between T1 and T2 is easy to depict and imagine, it would be impossible to detect in practice. Since both T1 and T2 ascribe exactly the same causal powers to atoms and space as T does, they make no novel or testable predictions: objects composed of Q1-type atoms look and behave in the same way as objects composed of Q2-type atoms. Quite generally, the only properties that science recognizes or should recognize are those that contribute to explaining and predicting the observable features of the physical world.

All such properties are causal in character or have causal implications in the manner of structural properties. It is precisely because of their causal character that they can contribute to explaining the observed behavior of material things. Since intrinsic natures are properties that things possess over and above all their causal and structural characteristics, they do not fall within the proper purview of science. Hence, Foster's claim: the concepts that can legitimately feature in science are topic neutral.

Substantivalism, in its traditional guise, is the doctrine that space is an entity in its own right, possessing its own inherent structure. The existence and structure of such space is, to some degree at least, independent of the existence, distribution, and behavior of any material bodies that it may contain. If we follow Foster and subscribe to the content thesis, then a substantival space will also possess an intrinsic qualitative nature of its own. Given the inscrutability thesis, we cannot hope to discover what the intrinsic nature of our own space is. Nonetheless, it is easy to imagine how a space could have its own intrinsic nature. A Flatland space could take the form of a homogeneous circular blue plane. To imagine a space with the same structure but a different intrinsic nature, simply picture a homogeneous circular red plane. The surface of a red sphere represents a Flatland space with the same intrinsic nature as the latter but a different structure. Three-dimensional analogues of flat two-dimensional spaces are easily imagined—just picture a color-pervaded volume. Three-dimensional analogues of curved Flatland spaces are beyond our imagining, but certainly possible. These examples make plain that the geometry of such spaces and their intrinsic natures are linked, but the determination goes only one way: the geometry of a space is determined by the structure of its qualitative "filling," but spaces of different qualitative types can have the same geometrical structure.

Foster's argument is general in scope but easier to expound in the context of simple spaces. So, let us suppose that our space—or spacetime—call it S—is Newtonian in character, and that material bodies are composed of a single kind of atom. To make matters more concrete, we can imagine these atoms as minuscule mobile spheres of red and S as an unbounded volume of uniform grey. Thinking of these items thus is a useful way of reminding ourselves that they possess intrinsic natures, but these "colors" are not visible to us. S does not impede light and so is entirely transparent, whereas material objects reflect light in different ways and so have different observable colors, depending on the way their surface atoms are configured.

Since the material things we perceive are composed of atoms moving through S, it is natural to think that S and physical space—call it P—are one and the same. Foster's key claim is that S and P cannot possibly be one and the same: they are, and must be, numerically distinct entities. The argument for this conclusion relies on the possibility of certain modes of deviancy, ways in which P and S might differ from one another. One mode of deviancy features variations in the intrinsic natures of S and its occupants. For example, S could contain a cube-shaped region, R, which is systematically linked (through a law of nature) to variations in the intrinsic natures of atoms: as atoms enter R, they change from "red" to "blue" and remain "blue" until they leave. Both sorts of atoms have exactly the same causal powers, so they behave in exactly the same ways. Alternatively, we can suppose that R itself has a different qualitative nature than the rest of S; for example, it is a different shade of grey. Possibilities such as these pose no threat to the status of the physical world; there is no obstacle to our holding that S and P are one and the same space. Nonetheless, they are a useful way of bringing to the fore the implications of the inscrutability thesis.

Since the qualitative changes exist solely at the level of the intrinsic natures of the items concerned, given the inscrutability thesis, they are not empirically detectable. Hence, the qualitative variation or deviancy in S would not register in our physical theorizing: our best physical theories would posit a homogeneous space and a single kind of atom. Now, a natural response to this situation would be to suppose that these theories provide us with a largely accurate but incomplete account of the physical world, but this is a mistake. Not only are the changes in intrinsic nature undetectable, but they are not physical changes at all. If, as the inscrutability thesis states, physical concepts are without exception topic neutral, then the worlds described fall under precisely the same physical descriptions and so are physically indiscernible in all respects. The worlds are different but not physically different, and the relevant physical theories are not incomplete.

The mode of deviancy that Foster is most concerned with—a mode that poses more of a threat to the status of physical space—is geometrical rather than qualitative. His primary example consists of a case in which the laws governing the behavior of material things conspire to conceal their real spatial locations. Although the mechanism involved is very simple, its dramatic consequences may not be immediately apparent.

Once again, S is a three-dimensional Newtonian space. Although S itself is homogeneous, there are two spherical regions within it, R1 and R2, some distance apart, whose boundaries have unusual effects on any material objects that cross them, due to the relevant laws of nature. Any object that enters R1 emerges in R2 and continues on its way at the same speed and in the same direction; similarly, an object entering R2 emerges in R1 and continues on its way at the same speed and in the same direction. These transitions occur instantaneously, and all forms of matter and energy, including light, are affected. These deviant laws have the effect of making it appear as though R1 is located where R2 actually is and vice versa.

Let us suppose that R1 is a spherical region about ten miles in diameter in Cambridgeshire, and R2 is a similarly sized and shaped region in Oxfordshire.We go back several thousand years, to a time when these areas were just starting to be occupied by the first human settlers. A particular settler, walking through the region we now know as Oxfordshire, finds that he wants to be alone, and so decides to build a hut a few miles beyond his tribe's most outlying village, close to the boundary of R2. Since this boundary is invisible and has no observable effects, no one realizes it is there. Our settler walks five miles further on and gets to work. He builds a small hut. After a few months of solitude, he decides he wouldn't mind some company after all, and it is not long before a small village has formed, centered on his original hut. From this modest beginning, the city we now know as Oxford developed.

However, although Oxford appears to be within the Oxfordshire countryside, in reality it is situated in Cambridgeshire, in region R1. As our founding settler first crossed the boundary of R2, he was relocated inside R1, and since R1 is in Cambridgeshire, so too is the hut he built and the village that developed around it. When the villagers went in search of suitable stones for their constructions, they frequently crossed the boundary of R1 and emerged in Oxfordshire; the material they brought back—after crossing the R2 boundary—ended up inside Cambridgeshire, which is why the village is constructed from what seem to be "local" materials found in Oxfordshire, even though it is actually situated in Cambridgeshire. All subsequent movements—of animals, people, matter, light—undergo the same instantaneous translations. 

A similar story applies for a settler walking through the Cambridgeshire fenlands: on crossing the boundary of R1, this settler emerges in Oxfordshire and founds the village that develops into the city we know as Cambridge.

Thus, in this imaginary scenario, the actual locations of Oxford and Cambridge are the opposite of their apparent locations: Oxford is in Cambridgeshire, and Cambridge is in Oxfordshire. But given the immediate relocations that occur when things cross the boundaries of R1 and R2, no one realizes this.

Since light is similarly affected, anyone flying high over Oxfordshire who looks down onto R2 will see light emanating from R1 and so will actually perceive Oxford, even though Oxford lies many miles to the east.

Light from a spherical region of S in Cambridgeshire (where Oxford is situated) emerges in Oxfordshire, leaving the pilot of a plane flying over Oxfordshire with the impression that Oxford lies directly below.

It might be thought that none of this makes sense: if all physical processes are organized in exactly the same manner as they would be if Oxford were really in Oxfordshire and Cambridge in Cambridgeshire, what sense is there in the idea that this is not the case? It is here that the underlying (but invisible) space S plays a crucial role. With respect to this space, which we assume is perfectly real, Oxford really is in Cambridgeshire, and Cambridge really is in Oxfordshire. If, per impossible, we could see S, it would be obvious that Oxford is surrounded by the Cambridgeshire countryside, and Cambridge by the Oxfordshire countryside.

### 15.4 Intrinsic versus Functional Geometry

Let us say that the laws governing the behaviour of atoms in space are "nomologically uniform" if they impose the same constraints on the behaviour of atoms at all points in space. In particular, the laws apply uniformly to all the geometrical properties of atoms throughout space and time—by which we mean shape and possible spatial trajectories. The laws operative in S are not nomologically uniform: they require atoms to move continuously in regions outside R1 and R2, but discontinuously across the boundaries of these two regions. A spherical atom remains spherical as it moves through space, except when crossing a boundary of R1 or R2, at which point it ceases to be spherical and is effectively divided into two parts for a while.

Besides lacking nomological uniformity, the laws in S have a distinctive feature: if regions R1 and R2 were interchanged, nomological uniformity would be restored. In that imagined interchange, R1 (which contains Oxford) would be located in Oxfordshire and R2 (which contains Cambridge) in Cambridgeshire, removing the forced discontinuities in object paths. A car driven through Oxfordshire along a road leading to Oxford would pass through Oxford, not Cambridge; as it crosses the critical boundary, it "emerges" in R1 as before, but since R1 would now be in Oxfordshire, the car's path through S would be continuous.

Taking nomological uniformity as the norm for a world’s organization, Foster calls worlds that are nomologically non-uniform but would regain uniformity by swapping R1 and R2 "nomologically deviant." A nomologically deviant physical world is one where uniformity would be restored by some alteration in geometry. In this example, the alteration is mild—the points in two regions are switched—but Foster describes more radical cases involving dimensionality, where laws range over three spatial dimensions but underlying space is only two-dimensional.

Foster draws a distinction between functional and intrinsic geometry. The intrinsic geometry of a space is its real geometry—the "colour-determined" geometry in the case of S. The functional geometry is the geometrical structure that empirical theory ascribes to the physical world, based on observed behaviour of material bodies and assuming that the laws of nature are uniform. Because space itself is invisible, we have no direct access to its real geometry; functional geometry is the apparent geometry that beings like us would ascribe to it, based on experience.

In the Oxford-Cambridge case, functional and intrinsic geometries differ. We can call the intrinsic geometry "Gi" and the functional geometry "Gf." According to Gf, the points in R1 (underlying Oxford’s material processes) are close to and surrounded by points in Oxfordshire; according to Gi, these points are close to and surrounded by points in Cambridgeshire. The same applies to Cambridge and Oxfordshire. Gf and Gi thus assign different metrical and topological properties to points and regions associated with the same physical objects and processes. How to interpret this difference is a difficult question addressed shortly.

Foster’s distinction between intrinsic and functional geometry differs from the usual distinction between pure and applied (or physical) geometry. Both functional and intrinsic geometries are applied; the difference lies in what they are applied to. Intrinsic geometry is what we would ascribe to a space if we could observe it as it really is, from an all-revealing perspective—Foster calls this "transcendentally natural intrinsic" geometry. A space conceived as a single colour-field illustrates this concept. Functional geometry is the geometry a space would have if the laws governing its occupants were nomologically uniform. Foster contends that these two can differ, assuming a substantival space has both an intrinsic nature and spatial structure independent of occupant behaviour.

### 15.5 The Nomological Thesis

Foster argues that it is logically impossible for any physical space to be nomologically deviant, since it is a conceptual truth that physical geometry coincides with functional geometry. He calls this the "nomological thesis," positing a conceptual connection between the structure of physical space and the laws governing its occupants. These laws underpin functional geometry. The nomological thesis is crucial to the anti-realist argument, and its defence occupies several chapters of *The Case for Idealism*.

Notably, no empirical evidence could support a physical space’s nomological deviance, since empirical evidence points only towards functional geometry.By definition, the hypothesis that the geometry of physical space is its functional geometry will be simpler than, and to this extent preferable to, any alternative geometrical hypothesis (which does not preserve uniformity), provided we take nomological uniformity to be our criterion of simplicity, which, of course, we do. Consider, for example, what we would make of the hypothesis that Oxford is really in Cambridgeshire when all the observational evidence suggests that Oxford is in Oxfordshire. Although someone could construct a theory involving non-uniform laws that explains why it seems that Oxford is in Oxfordshire when really it is in Cambridgeshire, the needless complexity of this theory means we would never be inclined to take it seriously.

But Foster's claim is stronger than this. He holds that not only could we never have any scientific grounds for accepting the hypothesis that our physical space is nomologically deviant, but the hypothesis itself is incoherent. Consider again the Oxford and Cambridge case, in which the intrinsic geometry of the external colour-field (S) does not correspond with the functional geometry. What can we say here about the physical world and its geometrical structure? Three positions are open to us:

1. The physical world does not exist.
2. The physical world exists, and its geometry is the intrinsic geometry of the underlying space S.
3. The physical world exists, and its geometry is its functional geometry.

Foster suggests that option 1 is not a genuine option: "It is just obvious that the local organizational quirk with respect to R1 and R2, and the resulting effects on empirical appearance, do not suffice to eliminate the physical world altogether." Since this is clearly right, the issue lies between options 2 and 3.

In regard to option 2, Foster's claim is that it simply makes no sense to think that the physical world could have a structure that is both quite different from its apparent structure and wholly concealed from us as a consequence of natural law. However, as Foster acknowledges, it is far from obvious that this is so, and he defends his claim with a variety of arguments.

Where the deviation between functional and intrinsic geometry is as slight as in the Oxford and Cambridge case, it might be objected that surely, in this case, it does make sense to hold that the structure of physical space might be that of intrinsic geometry, even if we could never discover what this geometry is. After all, our conception of the physical leaves room for some discrepancy between how the physical world really is and how our best scientific theory represents it as being.

The force of this point can be countered by considering cases in which the functional and intrinsic geometries deviate more wildly. Foster supplies two such scenarios, described in enough detail to show that they are workable.

In the first case, the underlying geometry is two-dimensional, whereas the functional geometry of the resulting physical world—the world to which we have access—is three-dimensional. In the second case, a single physical space is sustained by events in two quite separate spatial systems. Thus, there is spatial unity at the functional level but disunity at the intrinsic level. Since in both cases we can suppose that the empirically manifest world is exactly like the physical world we are actually acquainted with, it would be very implausible to claim that the physical world in either scenario really possesses the structure of the underlying space, a structure to whose existence we are entirely oblivious. A more plausible position would be that physical space exists in such cases as a unitary three-dimensional continuum; however, given the discrepancy between the geometries of this space and the underlying reality, we cannot regard physical space as being metaphysically fundamental.

What Foster says might be correct for cases in which there are radical discrepancies between intrinsic and functional geometries. We must admit that such cases are logically possible, but what about those cases in which intrinsic and functional geometries exactly coincide, or nearly coincide, as in the Oxford and Cambridge case? Can't we say that in these sorts of cases the physical geometry is the intrinsic geometry, or at least that it makes sense to think this? Foster rejects this. What grounds could there be for adopting two different methodological principles for selecting a geometry for physical space? If we admit that the functional criterion is appropriate in cases of radical divergence, then it must be appropriate in all cases, since even when functional and intrinsic geometries coincide, our epistemological predicament is exactly the same as when the geometries are radically different. We never have any evidence that functional and intrinsic geometries are similar, and so we never have any grounds for employing any selection criterion other than the functional one.

But an objector might continue: Isn't the hypothesis that the functional geometry coincides with the intrinsic geometry the simplest hypothesis? Doesn't this hypothesis yield a simpler set of physical laws? If we take nomological simplicity to be an explanatory virtue, doesn't this hypothesis provide the best explanation for the world's being as it seems to be? If so, then the hypothesis that physical and intrinsic geometries coincide at least makes sense, even if it is not true.

The inscrutability thesis comes into play here. Foster does not deny that simplicity considerations have a role to play in the selection of an explanatory hypothesis concerning the structure and laws of the physical world. However, since physical theory is topic neutral, simplicity considerations can only enter into the empirical justification of theories that make no claims about the intrinsic nature of physical reality. The hypothesis that intrinsic and functional geometries coincide clearly isn't topic neutral, and so is not a hypothesis to which the usual canons of empirical reasoning can apply.

This point seems decisive and doesn't rest simply on an appeal to intuitions about the sorts of reality it is appropriate to call "physical." The intrinsic geometry of the external item underlying the physical world is grounded in the intrinsic nature of this item. For example, the intrinsic geometry of the colour-field S is fixed by the trajectories and distance relations between the different regions of this field. The same applies in other cases, regardless of what the intrinsic nature of the external item is—we can't, of course, imagine in any way the nature of this item if we suppose it to be non-phenomenal. Now, if we accept the inscrutability thesis, it follows that considerations concerning the intrinsic nature of the external item cannot play any role at all in the considerations that lead to the adoption of a physical geometry. The only considerations that do have a role to play are those concerning the behaviour of those aspects of the external item to which we have empirical access.

The empirical evidence may underdetermine physical spatial geometry—there may be a family of competing hypotheses, each assigning to the physical world a different combination of geometries and laws, each of which is equally compatible with all possible empirical evidence—but every member of this family will be an acceptable hypothesis only because it helps explain the observed behaviour of physical objects. The intrinsic geometry of the underlying external reality does not, and cannot, enter into the selection principles we employ in choosing a physical geometry.

If we grant this much, the case for the nomological thesis seems compelling: it is a conceptual truth that the physical geometry of a space is its functional geometry.

15.6 Nomological Contingency

One further general assumption is relevant to the main argument. Foster assumes that the physically relevant geometry of S is contingent. This is called the nomological contingency assumption. It claims that the physically relevant laws governing S and its occupants could be different from how they actually are. Given its importance to his central arguments, it is somewhat surprising that Foster never provides any argument for this assumption; he simply takes it to be self-evidently true.

However, it is quite reasonable to accept this assumption. Suppose that S is a colour-field, and its occupants are tiny mobile spheres of a different colour moving within it. It is easy to imagine these spheres conforming to many different laws of motion without the structure of S changing. The intrinsic geometry of S is no more affected by the behaviour of the atoms moving through it than the geometry of a cinema screen is affected by the play of light across its surface during a film projection. Although this lends the nomological contingency assumption considerable initial plausibility, we are dealing here only with a model. Might not the laws governing real-life equivalents of S and its occupants be necessary rather than contingent? If the "bare powers" metaphysic applied—that is, if a purely causal-dispositional account of the constituents of the physical world were viable—then it would be very plausible to maintain that physical things possess their causal powers essentially, since such things are nothing but clusters of causal powers.

But given the content thesis, S and at least some of its occupants must possess space-filling intrinsic natures of some kind, so this hypothesis can be ruled out.Consequently, the question we need to focus on is whether there could be necessary connections between intrinsic natures and their causal powers. Since the intrinsic natures of S and its occupants are entirely inscrutable, it is hard to see how the claim that a particular intrinsic nature is necessarily associated with a particular set of causal characteristics could ever be substantiated.

A non-contingent connection between an intrinsic nature and its causal characteristics cannot be analytic; since, by assumption, we have no conception of the intrinsic nature we are considering, no amount of conceptual analysis will do the job. But nor can the connection be a posteriori since, given the inscrutability thesis, no amount of empirical investigation will shed even a glimmer of light on the intrinsic nature of something external to our minds. The claim that water is necessarily H2O may be an a posteriori truth, but this truth does not reveal any connection between an intrinsic nature and a set of causal properties, simply because we have no idea of the intrinsic nature of hydrogen and oxygen atoms or their constituents.

The nomological contingency assumption is also supported by purely metaphysical considerations. We are working on the assumption that S is the metaphysically basic item. If we held that S itself could not exist if its occupants obeyed different laws, we would be saying that S depended for its existence on its occupants behaving in a specific way, a claim manifestly incompatible with the assumption that S is ontologically basic. An ontologically basic item is one that depends on nothing else for its existence.

### 15.7 Realism Rejected

We can now turn to the crucial part of Foster's case: the argument that yields the conclusion that physical space cannot be part of what is metaphysically basic or ultimately real. To simplify, we assume that the external reality corresponding to the physical world is a giant colour-field, S. We also assume that the physical world exists and that one of its metaphysically basic constituents is physical space, P. Facts about physical objects supervene upon facts about the distribution of atoms through S. There is a sense in which S can possess two geometries: its own intrinsic geometry and the functional geometry that applies to its occupants. Since the physically relevant geometry is the functional geometry, the geometry of P is the functional geometry of S. The question now arises as to the relationship between these two spatial items, P and S. Foster's claim is that S and P are, as a matter of logical necessity, numerically distinct entities.

His argument rests on widely accepted assumptions concerning the identity conditions for spaces and their constituent points. The most general level at which the geometrical properties of a space can be characterized is topological.

Spaces that are topologically indistinguishable may differ in other geometrical respects, such as whether a Euclidean metric applies. Foster makes the minimal assumption that the topology of a space is essential to it. So for a given space S with topology T, in any possible world in which S exists, it possesses T. We assume here, of course, that S is a concrete item, a particular, not merely a mathematical structure (which exists in all logically possible worlds). Foster further assumes that spatial points owe their individuality to the spaces to which they belong: whatever geometrical features are essential to a space are also essential to the points that constitute the space.

From this, it follows that there are logically possible worlds that outwardly resemble ours in all respects, but where S and P are numerically distinct. Foster provides several examples of worlds where S and P are topologically different — the Oxford-Cambridge scenario is one such example — and in these cases, not only must S and P be numerically distinct spaces, but none of the constituent points in S exist in P and vice versa. However, as Foster concedes, we have no reason to suppose that our world is like this. Suppose that in our world, there is no discrepancy; the laws are uniform, and S and P have the same topology. In this instance, is there any obstacle to holding that S and P are one and the same space? Foster maintains there is: S and P may share their actual properties, but they differ in their modal properties. If P is a genuine space, it must possess its topology essentially. S shares this topology, but does it do so essentially? No. Given the nomological contingency assumption, there are possible worlds where S has exactly the same intrinsic geometry as in the actual world, but where the laws governing its occupants are such that it has a very different functional (physically relevant) geometry. In some of these worlds, the functional topology of S differs from that of P. In such worlds, P does not exist—in its place is a distinct physical space. Hence there are possible worlds where S exists but P does not.

Assuming that identities are necessary—that if x = y, then x = y in every possible world where either exists—it follows that S and P cannot be numerically identical. The same conclusion can be reached differently. Leibniz's principle of the identity of indiscernibles may be controversial, but the converse—the indiscernibility of identicals—is not. According to this principle, if x and y are numerically identical, then they must possess the same genuine properties. If x and y are merely different names for the same object, it is hard to see how this principle could be false. Now, S has a physically relevant geometry with a certain topology, call this topology T. P has precisely the same topology. But there is a difference: S possesses T only contingently, but P does not. Although in fact both S and P have the same physically relevant topology, S could exist without this topology—if the occupants of S obeyed different laws of motion, S's physically relevant topology would be different—but P cannot do likewise. On the assumption that P is a genuine space in its own right, it possesses its topology T essentially. Let us label the modal property "could exist without T" as X. The argument that P and S are numerically distinct is:

1. S possesses property X.  
2. P does not possess property X.  
3. Therefore, by the indiscernibility of identicals, S cannot be numerically identical with P.  

Provided X is a genuine property, which it seems to be, the argument is sound.

Foster's argument resembles a well-known argument about the relationship between ordinary physical objects, such as statues and plastic dishpans, and the matter from which they are composed. The idea that two physical objects could exactly coincide in space throughout their entire existence but nonetheless be numerically distinct may seem odd, just as Foster's claim that S and P can have the same geometry but still be distinct spaces seems odd. However, a powerful argument leads to this conclusion.

Consider Lumpl and Goliath. Goliath is a statue, and Lumpl is the lump of clay from which Goliath is made. Both come into existence at the same time and cease to exist at the same time. Since Lumpl and Goliath are composed of exactly the same material constituents and share the same physical properties, it might be argued there is only one physical object. However, an obstacle arises. Even though Lumpl and Goliath coincide throughout their existence, it is possible they might not have. Shortly after completing Goliath, the artist might have squeezed Lumpl into a ball without destroying it. Lumpl would survive, but Goliath would not. This hypothetical shows that the modal property "might have been squeezed into a ball without being destroyed" is possessed by Lumpl but not by Goliath. Hence, by the indiscernibility of identicals, Lumpl and Goliath cannot be numerically identical. Anyone accepting this must also accept the validity of Foster's modal argument. To resist Foster's conclusion, one must reject his premises.If we do accept Foster's conclusion, that \( S \) and \( P \) cannot be numerically identical, the only remaining task is to settle on the ontological status of physical space. Since physical space exists only in virtue of the particular contingent laws that apply to the underlying external reality, and the latter could exist without sustaining any physical space at all, it is clear that physical space has an ontologically dependent status, contrary to our initial assumption.

Furthermore, if we take the view that physical space is the metaphysically basic item in the physical world, and that physical objects depend for their existence on physical space, then given our conclusion that physical space is necessarily numerically distinct from the space of the underlying reality (should this reality be spatial at all), the underlying reality is, necessarily, wholly non-physical. Even if we discard this assumption and take the view that material things are not ontologically dependent on physical space, we are left with the conclusion that a substantival space cannot be an ontologically basic ingredient of reality. Spatial realism in its most interesting form is thus undermined.

Although he argues that we should be anti-realists with respect to physical space, Foster is not saying that statements such as "Paris is closer to London than to New York" cannot be true. His position is analogous to that of the B-theorist, who recognizes tensed truth while maintaining that reality itself is not tensed: the truthmaker of a tensed statement such as "World War One is past" is the tenseless fact that this statement occurred at a time later than the ending of the First World War. In a similar vein, Foster would argue that statements about the spatial properties of physical objects have as truthmakers states of affairs that are entirely non-physical. Foster concedes that there are physical facts, but only in the trivial sense that there is a fact corresponding to every true statement. Since statements about the physical world are made true by non-physical facts, physical facts are not basic; rather, they supervene upon, or are logically sustained by, non-physical facts.

What holds for facts also holds for objects. Not all objects are basic: chairs exist, but — in our world at least — chairs are always composed of various particles, and any particular chair supervenes on, or is the logical creation of, facts concerning these more basic entities. For Foster, a fact is basic if it is not logically sustained by any other fact or facts, and an entity is basic if it is not the logical creation of any fact or facts. What he calls "ultimate reality" is the totality of basic entities and facts. Formulated in these terms, Foster's anti-realism amounts to the claim that ultimate reality is wholly non-physical. On the assumption that there are physical entities and facts, these must be logically dependent on something else, some reality that is non-physical. Foster suggests that this ultimate reality is mental; hence his idealism. But whatever its nature, it is this non-physical reality that is responsible for our sensory experience being as it is.

---

15.8 Geometrical Pluralism  
Any argument as complex as Foster's can be challenged in different ways.

For example, the argument presupposes the necessity of identity and "geometrical essentialism"—the view that the geometry of a space is essential to the identity of all its points. Although both doctrines are widely accepted, they could be questioned. Indeed, if there were no alternative, many might prefer to abandon one of these doctrines rather than accept Foster's anti-realist conclusion.

However, a better response is available. It emerges as soon as we question another basic assumption about the nature of space—an assumption that is by no means unique to Foster, but that his own argument shows to be questionable. Not only is Foster committed to geometrical essentialism, but he is also committed to what we can call "geometrical monism." His anti-realist argument is based on the unstated assumption that a genuine space can only have a single geometrical structure. It is taken as obvious that when P and S have different geometries, they must be numerically distinct. Even when their geometries coincide, the possibility that they could diverge ensures their distinctness. But if a genuine space is not restricted to a single geometry—if geometrical pluralism obtains—we have the option of regarding the divergent geometries of S and P as belonging to one and the same space. If a case for pluralism can be sustained, realism can be restored without rejecting the other essentialist doctrines that Foster's argument requires.

There are two viable accounts of distance relations within a space. Intrinsic distance consists of a direct spatial relation between a pair of points, while Gaussian distance is measured by the shortest continuous path through space. Although the two accounts yield the same results for classical Euclidean spaces, they can easily diverge. For example, creating a hole in a space leaves the intrinsic distances between points on either side unaltered but increases their Gaussian distances.

When presenting these accounts, it is often assumed that the points in any given space could be related by only one sort of distance relation; however, there is no obvious reason why this must be so. Consider the case of a corrugated space: an infinite two-dimensional plane that undulates in a regular way, its crests and troughs forming alternating half-cylinders. Call this space C and compare it with the flat Euclidean plane E. When a flat surface is bent without any tearing or stretching, the lengths of paths confined to the surface do not change. Thus, from the Gaussian perspective, C and E are geometrically equivalent (both have zero intrinsic curvature). However, if we suppose that the points in C are connected by intrinsic distance relations, the two spaces are not geometrically equivalent.

Corrugating a space changes the distances between points; for example, the intrinsic distances between points on successive mounds and valleys are shortened as they are moved closer together.

Assuming the intrinsic distance relations are genuine properties of the points in C, it is tempting to suppose that C is really a bent space and that its true geometry is captured by the intrinsic measure of distance. Nevertheless, the Gaussian conception of distance remains perfectly valid. The facts about the continuous path lengths between points within C are not affected by the existence or non-existence of intrinsic distance relations.

This significance becomes clear when we avoid picturing C embedded within three-dimensional space. Without such embedding, and the Gaussian paths connecting its peaks and troughs through that space, the undulations are not obvious. If we were Flatlanders living in C, no ordinary measuring devices would reveal the ripples in our space, and their existence would not be obvious at all.

One could interpret this as Foster does: the true geometry of C is its intrinsic geometry, discernible by a being with transcendent powers observing all intrinsic distance relations. The Gaussian geometry of C is its physical geometry, since the Gaussian properties are those discoverable by geometers working within the space using rods and strings. Since C is intrinsically corrugated but physically flat, physical space is numerically distinct from C and, given that C really exists, physical space itself cannot be ultimately real.

However, this interpretation is not compelling. The Gaussian geometry is a real feature of C itself. Gaussian distances are shortest continuous path lengths determined by the presence or absence of points within C. If a hole is created in C, path lengths connecting points across this region increase. Rather than introducing additional and only partially real spaces, it is better to accept that C possesses two equally valid geometrical structures simultaneously, each depending on real features of C itself—Gaussian and intrinsic distances.

The discrepancy between physical and intrinsic geometry in C is relatively mild. The sort of divergence found in Foster's Oxford and Cambridge example is more extreme geometrically and involves a form of nomological deviance absent from C. This reveals not merely the possibility of Gaussian and intrinsic distances coexisting while diverging, but rather the existence of two distinct forms of Gaussian or path-based distance, which also can coexist without coinciding.

We can distinguish two varieties of "shortest path-length," which may be called "intrinsic geodesics" and "material geodesics." The material geodesics of a space are the paths of shortest distance as measured by some specified physical process. A natural way of defining this is to say that inertial paths—those followed by bodies not acted upon by force—are geodesics. Other definitions are possible, but this inertial account suffices for present purposes.Intrinsic geodesics, on the other hand, are defined relative to the structure and composition of space itself, and thus independently of the behavior of the material contents of a space. On the assumption that space is substantival and thus a real entity in its own right, the idea that space has intrinsic features possessing a specific geometrical structure seems reasonable.

An entity shaped like the surface of a sphere is geometrically distinct from one shaped like a flat piece of paper; likewise, a flat Euclidean plane with a hole and one without a hole are geometrically different. The geometrical features a given space possesses depend on the nature of the spatial fabric, which may take different forms. For present purposes, it suffices to accept the hypothesis that a substantival space can possess geometrical features intrinsic to it, something most substantivalists accept.

We can draw a distinction between naive and circumspect substantivalism. The naive substantivalist assumes without question that material and intrinsic geodesics are the same; the idea they could differ is never considered. The circumspect substantivalist acknowledges the possibility that the two might not always coincide. Upon finding a region where bodies in inertial motion do not move in straight lines and where this cannot be explained by known forces, the naive substantivalist concludes that a non-Euclidean hole has been discovered—a region of strong local curvature in an otherwise flat space.

Circumspect substantivalists accept this as one explanation but also consider an alternative: that the intrinsic geometry of space in that region is flat, but the laws governing the world are such that it appears holed. Bodies behave as if moving along geodesics in a holed space, but they are not. In this region, material geodesics and intrinsic geodesics fail to coincide; possibly, occasional lapses in natural laws occurred when the universe was created. Unless this possibility is ruled out, the alternative explanation remains viable.

The naive substantivalist might argue that if one accepts this kind of explanation, one must accept another possibility: regions of space that are intrinsically curved, yet where material geodesics are Euclidean due to laws governing space-body interactions. Though it sounds absurd to posit structures that are by nature empirically undetectable, the circumspect substantivalist responds that this cannot be ruled out. There is no metaphysical necessity for bodies in force-free inertial motion to follow intrinsic geodesics; if they do, it is due to the particular laws in this world, while other law regimes are conceivable.

Recognizing the possibility of geodesic dislocations also means acknowledging concealed dislocations. There is no reason to assume such dislocations are common or even exist at all. It is reasonable to believe that our world is governed by simple, uniform physical laws, but if a region is discovered where material geodesics are curved for no apparent reason, it could be due to unusual laws rather than variations in space's shape.

In the absence of evidence that dislocations between intrinsic and material geodesics are impossible, substantivalists should be circumspect rather than naive. In cases where intrinsic distance and physical distance conflict, the substantivalist might embrace geometrical pluralism, holding that a space can simultaneously possess multiple geometries. This applies when material and intrinsic geodesics diverge; since material geodesics are paths through physical space privileged by physical law, they represent genuine geometrical features. If intrinsic geodesics also exist, pluralism becomes unavoidable.

One might claim intrinsic geodesics are more basic metaphysically than material ones. This is justified since some counterfactual situations show a space retaining intrinsic geodesics but having different material geodesics due to different laws. However, this does not undermine the geometrical status of material geodesics, as not all features of a physical entity are essential. Considering the physical world as a whole, material geodesics hold prominence as the paths privileged by laws connecting material objects with space, providing the main clues about space's structure. These considerations support taking both geodesic structures as essential to the identity of a space, at least in worlds where they differ.

Regarding Foster's case for anti-realism, if we grant that in worlds with significant discrepancy between the real and apparent spatial structures, physical space is an indispensable theoretical construct supervening on a non-physical reality (as in Foster's radical examples involving multiple external spaces), then since our world likely does not resemble those cases, the more interesting scenarios are those where discrepancies are small or non-existent. By rejecting geometrical monism and accepting that a substantival physical space can have multiple geometrical structures simultaneously, we can acknowledge Foster's possible scenarios without rejecting realism.

In the Oxford and Cambridge examples, extreme but localized divergences between material and intrinsic geodesics occur, yet the circumspect substantivalist regards both as real features of the same physical space. Importantly, in the actual world, where there is no reason to suppose divergence, the mere possibility they could diverge does not compel us to reject realism. The realist can claim that material geodesics are contingent geometrical features of physical space. Perhaps the realist need not concede even this: if physical spaces are individuated by both intrinsic and material geodesics, then if they in fact coincide, it makes no sense to claim they could diverge.

---

16. Zeno and the Continuum

16.1 Motion and the Continuum

Motion may seem unproblematic. Things do move: watching a car on the street or a hand waving, we see objects in motion. Even if some argue motion is not directly perceived, it is clearly ubiquitous. Theoretically, in simple models of space and time like flat Euclidean Newtonian frameworks, motion simply consists of an object changing location over time. In more complex spacetimes, additional complications arise, but many ideas remain the same.

A spacetime is generally viewed as a structured collection of points, and whether one is moving in spacetime depends on the trajectory one's worldline takes—the points it passes through. In spacetimes where motion is relative, there is no absolute fact about who is moving; however, motion itself is discernible: two objects steadily moving toward one another over an interval means the spatial distance between them is steadily decreasing during that time; moving apart means the distance increases; and at rest relative to one another means the distance remains constant.

For present purposes, we can set aside the complications introduced by more complex spacetimes.The motion-related problems that are the focus of this chapter and the next do extend to the spacetimes of less familiar varieties, but they also apply vigorously to the more familiar forms—such as Euclidean and Newtonian—and can be more easily explored in the context of those latter types. We can also temporarily set aside the debate between substantivalists and relationists, as the problems in question apply to both conceptions of space, time, and spacetime. (However, we shall see in Section 17.5 that these differing conceptions are not entirely without relevance.)

As for the problems themselves, they derive from the eponymous "paradoxes" of Zeno of Elea. We know comparatively little about Zeno himself. If what Plato states in his *Parmenides* is accurate—that Zeno was forty when Socrates was a young man—then Zeno was born around 490 BCE. We also know that, in addition to being the author of one or more books of paradoxes, Zeno was close to Parmenides.

Despite appearances to the contrary, Parmenides believed that reality consisted of just one changeless entity. Zeno's paradoxes were intended to bolster this static monistic metaphysic by demonstrating that alternative conceptions—those that posit a plurality of moving and changing objects—lead to absurdity. Unfortunately, none of Zeno's own writings have survived, and what little we know of his doctrines comes from Aristotle's brief discussions and some passages in Simplicius, who mentions several Zenonian paradoxes in his fifth-century CE commentaries on Aristotle. Of some forty paradoxes directed against pluralism, only a small handful have survived, in addition to the four "paradoxes of motion" discussed by Aristotle.

Some of the latter are very well-known indeed and have been discussed in many a school playground. The "Achilles" paradox may well be the most widely recognized. In this ill-matched race, the fleet-footed warrior Achilles sets off in pursuit of a slow-moving tortoise, who, we are told, has been given a head start of a few meters. Given his speed, it should not take long for Achilles to overtake the tortoise, or so one might think. Zeno reveals a difficulty with this natural assumption.

Suppose Achilles has yet to overtake the tortoise, and the tortoise has reached point p1. It does not take long for Achilles to cover this distance and reach p1. However, the tortoise, moving continuously at a steady, albeit slow speed, has advanced to point p2. Once again, Achilles reaches p2, but the tortoise has moved on to p3. This process continues indefinitely, with Achilles always reaching the previous point only to find the tortoise has moved forward, suggesting Achilles will never catch the tortoise.

Hence, it seems Achilles will always lag behind in this manner, and so never succeed in catching up with the tortoise. This seems absurd: don't we know that faster-moving things can catch up with and overtake slower-moving things? Well, yes, but where has Zeno's reasoning gone astray?

Writing of Achilles, the American philosopher C. S. Peirce noted, "this ridiculous little catch presents no difficulty at all to a mind adequately trained in mathematics and in logic, but is one of those which is very apt to excite minds of a certain class to an obstinate determination to believe a given proposition" (1935: vol. IV 122). Bertrand Russell was more generous, claiming that Zeno's arguments are not "mere foolish quibbles: they are serious arguments, raising difficulties which it has taken two thousand years to answer, and which even now are fatal to the teachings of most philosophers" ([1929] 2001: 47). This divergence of opinion is frequently found in the literature. Some believe that one (or more) of the paradoxes involves trivial mistakes; others, like Russell, think they raise deep issues, which require the resources of modern mathematics to resolve.

Alfred North Whitehead wrote, "To be refuted in every century after you have written is the acme of triumph ... No one ever touched Zeno without refuting him, and every century thinks it worthwhile to refute him" (1947: 114). Sorenson responds, "I think this compliment will not be paid by future centuries ... all of Zeno's paradoxes were solved by Cantor a hundred years ago. Cantor's triumph shows that some important paradoxes can be solved. We now have answers to Zeno's riddles that satisfy the exacting standard set by modern mathematics" (2003: 56-7).

### Zeno and the Continuum I

The idea that Georg Cantor's work on infinity in the final decades of the nineteenth century—along with the work of Augustin-Louis Cauchy, Richard Dedekind, and others on the continuum—allows a satisfying and definitive solution to Zeno's more serious and significant paradoxes is widely held, and not without good reason. However, as we shall see, these more recent developments are not entirely puzzle- or problem-free.

### Numbering the Continuum

We shall look in more detail at some pertinent features of Cantor's conception of the infinite in due course (see 16.5), but before proceeding further, it will be useful to take a step back. In arguing against the possibility of motion, one of Zeno's main targets is the doctrine that space and time are continuous. This conception of the small-scale structure of space and time is the standard one today; it is built into the mathematical framework within which our leading physical theories are formulated. Hence, there remains continued interest in, and relevance of, Zeno's arguments.

The other main view concerning the microstructure of the continuum is that space and time are discrete, composed of finite atomic units or quanta. It is generally thought that Zeno also attacked this conception, and we shall look at what he had to say in due course. But let us start by taking a closer look at the mainstream view.

What exactly does it mean to say that space, time, or any other continuum is continuous? If someone says, "The engine has been running continuously for three hours now," they mean it has been running without any interruptions or gaps. So much is common sense and ordinary language. In basic Euclidean geometry, a simple example of a continuous entity is a straight line. Such a line is obviously gap-free; otherwise, it would consist of two lines rather than one. However, it also has the property of being infinitely or indefinitely divisible. Although the latter feature is not obviously part of our common-sense understanding of "continuous," it is not entirely excluded either. If you think of a continuous expanse of color—for example, what you see when looking up at a clear blue sky—it does not exhibit an obvious granular structure, and there is no evident limit to how finely it could be divided. Many of the changes or movements that we experience—such as a car we observe disappearing down the road—seem perfectly smooth and continuous.

In any event, that a geometrical line is infinitely divisible has long been assumed. Such a line is usually regarded as being composed of an ordered collection of points. Euclid defined a point as "that which lacks parts," which mathematicians subsequently took to mean that points are of zero size. Euclid also defined a line as "that which has no breadth." If a line is composed of extensionless points, there is no limit to how far it can be divided, and no limit on the number of points it contains. In more technical terms, a geometrical line has the property of being dense, meaning that between any two points, there is always another point.

Denseness, by itself, might seem to ensure that a line is free from gaps and hence continuous. How could there be gaps in a continuum that is dense? That something more than density might be required becomes evident when considering arithmetic number lines.

Consider first the positive and negative integers: … -3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3, … This number line is infinite in both directions; however large a positive integer you might encounter, it always has a successor, namely one greater; the same applies in the negative direction. Although there is an infinite number of integers, they are not fully continuous from a numerical perspective. The integer line undeniably contains all the integers there are, but it does not contain all the numbers there are. The fractions 1/2, 3/4, 33/4, and -5/7 are all perfectly respectable numbers but are not found on the line of integers.

Next, consider the rational numbers, which include all fractions, both positive and negative, and also include all integers (since integers can be expressed as fractions, such as 1 = 1/1, 2 = 4/2). Like the integers, the rationals are infinite in number. However, the rationals also possess a property that the integers lack: denseness. Between any two rational fractions, no matter how close, there is always another rational number between them. Does this denseness mean the rationals are fully continuous, without gaps? It does not.

As the Pythagoreans famously discovered, there are irrational numbers—numbers that cannot be expressed as rational fractions. One example is the length of the diagonal of a square with sides one unit long; by Pythagoras' theorem, this length equals the square root of 2, which cannot be expressed exactly as a fraction. Irrational numbers can be represented by infinite, non-repeating decimals—for example, 1.4142135... (the square root of 2), 3.14159... (π), and so forth.Adding the irrationals to the rationals gives us the so-called real number line, symbolized as R, and often simply called "the real line."

The real line is both dense and gap-free and can be regarded as being fully continuous from a numerical perspective. Not surprisingly, given this, it is often simply called the "continuum." The structure of the real-line continuum, along with precise and adequate definitions of "real number," were only fully worked out by Dedekind, Cantor, and others in the nineteenth century. Since the real line is fully continuous, it is generally taken as our reliable and informative guide to the structure of the geometrical line. More specifically, it is assumed that the points on any interval of the geometrical line can be put into a one-to-one order-preserving correspondence with a portion of the real line. More importantly for our purposes, the real line is also commonly taken to be a guide to the structure of the physical continua of space and time. This is one of the reasons why the term "continuum" often does double duty, being used to refer both to mathematical and physical spaces. Of course, whereas time is one-dimensional, space has three dimensions, and spacetime has four. However, R can easily be extended to three, four, or more dimensions. In a two-dimensional "real space," the location of each point requires two real numbers; in the three-dimensional case, we need three real numbers for each point; in the case of four dimensions, four numbers; and so forth.

If our own physical space can be coordinated in this way with triplets of real numbers, then our space must have the same small-scale structure as the real numbers and thus be dense and continuous. Since it is generally assumed that this is the case, we shall call this the "standard" or "orthodox" view.

There is a further point to note. Since the days of Newton and Leibniz and their independent invention of calculus, the principal equations of physics and cosmology have been "real-valued functions": functions that take as their inputs real numbers and deliver real numbers as their outputs. The distinctive and novel feature of calculus is its ability to determine how much some quantities are varying with respect to others at a precise point in space and time.

Hence, as Roger Penrose observes, the orthodox view of the small-scale structure of space and time has played a key role in our most successful physical theories, such as those of Archimedes, Newton, Maxwell, Einstein, and Schrödinger. All successful dynamical theories have required notions of calculus for their formulation. Now, the conventional approach to calculus requires the infinitesimal nature of the reals to be what it is. That is to say, on the small end of the scale, it is the entire range of real numbers that is, in principle, being made use of. The ideas of calculus underlie other physical notions, such as velocity, momentum, and energy. Consequently, the real-number system enters our successful physical theories in a fundamental way for our descriptions of these quantities as well. Hence the importance and contemporary relevance of Zeno's arguments.

According to the orthodox view, space and time have the same fine-grained structure as the real numbers and are therefore infinitely divisible. According to Zeno, anyone who holds that space and time are infinitely divisible cannot avoid disastrous paradoxes. Let us move on and take a closer look at these.

16.3 The "Dichotomy"

A runner is on the final lap of a race, heading for the finish line. Can this runner ever cross the line? In the paradox known as the "Dichotomy," Zeno suggests not. Suppose the runner is at point Z and the finishing line is at Z*. To reach the finishing line, the runner must reach the point halfway between Z and Z*. Next, he must reach the point halfway between that point and Z*, and so on, endlessly. Since the sequence of these intervening points has no end, the runner has to complete an infinite sequence of runs, and thus will never reach the finishing line. Since the same considerations apply to any moving object, no matter how short its journey, it is impossible for any object to reach any destination; motion is impossible.

Aristotle succinctly encapsulated Zeno's reasoning thus: "that which is in locomotion must first arrive at the halfway stage before it arrives at its goal." The rationale for the paradox's name is made clear: it involves the progressive division of distances.

There is an obvious kinship between the Dichotomy and the Achilles paradox — both involve an object moving successively through an infinite sequence of intervals — but commentators tend to agree that the Dichotomy is the more fundamental. If we know the speeds at which Achilles and the tortoise are moving, and when and where they started, it is possible—given basic physics—to work out precisely where Achilles will catch up with the tortoise. If it is possible for Achilles to reach this point, then Zeno is undone: Achilles can catch and overtake the tortoise. However, if the reasoning behind the Dichotomy is sound, it is impossible for Achilles ever to reach that point, and thus the conclusion of the Achilles paradox holds.

As for what we should make of the Dichotomy itself, the answer depends on what precisely we take the problem to be. In moving from Z to Z*, our runner has to complete the infinite sequence of runs in order to pass through the infinite series of points, and this is deemed impossible. But why exactly is this impossible? Aristotle took Zeno to be claiming that it is impossible to travel through an infinite number of intervals in a finite time. If this is right, Zeno may have had an argument along these lines:

1. In moving from Z to Z*, the runner passes through an infinite series of finite spatial intervals, such as half a meter, a quarter of a meter, and so on.  
2. To move through an infinite number of finite spatial distances requires an infinite amount of time.  
3. Hence, the runner will need an infinite amount of time to get from Z to Z*, and so will never arrive.

The same key assumption — that an infinite number of finite quantities adds up to an infinite quantity — leads to a similar absurdity if we focus solely on space:

1. The runner traverses an infinite series of finite intervals.  
2. An infinite number of finite lengths added together yields an infinite length.  
3. Hence, the distance between Z and Z* is infinite.

But, of course, the distance between Z and Z* is not infinite. Both arguments generalize to all movements through space, and if sound would establish that motion as we normally conceive it cannot occur.

Regarding the first argument, Aristotle objected that Zeno overlooked the fact that both space and time are infinitely divisible. He wrote that length and time are called "infinite" either in respect of divisibility or in respect of their extremities. While it is impossible to come into contact with infinite things in a finite time in one sense, it is possible when considering infinite divisibility, since time itself is infinite in this respect. Therefore, the time occupied by passing over an infinite number of divisibly small intervals is not infinite, and contact with the infinites is made by an infinite number of moments.

Usually construed, Aristotle’s point is that the infinite divisibility of time allows the runner — say, Zeno himself — traveling at a constant speed of one meter per second, to complete his first half-meter in half a second, the next quarter in a quarter of a second, the next eighth in an eighth of a second, and so on.
