Since the runner takes progressively less time to complete each segment, it is possible to complete the entire trip in a finite time—in this case, precisely one second.

In assessing Aristotle's reasoning, it is useful to distinguish these claims:

(A) The sum of an infinite number of finite lengths of constant size is always infinite.  
(B) The sum of an infinite number of finite lengths of ever-increasing size is always infinite.  
(C) The sum of an infinite number of finite lengths of ever-decreasing size can be finite.

Claim A appears true. For example, the sum of one plus one plus one and so on infinitely is infinite. Similarly, the sum of one-half plus one-half plus one-half and so on infinitely is also infinite.As a result, the partial sums never become indefinitely large. In this case, it can be demonstrated that there is no partial sum in the series that is larger than 1, even though, as the sequence progresses, the partial sums get ever closer to 1. A sequence of partial sums with this property is said to be convergent.

Needless to say, not all sequences have this property:
- S₂ = [1], [1 + 1], [(1 + 1) + 1], ...
- S₃ = [1], [1 + 2], [(1 + 2) + 3], ...

In cases such as these, the partial sums get bigger and bigger, without end. Such sequences are said to be divergent.

Focusing on the relevant properties of convergent series, if an unending or infinite sequence of partial sums approaches arbitrarily close to a particular number L as the sequence proceeds, but never exceeds this number, then L is said to be the limit of that sequence. Our Dichotomy sequence, S₁, can be rigorously demonstrated to have 1 as its limit, whereas S₂ and S₃ do not have finite limits at all.

The next key step is to hold that the sum of an infinite series of numbers s₁, s₂, s₃, ... is equal to the limit of the sequence of partial sums—[s₁], [s₁ + s₂], [(s₁ + s₂) + s₃], [(s₁ + s₂ + s₃) + s₄], ...—if such a limit exists. This provides us with what we need in the case of the Dichotomy. We can now safely say that the quantities that form the Z-series one-half plus one-quarter plus one-eighth and so on sum to a finite number, 1, rather than an infinite number.

And of course, the same applies in the case of many other infinite series that can be proved to converge on finite limits. It is important to note that in allowing ourselves to talk in terms of sums for infinite sequences and series, we are moving beyond our ordinary notion of adding up a series of numbers, for the latter is limited to finite series of numbers. As Huggett notes, it would be a mistake to suppose mathematicians have demonstrated that if one were to sit down for an infinite period of time adding one-half to one-quarter to one-eighth, and so on, one would discover that "1" is the answer reached after adding the final number in the series.

There is no last member of the series; no matter how far one advances, there is always another number to come. The arithmetic of finite and infinite sums is thus quite different. In defining the sum of an infinite series in terms of its limit, mathematicians are really introducing a new stipulation as to how the term "sum" can be used in a new context: that of infinite rather than finite series of numbers. Nonetheless, it would also be a mistake to think that we have made no advance on Aristotle. Whereas he simply claimed that an infinite series of quantities could have a finite sum, we now have a detailed mathematical theory that shows us systematically and consistently how it is possible for infinite sums to have finite totals.

Is that all that needs to be said about the Dichotomy—and by extension, the Achilles? By no means. We have dealt with one of the conundrums to which Zeno's puzzle gives rise, but arguably it is neither the deepest nor the most baffling. We now know that it is possible for an infinite series of quantities—whether spatial or temporal—to have a finite sum. There is thus no difficulty in supposing that Zeno takes no more than a single second to traverse all the infinity of Z-locations and arrive at his destination.

The difficulty that does remain is understanding how he can arrive at that destination at all: to arrive there, he must pass through each of the Z-points, but there is an infinite number of these points. Since there is no last member of the Z-series—no final Z-run—it seems that Zeno can never reach his destination after all. Yet he does.

This is puzzling indeed. But before addressing this difficulty, it will be useful to take a look at another of Zeno's paradoxes.

16.4 The Paradox of Plurality

Although only fragments of Zeno's forty paradoxes directed against plurality have survived, a line of argument that can be extracted from some passages in Simplicius is well worth considering. Indeed, some view the "Plurality" as the deepest of all the Zenonian paradoxes. It also goes by the name of the "Metrical Paradox," for reasons that will become clear.

Zeno takes as his target an extended thing. If such a thing can exist, he argues, it must be divisible into parts. What is more, this division into parts can be repeated indefinitely; for example, an object that is one meter long has two parts of one-half meter, three parts of one-third meter, four parts of one-quarter meter, and so on, to infinity. It may not be physically possible to carry out this division, but this does not matter. For suppose it turns out that ordinary material objects are composed of elementary particles; if these particles are spatially extended to the slightest degree, they still possess parts—for example, their upper and lower halves—even if these parts cannot be separated.

We are thus led to the conclusion that any extended object is composed of an infinite number of parts. So far as the size of these parts is concerned, there are only two options: either they have zero size or they have some small but finite non-zero size. If the latter, then our object will be infinitely large, since an infinite number of non-zero quantities seems itself to be infinite in size. If, on the other hand, the parts are of zero size, our object will itself have zero size, since zero plus zero plus zero and so on equals zero. It seems that our ordinary notion of an extended object collapses into absurdity.

Although this argument is not directed at motion per se, it easily extends to that within which motion supposedly takes place: the continua of space and time. Let us focus on the spatial case:

1. Any segment of the continuum is composed of an infinite number of homogeneous point-like parts.
2. The point-parts of this segment are either of zero length or of some non-zero length.
3. If they are of non-zero length, then the total length of the segment will be infinite.
4. If they are of zero length, then the total length of the segment will be zero.

As already noted, on the standard contemporary conception, space and time do consist of infinitely divisible continua. Given this, Zeno's argument is no strawman. Nor is this argument quickly and easily refuted. A natural first thought might be, "Ah, but as we saw in the context of the Dichotomy, it is possible for an infinite sum of finite quantities to have a finite sum; therefore, Zeno's premise that the total length must be infinite is undermined!" But this is to overlook the distinction—between infinite series whose terms are of constant size or increasing size, and those whose terms are progressively decreasing. Whereas the latter can indeed converge on a finite limit and thus have a finite sum, in the case we are currently considering, the terms of the series are of a constant finite size—thanks to the homogeneity of the continuum. Accordingly, they will indeed sum to infinity, just as Zeno claims.

So we are left with the problem of explaining how an infinite number of nothings can add up to more than nothing.As with the Dichotomy, proponents of the standard conception of the continuum claim that modern mathematics has the resources to meet Zeno's challenge. What follows is an attempt to present at least some of this response to Zeno in as non-technical a manner as possible. In the next section, some of the relevant mathematics will be expounded in an elementary way; in 16.6, we will see how this can be applied to the Plurality paradox.

16.5 Cantor's Continuum

As standardly conceived, a spatial or temporal continuum is an ordered collection of points that can be put into one-to-one correspondence with the real number system. The real numbers themselves are infinitely many, and a spatial (or temporal, or spatiotemporal) continuum is taken to consist of an actually existing infinity of points. Until comparatively recently, many philosophers—but also scientists and mathematicians—were suspicious of the idea that an actual infinity of anything could exist.

In part, this was due to Aristotle's well-known and influential rejection of the actual infinite in favor of the potential infinite. Continuous entities, Aristotle believed, could be divided into ever finer sections—whether in actuality, or merely in thought—but these smaller parts only come into being when their wholes are divided. On this long-influential view, wholes are not composed of pre-existing point-like parts.

The Greek word closest in meaning to "infinity" is "apeiron," which means "without bounds," but also "out of control"; in general, its connotations are closer to the English word "chaos." Zeno's paradoxes are but one illustration of the difficulties that dealing with infinity can bring.

Consider the seemingly innocuous series 1, -1, 1, -1, 1, -1, and so on. Given that each term cancels out its predecessor to leave 0, there may seem no doubt whatsoever that this series sums to zero. Grouping the terms as (1 - 1) + (1 - 1) + (1 - 1) + ... clearly shows the sum as 0 + 0 + 0 + ..., which is zero. However, rearranging the series as 1 + (-1 + 1) + (-1 + 1) + ... yields 1 + 0 + 0 + ..., which suggests the sum is 1. This contradictory result illustrates the complexities that arise when dealing with infinite series and infinite processes.

A different, and ultimately more substantive problem, was discussed by Galileo in the 1600s. In his *Dialogues and Mathematical Demonstrations Concerning Two New Sciences Pertaining to Mechanics and Local Motions*, he observed that each of the natural numbers can be paired off in a one-to-one fashion with its square:

- Natural numbers: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, ..., 91, 92, ...
- Their squares: 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, ..., 8281, 8464, ...

In a quite general fashion, if two collections of objects can be paired off in a one-to-one way without remainder, we say that they are equinumerous—that is, they have exactly the same number of members. These two infinite series can be matched in this way, suggesting they have the same number of members.

But while this seems unproblematic in one respect—both sets are infinite—it is puzzling in another. The set of squares seems like a proper subset of the natural numbers, omitting many numbers that are in the natural number set. So how can both have the same size? How can a proper part have the same number of members as the whole? This challenges the intuitive idea that "the whole is always greater than the part," as stated in Euclid's fifth axiom.

Galileo’s resolution was to suggest that the concepts of greater than, less than, and equal to apply only to finite quantities, not infinite ones; in other words, that infinite numbers follow different arithmetic rules. This idea was left undeveloped but was later formalized by Cantor in the nineteenth century.

Cantor took Galileo’s insight as foundational. He held that two sets have the same cardinality if and only if there is a one-to-one correspondence between their members. He defined infinite sets as those for which such a correspondence exists between the set and one of its proper subsets. Finite sets are those for which no such correspondence with a proper subset exists. This concept—though initially paradoxical—became the defining feature of infinite sets.

Using this approach, Cantor showed a remarkable result: not all infinities are equal in size. For instance, consider the set of natural numbers and the set of rational numbers (numbers expressed as ratios of integers). Both are infinite, but through Cantor’s methods, it can be shown that the rationals are countable—that is, they can be put into one-to-one correspondence with the natural numbers.

However, the set of real numbers—which includes all decimals, both rational and irrational—is uncountable. Cantor’s diagonal argument proves there can be no one-to-one correspondence between the natural numbers and the real numbers. Thus, the real numbers form a larger infinity than the countable sets.

Cantor also established that infinite cardinalities proceed through an unending hierarchy of sizes, denoted by symbols such as \( \aleph_0 \), \( \aleph_1 \), and so forth.

Of particular importance is the cardinality of the continuum, denoted \( c \), which corresponds to the size of the set of real numbers. Cantor demonstrated that even the interval from 0 to 1 contains as many real numbers as the entire real line, illustrating the profound nature of infinite sets.

This understanding of different sizes of infinity revolutionizes how we interpret continuity, the infinite, and the foundations of modern mathematics.There is nothing in the least special about the interval between 0 and 1: the same reasoning applies to all finite intervals on the real line. Every interval, no matter how small, contains the same number of points—c—as every other line, no matter how long—and indeed, the entire real line itself. The idea that there are as many points in an interval that is only a billion billionth of a nanometre across as in an interval that is a trillion trillion metres long can seem very odd indeed. But Cantor and his followers point out that finite and infinite collections have very different properties, and if the properties of infinite collections strike us as bizarre, it is only because our intuitions have evolved in response to finite quantities and collections.

In his "dimension proof," Cantor took matters a stage further: he established that there are enough points in a one-dimensional real line to cover, without any gaps, the entirety of a two-dimensional continuum. The method involved establishing a one-to-one correspondence between all the points in an interval of the real line and the points on a two-dimensional plane. This method can be extended to prove that the sets of points that compose objects of three or more dimensions also have the same cardinality as the real line, c.

---

16.6 Plurality, Measure, and Metric

For our purposes—getting to grips with Zeno's paradox of plurality—these last results are of prime significance. Zeno's challenge is as follows: given that an interval of a continuum is composed entirely of points that are uniformly of zero size, how can finite parts of this interval have lengths other than zero? Underlying this challenge is an assumption: that the length of an interval on a line is generated by adding up the sizes of its smallest constituents.

In the case of ordinary objects and their finitely sized parts, this assumption is correct. We can measure the length of a racetrack by counting up the number of metre-length parts it possesses. Similarly, if we know that a pile is ten bricks high, and each brick has a height of 10 centimetres, it follows that the pile is 100 centimetres tall. In cases such as these—the ordinary cases we encounter in everyday life—the lengths of wholes are determined by the magnitudes of their smaller parts.

Even in cases of infinite series with denumerably many members, the sum of the series is a function of the sizes of the parts: the series sums to 1 precisely because of the sizes of its parts. Hence, Zeno's claim that if spatial intervals are composed of dimensionless points, no spatial interval could ever have a size larger than zero.

---

This line of argument certainly poses a serious threat to the standard view of the continuum. Of all Zeno's known arguments, the Plurality may well have the greatest intuitive power. This does not mean, however, that it is correct or unanswerable. Zeno's conclusion would be unavoidable if the points that compose the Cantorian continuum behaved like ordinary finite quantities or denumerably infinite quantities. But this is not the case: there are the same number of points in a millimetre of the continuum as there are in a segment that is a trillion miles long. More precisely, both sets have cardinality c. Given this, the length of a line is not a function of the number of points it contains. How could it be if spatial intervals of very different lengths contain the same number of points?

Our ordinary concept of addition—the one that allows us to calculate the height of a wall by adding up the heights of the bricks—does not apply to non-denumerable collections of points. Since the spatial continuum contains a non-denumerable infinity of points, it is a mistake to think our ordinary concept of addition applies to these points. But Zeno's plurality paradox depends on the assumption that it does: he invites us to add up all the points in a line as if adding finite numbers.

However, if addition is inapplicable to non-denumerable collections of points, we must decline this invitation. Adolf Grünbaum summarizes the situation:

"[Zeno] could not anticipate that the addition of a super-denumerable infinity of numbers, be they zero or positive, presents a problem altogether different from adding a finite sequence of numbers or a denumerable infinity of numbers. Although arithmetic has evolved a definition of the 'sum' of a denumerable infinity of numbers based on limits, this definition is utterly useless and irrelevant when adding a super-denumerable infinity of numbers."

Thus, while length is denumerably additive in standard mathematics, the addition of the lengths of all the points composing a unit segment is meaningless and undefined, and cannot be used to compute the total length of the segment. In particular, the deduction of Zeno's paradoxical result—zero for the length of a unit segment—is precluded by this arithmetical fact.

When dealing with the continuum, to avoid paradox or inconsistency, addition must be restricted. The standard mathematical solution is that an interval can be regarded as the sum of its non-overlapping sub-intervals only under certain conditions: for example, the sub-intervals are all of finite length and finite in number, or denumerably infinite in number and converging to a limit. These rules do not allow us to generate lengths by summing individual zero-sized points.

Grünbaum also points out that mathematics has a more general way of measuring the sizes of sets containing non-denumerably many members: measure theory, developed by Henri Lebesgue and others. Suppose an interval on the real line lies between two endpoints. Does it include these endpoints? From the point of view of measure theory, it does not matter: adding a single point or two points to an interval makes no difference to its measure. Intuitively, adding a dimensionless point to an interval cannot change its length. Ordinary length is additive; measures are as well. If an interval divides into two non-overlapping sub-intervals, the measure of the whole equals the sum of the measures of the parts. This applies to countably infinite sets of intervals too, so measure is said to be "denumerably additive."

Since adding a single point does not increase measure, it follows that the measure of a single point is zero. Measure theory states this precisely: the "degenerate interval" where both endpoints coincide has measure zero. Because measure is denumerably additive, a countably infinite set of zero-sized points also has measure zero. An interval must contain a non-denumerably infinite number of points to have a positive, non-zero measure. Measure theory delivers the result that the real-line interval from 0 to 1 has measure 1, and more generally, that the measure of an interval corresponds to the distance between its endpoints.

Viewing matters from the measure-theoretic perspective, Zeno was both correct and incorrect. An infinite number of dimensionless points added together always yield zero measure if the infinity is denumerable. But a non-denumerably infinite collection of points adds up to something: the points can constitute an interval with non-zero measure. Metaphorically, only collections of points with cardinality c have extension.

If this seems peculiar, so too is the Cantorian continuum. Recall how every interval on the real line, no matter how small, has the same size (cardinality) as every other interval, no matter how large. Given this, it is not surprising that it takes c-many points to make any difference to size or length on the continuum.

Thanks to developments in mathematics, the proponent of the standard conception of the continuum has an answer to Zeno.Accepting that a line L is an aggregate of points that are each of zero length does not oblige one to accept that L itself is of zero length. Relying as it does on considerations deriving from analysis and set theory, someone might query whether Grünbaum's approach applies to non-mathematical entities, as we shall see in 17.5, but we shall not pursue this now. Accepting the mathematics-based solution—even if only tentatively—gives rise to a new question: if length is not an intrinsic feature of collections of points, what does determine the length of an interval?

According to the approach standardly adopted in contemporary geometry and spacetime theories, a collection of points has to be associated with a metric or distance function before it acquires metrical properties. The basic idea is that we assign coordinates to our collection of points in the form of clusters of real numbers; these numbers are chosen to accurately reflect the geometrical properties of the space. In the case of a simple space such as the Euclidean plane, coordinates for points take the form of pairs of real numbers, ensuring a Euclidean structure where the square of the hypotenuse equals the sum of the squares of the other two sides, in accord with Pythagoras's theorem. For more complex, curved spaces, real number coordinates are assigned to families of non-overlapping curves, and distances are determined by a metric tensor following work by Gauss, Riemann, and others.

This metricization procedure itself raises new questions. Those inclined to a strongly realist view hold that a metric reflects real and intrinsic features of spacetime itself. However, some argue there is an ineradicable conventional element to metrical spacetime features. Grünbaum has argued that such features are not fully objective, at least for spaces, times, or spacetimes structured as a Cantorian continuum. Such continua are "metrically amorphous," consisting of non-denumerably many points lacking intrinsic metrical features. Others dispute this, maintaining that metrical features can be as fundamental and intrinsic as topological properties in real physical spacetimes.

16.7 The dichotomy revisited  
In 16.3, we left Zeno in a precarious position regarding his attempt to reach the finishing line of his race. To complete the race, he must pass through all the infinitely many points between his start position and the finish line. One argument for the impossibility of this states that Zeno cannot reach the finishing line because he must travel an infinite sequence of progressively shorter distances—half a meter, then a quarter, then an eighth, and so on—each requiring finite time, leading to an infinite total time. However, as established earlier, an infinite series of quantities can sum to a finite value if those quantities converge. Thus, it is possible for Zeno to traverse all the infinite points and reach the finish line in finite time.

A further difficulty remains: understanding how Zeno can arrive at the finish line at all. To do so, he must pass through an infinite sequence of points with no final point in the series. The continuum's denseness implies that between any two points, no matter how close, there is always another point, so there is always a point between Zeno's current location and the finish line. This raises the question: how can Zeno ever arrive at his destination if there is always another point to cross?

Some clarifications help here. For Zeno to reach the finish line, he must complete an infinite series of tasks, traversing infinite finite intervals. Completing an infinite series often seems impossible because such a series has no last task. However, if we interpret completion as doing all tasks in their natural order, rather than finishing a last task, completing the series is coherent and possible.

Regarding how Zeno manages to reach the finish line, as philosopher Lowe explains, each of the infinite subtasks involves reaching a point distinct from the finish line. Although none of these individual subtasks ends with arriving at the finish line itself, completing all of them collectively results in arrival at the finish line. There is no further task after completing all subtasks; the whole journey is the sum of its parts.

While this clarifies much, a puzzle remains: if no individual subtask brings Zeno to the finish line, how does he arrive there at all? This puzzle may be inherent to motion through a continuous medium, which often defies ordinary intuition.

Examining the nature of intervals in the continuum sheds further light. In set theory and topology, one distinguishes between open intervals, which do not include their endpoints, and closed intervals, which do. For example, an open interval (a, b) includes all points between a and b but excludes a and b themselves, while a closed interval [a, b] includes both endpoints. Due to the continuum's denseness, there is no last point in an open interval; one can always find another point closer to, but distinct from, an endpoint.

Intervals can also be half-open or half-closed, including one endpoint but not the other. Zeno's journey is often described by a half-open sequence of points excluding the finish line itself, which makes it impossible for him to reach the finish line within that sequence. A more accurate representation includes the finish line as part of the sequence, forming a closed interval.

This adjustment helps explain how Zeno can reach the finish line despite the absence of a last point in the infinite series.A further set- (or measure-) theoretic property of open and closed intervals is of some assistance here, as registered briefly at the end of 16.6.

Although the closed interval [a, b] contains one point more than the corresponding half-open interval [a, b)—since the latter does not include point b, whereas the former does—when it comes to the lengths of these intervals, there is no difference at all. Not only is the length of [a, b] exactly the same as that of the half-open intervals [a, b) and (a, b], but it is also exactly the same as the length (or measure) of the wholly open (a, b). Hence, to reach point Z*, Zeno does not have to travel a greater distance than he has already traveled in completing the Z-series. In this metrical sense at least, completing the Z-series is sufficient to complete the race.

Appendix

For interested readers who have not encountered this material previously, here are a couple of brief informal illustrations of some of Cantor's easier proofs, drawn from Wallace (2005: 247-253).

The first proof concerns the relationship between the rationals (or fractions) and the positive integers. On the face of it, there are strong grounds for supposing that the rationals will vastly outnumber the integers. Not only are there many fractions between any two integers (e.g., the Dichotomy series lying between 0 and 1, or 1 and 2), but also, by virtue of the fact that the rationals are dense, there is an infinite number of rationals between any two rationals! However, by showing that the rationals and integers can be paired off in a one-to-one fashion and without remainders, Cantor established that the set of the integers and the set of the rationals have the same size, or cardinality.

All fractions can be expressed in the form of a ratio p/q, where p and q are positive integers. By arranging all such fractions in a grid that lists all values of p horizontally and all values of q vertically, we effectively list all rational numbers.

The next step is to show that the rationals can be paired off with the integers in the required way. It is possible to draw a path through this grid that visits every fraction exactly once, which shows a way to enumerate all rationals by matching each to a unique integer. In this manner, the rationals are shown to be countable and to have the same cardinality as the integers.

Zeno and the Continuum I

If we take the numbers picked out by this line and set them out in a long row, preserving their order, we get the following: 

1, 2, ½, 1/3, 2/2, 3, 4, 3/2, 2/3, ¼, 1/5, 2/4, 3/3, 4/2, 5, 6, 5/2, 4/3, ¾, 2/5, 1/6, 1/7, 2/6, 3/5, ..., p/q, ...

This series contains some redundancy since, for example, 1/3 and 2/6 designate the same rational. By eliminating all such redundancies, we arrive at the more streamlined series: 

1, 2, ½, 1/3, 3, 4, 3/2, 2/3, ¼, 1/5, 5, 6, 5/2, 4/3, ¾, 2/5, 1/6, 1/7, 3/5, ..., p/q, ...

Since this series includes all the rationals and can obviously be put into a one-to-one correspondence with the series 1, 2, 3, 4, ..., we have established that the rationals are countable. Therefore, they have the same cardinality as the integers, namely aleph-null.

Although the proof sketched above is sometimes referred to as Cantor's "diagonal proof" for obvious reasons, it is, in fact, incorrect. His real diagonal proof is one of the procedures he used to demonstrate that the cardinality of the real numbers is greater than that of the integers. This proof works by reductio: we start by assuming that the real numbers are denumerable—that is, that they can be put in a long row and paired one-to-one with the series 1, 2, 3, 4, ...—and then prove that this assumption leads to a contradiction.

The first step is to represent all the real numbers in decimal notation. Irrational numbers, such as the square root of 2, can be expressed in decimal form, as can rational numbers. Some rationals have non-terminating decimal expansions, like 1/3 equals 0.333..., while others terminate, like ½ equals 0.5. To maintain uniformity, we opt to express terminating decimals by their non-terminating counterparts—for example, 0.5 becomes 0.4999...

Now suppose it is possible to list all the real numbers in a sequence, pairing each with a positive integer. This list arranges the decimal expansions of the reals in rows, with each real number represented by its digits following the decimal point.

Since this list is supposed to include all reals, to reach a contradiction we need to prove that it actually omits at least one real number. We construct such a missing number by altering the decimals along the diagonal of the list: the first digit of the new number's decimal differs from the first digit of the first number; the second digit differs from the second digit of the second number; and so on. By choosing each digit to differ in a simple systematic way, this new number cannot match any on the list.

Therefore, this newly constructed real number is missing from the list, contradicting our assumption that all the real numbers were listed. Thus, the real numbers are uncountable, having a larger cardinality than the integers and rationals.

Zeno and the Continuum II

Imagine an arrow in flight. At every instant during its flight, the arrow is at some specific location, occupying a volume of space that corresponds exactly to the arrow's own size. Since instants are durationless, and motion takes time, the arrow is not in motion at any individual instant. Yet, the arrow's entire flight consists solely of these instants. If the arrow is not moving at any of these instants, it is not moving at all. This is Zeno's "Arrow" paradox. The reasoning obviously generalizes; what holds for the arrow holds for any object in motion—and so if the argument is sound, motion does not and cannot occur.

The argument is sometimes taken—along with the "Stadium" paradox—as challenging the doctrine that space and time are discrete rather than continuous. While this may be true for the Stadium, it is not obvious in the case of the Arrow. In any event, the Arrow paradox poses a significant problem for those who believe space and time are continuous. If space and time consist of dimensionless points, and the arrow's flight involves its occupying a continuous succession of different locations, we face the problem of explaining how it is possible for the arrow to have moved, given it is not moving during any of the individual instants that constitute its flight.

The At-at Theory

Those who subscribe to the orthodox conception of the continuum have a response to the Arrow challenge that is widely, though not universally, accepted. The proposal is this: whether or not an object is in motion at a given time does not depend solely on its condition at that time. Rather, it depends on how the object behaves at times neighboring that moment. If at these neighboring times the object occupies precisely the same location as at the given time, then the object is considered not to be moving at that time; but if it occupies different locations, then it is considered to be moving.Since everyone can agree that an object is moving if it is changing its location over time, this simple account seems to give us all we might want. Or, as Russell puts it, "Motion consists merely in the fact that bodies are sometimes in one place and sometimes in another, and that they are at intermediate places at intermediate times" (1917: 84); hence the "at-at" label for this view.

It will be useful to take a closer look at what proponents of the at-at account are proposing. Suppose we start with an interval I₁, running from half a second before t to half a second after t. We calculate the arrow's average velocity over I₁ by dividing the distance traveled during this one-second interval by the time. This gives us an initial, reasonably accurate approximation of the object's velocity at t. To get a more accurate approximation, we repeat the procedure for a shorter interval I₂, also centered on t but running from a quarter of a second before it to a quarter of a second after it. By working out the average velocities over shorter and shorter intervals centered on t, we generate increasingly accurate estimates of the object's velocity at t itself.

We now introduce the limit value of this series at t: it is the average velocity to which the sequence of smaller intervals converges as their endpoints approach t. We stipulate that the object's instantaneous velocity at t is simply this limit value.

This procedure applies generally to deliver instantaneous velocities of any moving object at any time. Indeed, this is essentially the method used in calculus, where velocity is the "first derivative" of position with respect to time. Since Newton and Leibniz, calculus has been at the heart of physics and applied mathematics. Much of physics' success is due to calculus; without it, we could not calculate precise rates of change of interdependent quantities as required in science and technology.

Velocity as Intrinsic

Despite its advantages, the at-at conception of motion has critics. It seems natural to think of movement as a purely intrinsic property of objects. If an object is moving at time t, that fact is part of the object's intrinsic condition at t; what happens at other times or places is irrelevant. But if the at-at conception is correct, motion is not intrinsic in this way. Whether an object is moving at t depends entirely on whether it changes its position at other times near t: the object's state at t alone doesn't determine this. This can seem strange or even wrong.

Consider a billion-ton rock \( R_1 \) moving swiftly toward the Moon’s surface, about to cause a massive explosion on impact. On the opposite side of the Moon is another rock \( R_2 \), very similar in size, shape, and mass, but motionless in a peaceful dustbowl. Focus on the instant just before \( R_1 \) hits the surface. According to the at-at account, there is no intrinsic difference between \( R_1 \) and \( R_2 \) at that instant: their instantaneous intrinsic properties are indistinguishable. But can this be right? \( R_1 \) is moving, with a frightening velocity, while \( R_2 \) is still. Surely there must be a significant difference between their conditions at that instant.

Proponents of the at-at approach argue that much of their view’s power depends on assuming a real, frame-independent distinction between motion and rest. Velocity may be absolute in Newtonian spacetime, but as discussed in Chapter 12, there are classical spacetimes where uniform velocity is frame-relative. If a body at time t can be equally described as moving or at rest, then the notion that the intrinsic state is affected by motion becomes tenuous. Since velocity in our universe is relative rather than absolute, recognizing this makes the at-at theory more acceptable.

However, other challenges remain. Conceptually, if the at-at account is correct, it is senseless to suppose that an object existing only for a single moment can be in motion. Motion, under the at-at view, requires occupying different spatial locations at neighboring times, so an object that does not exist at times adjacent to t cannot be in motion at t. But this seems strange. Could an object flash into existence for only a moment and still be in motion during that time? Tooley presents a scenario involving an unusual force field making objects flash in and out of existence infinitely many times, existing only at moments corresponding to irrational distances from the field’s center. An object moving through this field at constant speed seems naturally described as moving at the moments it exists, despite not existing at neighboring times. Defenders of the at-at theory deny this object is genuinely in motion and cast doubt on the intuitions suggesting otherwise.

Arntzenius highlights another difficulty: imagine a ball moving right to left through a region in space, and later an identical ball moving left to right through the same region. If the full state of a ball at a time does not include instantaneous velocity, then their states are identical when occupying the same region. How then does one ball "know" to continue moving right and the other left? More precisely, why does one ball continue moving in one direction and the other in the opposite? It seems that conservation of velocity or something like it must determine their directions. But if instantaneous velocity does not exist as in the at-at theory, why do the balls continue in different directions?

Classical motion depends on dynamic and kinematic factors: when a force acts on an object at time t, the reaction depends on the object's mass, the force’s size and direction, and its velocity at t. Classical physics equations treat velocity as a property of instantaneous states. However, these equations are formulated in calculus, where velocity at t is defined as the limit of average velocities over intervals shrinking to t. Thus, strictly speaking, instantaneous velocities do not play a direct role; what matters are temporally extended phases — changes in position over finite time intervals.

Taking these extended segments as fundamental may be acceptable, Arntzenius suggests, but it has a cost: the state of a ball at a time does not determine the direction it continues moving. This implies that all influences from past to future are not fully represented in the present. Consequently, the at-at view is incompatible with determinism as Laplace described it. Laplace wrote in 1814 that an intellect knowing all forces and positions at a moment could predict the future as precisely as the past. The key phrase is "at a certain moment."Determinism, in its classical guise, is the doctrine that it is possible to predict the entire future history of the universe, and retrodict its entire past history, from a complete description of its condition at the present moment of time. Suppose an intellect had all the positions and velocities of all objects at any one moment; it could then work out everything that happens before and after that moment. But if velocities are not intrinsic to the instantaneous states of moving objects, this form of determinism is untenable. In light of quantum and chaos theory, we might think there are strong grounds for supposing that Laplacean determinism does not apply in our universe. Still, it is odd to think that the nature of velocity alone rules out determinism—even in classical universes.

If the consequences of the at-at theory (which defines motion as change in location over time) seem unacceptable, what alternatives exist? One option is to hold that motion involves more than just change in location. Some medieval thinkers posited an intrinsic property called impetus, which disposes an object to move at a certain speed and direction. This property was thought to be gradually used up, which is why objects slow down and stop.

Contemporary authors have proposed similar ideas. Lowe suggests that instantaneous velocity may be a directional tendency an object possesses at a time, indicating a future change in position if it continues to possess that tendency. Tooley considers instantaneous velocity a functionally defined intrinsic property—a property that plays the role of velocity according to the laws of nature. Tooley and others argue velocity should be regarded as a primitive property like mass or charge. Critics counter that since this property has the same empirical consequences as the standard at-at account, it adds unnecessary complexity. Marc Lange objects on the basis that, unlike mass or charge, velocity is clearly connected to motion along a trajectory, so positing an inscrutable intrinsic nature for it is implausible.

Lange, doubting the at-at account's adequacy for velocity’s causal and explanatory roles, proposes a variant of impetus theory: classical instantaneous velocity is a dispositional property—a tendency or power. To be moving at a certain speed and direction is to have a potential trajectory or a propensity to proceed along it. This dispositional property can be explained in counterfactual terms—an object has it at a time if it would be moving in a certain way after that time, assuming it exists. This approach analyzes velocity purely kinematically but has counterintuitive features: two objects could be identical in all categorical respects yet have different velocities due to different potential trajectories. Some may find this view unpalatable, preferring the traditional idea of an intrinsic property that makes objects prone to move in certain ways.

### 17.3 The "Stadium"

The fourth and last of Zeno's motion paradoxes is often called the "Stadium," or the "paradox of the moving rows." Its precise formulation and intended lesson are unclear, but there is broad agreement on its most interesting interpretation.

Imagine three rows, A, B, and C, each containing three equally spaced objects. Row A is stationary, while rows B and C move at the same constant speed in opposite directions: B moves right, C moves left. One second later, the objects have new positions aligned vertically (A₁ with B₁ and C₁, and so forth). Aristotle noted that these objects pass one another like runners in a race, which inspired the "Stadium" name.

At first, this seems straightforward. But consider object C₁: it starts aligned with B₃ and ends aligned with B₁, moving the entire distance between B₁ and B₃ in one second. Relative to row A, however, C₁ moves only half that distance—starting aligned with A₂ and ending with A₁. Thus, C₁'s movement relative to B is twice its movement relative to A.

Zeno found this discrepancy troubling. If C₁ had moved at the same rate relative to both rows, a problem would arise. But it is moving twice as fast relative to B as to A because B and C move in opposite directions. Aristotle explained the error: assuming a body takes equal time to move past a moving and a stationary body of equal size is mistaken.

Was Zeno simply unaware that objects can have different velocities relative to others in motion? A more charitable view suggests a deeper argument. While Zeno’s earlier paradoxes challenged the continuity of space and time, he might also have targeted the opposite belief held by some ancient Greeks—that space and time are discrete, composed of indivisible atoms of space ("hodons") and time ("chronons"). In this discrete view, there is a minimum spatial and temporal unit; no movement or division occurs within these indivisible atoms.

The discrete concept can be modeled by systems like cellular automata, where space and time progress in finite steps. 

Considering the Stadium in terms of discrete time, suppose the two phases are separated by just one temporal atom, meaning nothing happens between these moments. At the first instant, C₁ aligns with B₃ and A₂; at the next, it aligns with A₁ and B₁. Since A₁ is adjacent to A₂, this is unsurprising. However, regarding row B, what happened to B₂? It appears that C₁ bypassed B₂ without ever being aligned with it. If no time exists between these instants, this seems paradoxical.

Faced with this, one might abandon the discreteness hypothesis in favor of continuity—that there is always a moment between any two moments. 

However, proponents of the discrete model respond that the paradox reflects unusual but not incoherent features. Regarding C₁ and B₂, they argue these objects don't pass each other in the usual sense; simply, there is no time at which they align.A complete description of what happens to C₁ over the relevant interval is simply this: at one instant, C₁ is aligned with B₃ (and A₂); at the very next instant, it is aligned with B₁ (and A₁).

To assert that there must be a time at which C₁ is aligned with B₂ is to beg the question against the discreteness hypothesis, as it assumes that there is always a time between any two times. While objects may seemingly "leap" discontinuously from one location to another in this scenario, such discontinuous movements are problematic only if one begins with the assumption that space and time are continuous. If they are discrete, then there is simply no other way to move. Indeed, as Wesley C. Salmon points out, if space is discrete, the kind of "passing without actually passing" Zeno found puzzling would be commonplace. Imagine two arrows moving towards one another through a discrete space along adjacent parallel paths, moving one grid square per unit of time. The arrows pass each other without spending any time adjacent to one another.

More complicated forms of motion can also be envisaged. Instead of moving in a straight line at one spatial unit per unit of time, an object might move two or more units per time unit, or move only every other time unit. While these modes of motion—bigger jumps and pauses—may seem odd, they are not logically incoherent and may be necessary to handle objects moving at a variety of speeds.

The discrete model faces other challenges beyond Zeno’s paradox. Hermann Weyl, in his *Philosophy of Mathematics and Science*, highlights a problem now known as "Weyl's tile argument." Suppose we model a plane in a discrete space with a 5-by-5 grid. The vertical and horizontal sides of a right-angled triangle formed this way are each five units long, but the diagonal measured by counting tiles along that path is also five units. This contradicts Euclidean geometry, where the diagonal should be about 7.07 units long according to the Pythagorean theorem.

One might think that making these atomic spatial units smaller would solve the discrepancy. However, as Wesley Salmon explains, doubling the number of units to a 10-by-10 grid yields the same problem: the diagonal length measured in tiles equals the sides, showing that smaller units don't resolve this issue.

There are responses to Weyl's tile problem. One is to assume that lines in discrete space have finite thickness, defining length by the number of spatial atoms contained in rectangles along the line. This approach, developed by Jean Paul van Bendegem, avoids the issue but relies on classical continuous geometry concepts, which don’t fully align with the discrete model.

Alternatively, Peter Forrest suggests it is mistaken to represent discrete space as a tiling of regular shapes, like squares, because this implies points have extension and introduces unwanted metrical problems. Instead, Forrest models discrete spaces as chains of adjacent points. On a micro-level, distances in such spaces are non-Euclidean, but at larger scales, they can approximate Euclidean geometry closely.

The tiled-plane representation has another drawback: it implies preferred directions, like the horizontal and vertical axes on a checkered board, while Euclidean space is isotropic—meaning it has the same properties in all directions. To address this, Van Bendegem proposes using non-periodic tiling, such as Penrose tilings, which are aperiodic and lack classical straight lines. In physics, quasicrystals exhibit this sort of aperiodic structure, suggesting that discrete space could also be aperiodic.

Another option is to accept Forrest's view and abandon the idea that spatial atoms have a definite shape. His models are locally anisotropic, meaning directionally dependent, but this effect diminishes at larger scales. Given estimates of about 10^30 spatial atoms per centimeter, these irregularities would be imperceptible macroscopically.

Could Our Spacetime Be Discrete?  

The discrete model may well be conceptually coherent, raising the question: might it apply to space and time in our universe?

The discrete model conflicts with the mathematical methods commonly used in physics, which assume continuity. However, it also has clear advantages. Zeno's paradoxes rely on infinite divisibility of space and time; if space and time are composed of finite atoms, these paradoxes do not arise. Similarly, if time is not made of durationless instants, the puzzle of how infinitely many such instants could sum to a finite duration disappears.

Another advantage is that discrete spaces and times possess intrinsic metrical properties. Counting the number of atoms comprising an interval provides a natural measure of its size, applying equally to volumes of space or spacetime.

None of this proves that the discrete model describes our world, but it shows there would be benefits if it did. The strongest objection is that motions in our world appear smooth and continuous even when measured precisely. The proponent of discrete space and time responds that this granularity exists only at microscopic scales beyond our ability to observe, which explains why the world seems smooth even if it is fundamentally discrete. Although this reply lacks direct empirical support, it remains a plausible explanation given the nature of microscopic granularity.Nonetheless, the discrete theorist can at least claim an account of the world that appears empirically equivalent to the orthodox continuity-based model. More importantly, ultimately, is the conception of space and time (or spacetime) found in our best scientific theories. Newtonian space and time are continuous, not discrete, and the same applies to the spacetimes of Einstein's special and general relativity. By contrast, quantum theory might seem more congenial to the discreteness hypothesis: if physical quantities such as angular momentum and charge are quantized, might not the same apply to space and time? Grünbaum warns against this. From the fact that some properties of single-particle systems are quantized, it would be wrong to conclude that space and time are also quantized in the theory: they are not. Every point of continuous physical space is a potential sharply defined position of, say, an electron, and every instant of continuous time is potentially the time of a physical event. This sense of continuity fully allows quantum theory to repudiate, via the Heisenberg Uncertainty Relations, the existence of well-defined particle trajectories as in classical mechanics.

However, the fact that our best current theories are formulated in terms of continuous space and time does not mean that future theories will be. Quantum theory and general relativity—our best guides to the very small and the very large—are incompatible. The form a viable quantum theory of gravity will take is currently unknown; much work has been done and some progress made. Interestingly, some promising approaches reject the continuous spacetime of general relativity in favor of a quantized conception. Lee Smolin describes one such approach called loop quantum gravity as proposing that space is made of discrete atoms, each carrying a tiny unit of volume. Unlike ordinary geometry, a given region cannot have a volume arbitrarily big or small, but only one of a finite set of numbers. Since these minimum volumes are exceedingly small, it is not surprising that the macroscopic world appears smooth.

We should not rush to conclusions—other approaches, such as some versions of string theory, are formulated with orthodox continuous spacetimes. Nor should we rule out the discrete conception simply because history has favored the smooth alternative so far.

### 17.5 The Standard Continuum: Basic Concerns and Further Puzzles

The standard view holds that space and time are composed of dimensionless points, forming a non-denumerable continuum, like the real number line. Despite its success in physics and engineering, this view has faced opposition regarding the micro-structure of space and time. Some of this opposition stems from paradoxes related to Zeno.

As seen earlier, proponents of the orthodox view have responses to these paradoxes that many find satisfactory. Nevertheless, opposition continues, even from mathematicians and physicists well-versed in the underlying mathematics. To conclude, we examine what motivates this opposition and some developments it has inspired. One alternative is the doctrine that space and time are quantized or discrete rather than continuous. This is not the only alternative.

### Density and Continuity

The defining characteristic of a continuum is continuity. Can a collection of discrete dimensionless points form something truly continuous? Poincaré acknowledged Cantor's achievements but complained that the continuum, as conceived, is just an infinite collection of separated points, lacking the intimate connection that makes it one whole. According to him, the point has no existence prior to the line, but the line exists prior to the point. Multiplicity alone subsists; unity has disappeared. This conception differs from that of physicists and metaphysicians.

Gödel similarly said that summing all points does not yield the line but rather forms a scaffold on it. Weyl referred to the orthodox model as "shattering the continuum into isolated points" and sought an alternative, as did Brentano and Peirce.

One concern is that the orthodox continuum is dense, meaning between any two points there is always another point, so no two points are immediately adjacent. Points in a dense continuum can never be in immediate contact, so such a continuum might not be maximally or truly continuous. Defenders of the orthodox view counter that the continuum is truly continuous because it contains no gaps; all gaps between points are filled with more points. This is correct; however, because no points are ever in immediate contact, critics argue that true or maximal continuity remains unachieved. Our perceptual experience of smooth spatial colors or temporally extended sounds suggests continua that appear seamless. The orthodox continuum's point structure and density lack this unity, and for thinkers like Bergson, Brentano, Poincaré, Weyl, and Whitehead, it falls short of authentic continuity, motivating the search for alternatives.

### Plurality and Substance

Zeno's Plurality challenge asks how an extended line or volume can be made of dimensionless parts; an infinite number of zeros still sums to zero. Orthodox proponents have responses. Grünbaum noted that the idea of a whole's size being the sum of its parts applies to ordinary finite things and denumerably many parts but not to things with non-denumerable parts, like the continuum isomorphic to the real number system. Our ordinary ways of assigning and combining magnitudes do not apply to non-denumerable infinity; new methods are needed. Contemporary measure theory allows assigning a positive, non-zero measure to continua made entirely of dimensionless points.

Some find this satisfactory—for example, Graham Oppy praised Grünbaum's analysis—but there is an objection: these considerations address mathematical issues but contribute little to resolving key metaphysical problems.

For example, can we truly make sense of a material object being composed entirely of zero-sized parts? Ontologically, bricks, trees, and planets differ greatly from abstract entities like sets or numbers. Mathematicians' ways of assigning magnitudes to infinite collections of abstract entities are of limited relevance to Zeno's real problem. It remains hard to understand how a concrete material thing could consist solely of dimensionless parts. A zero-size piece of matter would be indistinguishable from nothing. A million nothings is nothing; even an uncountable infinity of nothings is still nothing. You cannot build a brick, tree, or other substantial object from parts that individually and collectively amount to nothing.

This objection has strong intuitive appeal regarding ordinary material things, or rather, ordinary material things as conventionally conceived.The pre-scientific (or naive) conception of a material object is that of a three-dimensional impenetrable solid, filled with colored stuff of some sort, in the manner of a hunk of modeling clay. It is certainly not easy to comprehend how substantial solids of this sort could be entirely composed of zero-dimensional parts.

But of course modern physics undermines the intuitive conception of the material world. If current theories are broadly correct, bricks and trees are composed of elementary particles that are themselves patterns of activity in various space-filling quantum fields. It is by no means so obvious, intuitively, that these continuous fields could not be composed of zero-sized parts.

However, our concern here is not with matter, but with space (or spacetime). Substantivalists regard space as a concrete material thing in its own right, albeit of a distinctive kind. Further, by virtue of its continuity and homogeneity, substantival space has at least some of the features of the ordinary pre-scientific conception of material objects; it consists of extended volumes, filled everywhere with (spatial or spatiotemporal) stuff. Accordingly, if the substantival conception of space is the correct conception of space, the objection to Grünbaum outlined above still has considerable force. It is not easy to see how a substantival space could be entirely composed of zero-sized parts, for a zero-sized portion of substantival space just does not exist, or so it might reasonably be claimed.

The situation looks very different, however, on the opposing relationist conception of space. For relationists, the spatial (or spatiotemporal) continuum does not exist as a physical ingredient in its own right; all that exists are material objects and their distance relations. On this view, the extended space-filling continuum is, at best, a useful fiction. Since this fiction standardly takes the form of a mathematical or set-theoretical representation, Grünbaum's treatment of Zeno's plurality puzzle looks eminently appropriate: if the continuum is no more than a mathematical abstraction, there is no obvious objection to applying a consistent set-theoretic account of magnitude or measure to it. But since one of the main lessons of earlier chapters is that the substantival view of space and spacetime has a great deal to be said for it, this result does not have the significance it might otherwise possess.

### Division and Contact

The standard view of the continuum is afflicted by a variety of puzzles and problems, some of which we have already encountered, explicitly or implicitly; others are new. To start with one of the simplest, take the real number interval from zero to one, and imagine dividing it without remainder into two portions of exactly equal size by inserting an infinitely sharp division point halfway between zero and one. At first view, this may seem unproblematic. Locating this point is easy: it lies exactly halfway between the extremes, so it suffices to insert our mental dividing knife right there.

But matters are not quite so straightforward. What do we do with the division point itself? Since our division is to leave no remainder, this point must belong to one portion or the other. If it belongs to the first portion, that portion becomes a closed interval and the second is left half-open, whereas if it belongs to the second portion, the situation is reversed. Peculiarly, the facts of geometry or arithmetic do not determine where the division point should go—either choice is equally justifiable. Furthermore, whichever option we choose results in one portion having a point the other doesn’t, making it, in this respect, larger, even though both are the same length from a metric perspective. As Gödel once remarked, it is natural to think that dividing a geometrical line at a point would produce a perfectly symmetrical result, yet the standard model cannot yield this.

There are further oddities when applying the standard model to physical reality. Consider a cube-shaped region of substantival space divided into two equal parts by a two-dimensional plane. One resulting region is open near that plane and the other is closed, having the plane as its outermost surface. While imagining a closed region with well-defined boundaries is straightforward, an open region is harder to picture. This distinction raises a question: can there be regions of physical space that differ by only a single layer of points? Do dimensionless points have any physical significance at all? The standard model may be positing an excess of structure. Michael Dummett concludes:

"The classical model [of the continuum] is to be rejected because it fails to provide any explanation of why what appears to intuition to be impossible should be impossible. It allows as possibilities what reason rules out, and leaves it to the contingent laws of physics to rule out what a good model of physical reality would not even be able to describe."

The density of the continuum also leads to puzzles. If material objects exactly occupy closed regions of space—as seems plausible—their outermost layer would be a two-dimensional skin just one point thick. This implies that material objects cannot truly come into direct contact: no matter how close their outer points get, the density of space guarantees another point always lies between them, preventing actual touching. One might suppose that when objects get very close, they cross these points instantaneously and achieve contact by sharing a boundary layer of points common to both. But this would be interpenetration or overlap, not contact as normally conceived. Is it truly impossible for material objects simply to touch each other?

As noted earlier, modern physics teaches that macroscopic objects are not solid in the naive sense, and are kept apart by repulsive forces among electrons at their surfaces. Nonetheless, it is surprising that the standard continuum seems incompatible with contact as we intuitively understand it. If truly solid objects did exist, space itself would prevent them from touching. For more on this, see Zimmerman (1996a, b) and Hudson (2005: chapters 2 and 3).

### Measure-Related Puzzles

Among the well-known paradoxes is the Tarski-Banach paradox, which proves that a spherical region of space can be divided into a finite number of pieces that, when rearranged, form two spheres each identical in size to the original. This is bizarre: ordinarily, dividing a sphere into parts should not enable one to double its volume simply by rearranging those parts.

The explanation lies in the peculiar properties of the Cantorian continuum. Every interval of the real number line contains as many points as any other, and indeed as many as a two-dimensional plane or higher-dimensional volumes. Thus, the original sphere and the two resulting spheres contain the same number of points. However, the rearrangement involves manipulating finitely many regions that are infinitely complex and "unmeasurable," meaning they lack a meaningful size or volume. This indeterminacy enables the paradoxical outcome. Since it is difficult to believe that real space behaves this way, the paradox presents a strong challenge to the standard continuum model.

None of the difficulties discussed may be decisive alone, but together they suggest that an alternative model of the continuum—with fewer or different problematic features—would be welcome. Proposals have mainly taken two forms: some theorists have suggested positing more points, while others deny the existence of points altogether. Let us begin with the first option.Are More Points the Answer?

Although Peirce held the view that the continuum is metaphysically prior to the existence of any points it may contain, he also believed—unlike Aristotle—that we could legitimately view the continuum as possessing such points. However, while the orthodox view maintains that there are only as many points in the continuum as there are real numbers, Peirce argued that this perspective falls short. As Cantor demonstrated, there are higher-order infinities—an infinite number of them.

Since there are larger infinities than c, if we assert that the continuum has only c points, then it does not possess the maximum possible number of points. For Peirce, this was a deficiency: the true continuum would have the maximum possible number of points. This "absolute infinity," as it is sometimes referred to, includes all the infinite sets in Cantor's hierarchy of ever larger infinities; thus, it is larger than any single set and is not itself a set (in contemporary terms, it is known as a "proper class"). Somewhat obscurely, Peirce maintained that when points are packed together in this hyper-dense manner, they lose their individuality and fuse together to form a true, cohesive continuum. This doctrine allows Peirce to develop a more intuitively plausible conception of geometrical division.

On the orthodox view, slicing a rectangle into two along a line L will produce one open and one closed object. Peirce argues for a different result. In his view, the points that jointly constitute the line have a rich inner structure; in effect, each individual point on the real line contains many further points—at least as many as the power set of c. When the rectangle is divided along line L, the points in L themselves undergo fission, and the newly created points form two whole and complete lines, L* and L. As a result, we end up with two fully closed objects, rather than the odd combination of one closed and one partially open object.

In a more measured—but perhaps not entirely dissimilar—vein, the various forms of "non-standard analysis" developed by Robinson and others since 1960 have successfully rehabilitated the infinitesimal. Infinitesimals—quantities possessing the property of being larger than zero but less than any ordinary finite quantity—were indispensable tools in calculus for a long time but were rendered redundant by Cauchy and Weierstrass's work on limits in the nineteenth century. Thanks to Robinson and his followers, mathematicians are now perfectly willing to use infinitesimals in various branches of their subject.

McLaughlin and Miller have argued that non-standard analysis, particularly in the form of Nelson's "internal set theory" (IST), can be used to resolve some of Zeno's paradoxes in a more intuitively compelling manner than would otherwise be possible. Consider first the Dichotomy. To reach the finishing line, Zeno has to complete all the Z-runs, yet there is an infinite number of these; how is it possible to complete an infinite sequence of distinct tasks? In rough outline, non-standard analysis of the IST variety aims to assist as follows: call the parts of Zeno's journey that have coordinates expressible in real numbers the R*-points, and those that require coordinates expressed in non-standard (or infinitesimal) terms the I*-points. Despite this additional complexity, from the perspective afforded by IST, Zeno's journey as a whole can be viewed as consisting of a finite sequence of steps.

McLaughlin and Miller further argue that although Zeno passes through all the I*-points, these portions of his journey have no empirical significance: our best measuring techniques will only ever discern his position on R*-points. Thus, as Zeno approaches ever closer to the finishing line, he will eventually move beyond the last R*-point and into the infinitesimal realm, from which he will actually arrive at and cross the finishing line itself. As for the way Zeno moves through this infinitesimal region, there are many possibilities: might the process of motion inside one of these intervals be a uniform advance across the interval, an instantaneous jump from one end to the other, a series of intermediate steps, or a process outside of time and space altogether? The possibilities are infinite, and none can be verified or ruled out since an infinitesimal interval can never be measured.

The Arrow paradox is treated similarly. It is true that Zeno's flying arrow is motionless or "frozen" at all the R*-points, and these are the only points at which we can hope to observe this (or any other) moving object. However, the arrow also passes through the I*-points along its trajectory. Consequently, we can regard the arrow as being in motion over the infinitesimal regions surrounding each of its R*-points, even though this motion cannot be empirically detected and its precise character remains indeterminate.

These resolutions are intriguing, but they also raise many questions. Joseph Alper and Mark Bridger point out that although McLaughlin and Miller's use of "finite" corresponds with Nelson's use in IST, in the present context it is misleading: a finite set (in the sense they intend) includes all standard real numbers plus much more. Aside from this, have we truly been provided with an intuitively clear picture of how Zeno arrives at his finishing line? This is certainly questionable: since McLaughlin and Miller's solution leaves parts of Zeno's journey indeterminate, it can be argued that one mystery has merely been swapped for another.

A further and related concern is their overt reliance on a form of verificationism: "The tactic of appealing to infinite sets to argue against the possibility of motion has been denied to Zeno by showing that such sets always contain references to non-verifiable occurrences." Is it really true, as McLaughlin asserts, that we "are not responsible for explaining situations we cannot observe"? This is not obviously true; after all, particle physicists and cosmologists spend a great deal of time trying to uncover the secrets of regions that are not directly observable.

In fact, it is not entirely clear what brand of verificationism McLaughlin and Miller are endorsing. There are two main options:  
(A) The movements of objects through the infinitesimal regions of space are in principle empirically inscrutable, and for this reason, there is no fact of the matter regarding what these movements are.  
(B) The movements of objects through the infinitesimal regions of space are in principle empirically inscrutable, but there is nonetheless a fact of the matter regarding what these movements are.

Each of these options is problematic, although for different reasons. Option A introduces genuine ontological indeterminacy into the world, a step that should not be taken lightly. Quantum theory also introduces indeterminacy into the micro-realm, but this indeterminacy was accepted as a reality only after exhaustive investigations failed to reveal any alternatives. The indeterminacy McLaughlin and Miller want to introduce arises only if we reject the standard model of the continuum in favor of their non-standard alternative. It is by no means clear that this proposed resolution of Zeno's motion paradoxes is sufficiently superior to orthodox alternatives to warrant taking such a step.

Option B does not introduce ontological indeterminacy and is thus less objectionable. However, anyone opting for B is vulnerable to the charge that Zeno's worries are merely being swept under the carpet. We are now to suppose that moving objects pursue a quite definite trajectory through the non-standard points found in the infinitesimal regions of space, even though we cannot ascertain what these trajectories are. Since some of these trajectories presumably involve traversing infinite series of non-standard points, we have not circumvented the Dichotomy puzzle: how is it possible to complete an infinite series of movements? As the trajectory of a moving object will consist of its being located, in a static fashion, on a series of non-standard points, the Arrow is also still a pressing concern.

In this context, McLaughlin's assertion that we are under no obligation to explain what we cannot observe is surely problematic; we can observe motion, even if only along paths formed from R*-points.

Under option B, the motion we can observe is possible only because objects also move along perfectly determinate—although unobservable—paths through the infinitesimal regions of space. If observed motion depends in this way on unobserved motion, are we not under an obligation to understand how the latter is possible?Extension as Fundamental

If we want a model of the continuum that best accords with our intuitive conception of what a continuum is truly like—a conception rooted in part in the smooth homogeneities we find in our sensory experience—the more promising approach is to “go pointless,” that is, to devise a physical geometry that makes no appeal to points whatsoever, at least at the fundamental level. According to Peirce, "A continuum (such as time and space actually are) is defined as something any part of which, however small, has parts of the same kind. Every part of a surface is a surface, and every part of a line is a line" (quoted in Moore 2007b: 429).

This characterization entails that space or time (or any other true continuum) is infinitely divisible in a distinctive way: take any part you like, however tiny, and it will always be composed of further and smaller extended parts, and so on without end. Since the orthodox continuum—modeled on the real line—is also infinitely divisible, the difference lies in that the orthodox continuum possesses parts that do not themselves possess parts and parts that are not extended; these are the dimensionless points that have been the cause of much concern.

The sort of continuum implicit in Peirce's characterization is very different. Since all of its parts, without exception, can be divided into ever smaller extended parts, it does not include any points as traditionally conceived, only smaller and smaller finite segments. As Weyl puts it, "That it has parts is a fundamental property of the continuum... it is inherent in the nature of the continuum that every part of it can be further divided without limitation" (1998: 115).

From a metaphysical perspective, this view takes extended entities as fundamental; an extended thing is not composed of more basic parts that themselves lack extension. In the case of a two-dimensional line, the basic entity is an extended line segment; in the case of three-dimensional space, it is an extended spatial volume. Points and lines remain as useful abstractions imposed on fundamentally extended reality, which does not possess parts corresponding to isolated points or lines. Like its Cantorian counterpart, the essentially extended continuum has infinite complexity: each part, no matter how small, is infinitely divisible. Indeed, lacking a “bottom level” of indivisible points, it may be regarded as more thoroughly divisible than the orthodox continuum.

Conceiving the continuum this way brings a significant advantage: it circumvents Zeno's Plurality paradox. If space and time are not composed of dimensionless points, the issue of how such points could combine to form a finite extension does not arise. It also yields a metaphysically simpler and arguably superior conception of substantival space compared to both the orthodox point-filled continuum and discrete or quantized alternatives. In point-based or discrete models, a question arises as to what unifies the basic spatial parts into a single unified space. There must be some unifying connection among the parts to constitute a single spatial substance, which may be ontologically costly to posit. In the essentially extended conception, no such additional unifying relation is needed because there are no zero-dimensional points. Instead, small extended regions exist, but these are not indivisible or exclusive like spatial atoms. Regions of essentially extended space can contain other regions nested within them, be wholly contained within larger spatial or spatio-temporal regions, and connect to one another through overlapping regions. In effect, a space or spacetime of this kind is self-unifying.

In recent terminology, an entity structured in this way is described as composed of “atomless gunk,” or simply “gunk.” This term originates from Lewis (1991), who used it to characterize infinitely divisible but atomless matter. Now, it is often applied to space, time, and spacetime, contrasting the “pointy” conception with the “gunky” view of space (cf. Arntzenius 2008). Although the term is recent, the view has a long history, arguably supported by Aristotle, Descartes, Kant, and Peirce on some occasions.

In the twentieth century, Whitehead notably championed the essentially extended gunk-like view (1919, 1920, 1929). He devised a method of "extensive abstraction" to define planes, lines, and points in terms of extended volumes. For example, a point can be defined as an infinitely converging set of nested spherical regions. Broad praised Whitehead's point-free approach and described its application to space (1923), and Tarski (1956) developed it axiomatically. Although Whitehead's systems had mathematical flaws, later work has addressed these issues in various ways (see Grzegorczyk 1960; Clarke 1985; Gerla 1990; Forrest 1996a; Roeper 1997; Biacino & Gerla 1996).

As interest in this point-free conception of continua has grown, new problems have emerged. Gunk also gives rise to measure-theoretic paradoxes (see Forrest 1996b; Hawthorne & Weatherson 2004; Arntzenius 2008). Some forms of gunk may be more finely divisible than others; Nolan (2004) suggests "hypergunk" as a possibility.
