Another issue is how to reconcile gunk-like space and time with orthodox point-based mathematics used in major physics theories. One option is to follow Whitehead by defining points and lines in terms of extended volumes and then using them in physical theorizing. Although this has advantages, it may be objected that points have not truly been eliminated from our ontology if they remain fundamental in our theories. For surveys of alternative and more radical approaches, see Arntzenius & Hawthorne (2005).

Regarding Zeno's paradoxes, if space and time are gunk-like, the paradox of Plurality disappears. The problem posed by the Arrow paradox is also diminished. Without points, space and time always come in finite intervals, however small. It no longer makes sense to conceive of a moving arrow as located at a particular point at an instant; rather, the arrow exists over finite intervals—however brief—during which it changes its spatial location continuously. Thus, the "stroboscopic" character of motion in the orthodox pointy continuum is avoided.However, since gunk-like space is also infinitely divisible, Zeno will still have to complete an infinite number of sub-journeys to reach the terminus of any one of these sub-intervals. For those who find this unacceptable, one alternative is the discrete model we encountered in 17.3. This conception of the continuum also faces difficulties, not least of which is the "unity problem" we have just encountered.

Another option would be to remain within the confines of the essentially extended framework but to find a way of introducing a discrete or quantized element into it, such that Zeno can reach his destination after only a finite number of steps. The "fuzzy realist" model of the temporal continuum outlined in Dummett (2000) is one way forward; no doubt, there are other possibilities to be explored. As we saw in 17.4, when it comes to discreteness, developments in physics may have an essential role to play.

As of now, only one thing is clear: it may well be that we will never arrive at a conception of the continuum that offers all that we might want and expect. Nonetheless, since there are several avenues worthy of further exploration and no doubt others that have yet to be discovered, there is every reason to persevere with the search.

### 18 Special Relativity

### 18.1 Time, Space, and Einstein

Our investigations up to this point have not been in vain. They have certainly led us to a deeper understanding of the complexities of the issues we are concerned with, but they have also been inconclusive. Very different accounts of the large-scale structure of time seem both metaphysically viable and compatible with the character of our everyday experience. Space has proved an equally stubborn topic. Substantivalism may be ahead on points in certain respects, but there have been few clear-cut victories. However, for those looking for definite answers, all is not yet lost.

We have yet to consider the impact of more recent scientific developments, and some believe that answers to the questions we have been considering can be found here. Of course, these answers will only concern the space and time of our world, rather than space and time in general, but being so restricted would scarcely reduce their interest.

However, although contemporary science has a great deal to teach us, anyone looking for definitive answers to our questions will be disappointed. Our current best theory of the very small—quantum theory—and our current best theory of the very large—Einstein's general theory of relativity—have yet to be reconciled, and there is no consensus among physicists as to what the overall character of the theory that synthesizes the two, "quantum gravity," will be. Some believe that the successor theory will retain the most distinctive features of both relativity and quantum theory; others believe that at least some of these features will disappear.

The waters are further muddied by ongoing disputes over the correct interpretation of relativity and quantum theory themselves. As a consequence, much of what we can learn about our space and time from current physics can only have a provisional status. This said, the results gleaned from physics are vitally important, for no matter how unreliable they may prove, there is at present no better guide to the real nature of our own time and space. They are also fascinating in their own right. The advances in physics made in the past century make one thing almost certain: time and space are stranger than Newton ever imagined. That we can know this owes much to Einstein's special theory of relativity, which we will be exploring in this chapter and the next. Among the counterintuitive consequences of special relativity, according to its standard interpretation, are the following:

1. Common sense says that the rate at which time passes, as measured by clocks, is the same for everyone and everything, regardless of their motion relative to one another. Consider two twins, Alice and Bob; if Alice embarks on a long journey and returns, she will still be precisely the same age as Bob. According to special relativity, this is wrong: Alice will be younger than stay-at-home Bob—in effect, time has passed at a slower rate for her than for Bob.

2. Common sense says that temporal and spatial intervals are independent. If two events are separated by a spatial distance and a temporal distance, then this is just a fact about the world. According to special relativity, this is wrong: these events will be separated by different spatial and temporal intervals in different frames of reference. Time and space thus lose their independence from each other, and we are left with a notion of Einsteinian—or Minkowski—spacetime, a unified entity that decomposes into different spatial and temporal intervals depending on the reference frame.

3. Common sense says that there is a fact of the matter about which events happen at the same time as other events, regardless of their spatial separation, and hence that simultaneity is an objective feature of the universe. This belief also falls victim to special relativity: as a consequence of the second point, whether or not two events are simultaneous depends on the reference frame from which they are considered; observers in different frames of reference will find different events simultaneous, and there is no sense in the idea that one observer is right and the other wrong.

There is no denying the strangeness of these ideas, so it is important to understand the grounds for accepting the theory that yields them, and I will provide some of the necessary background. However, I will also consider the metaphysical implications of the theory. The debate between substantivalists and relationists takes a new turn within the context of special relativity, but since I will be focusing on this debate when discussing general relativity (in Chapters 20 and 21), I will postpone dealing with it until then. More intriguing is the impact of special relativity on the debate about the nature of time, which is commonly thought to be dramatic. Influential philosophers have argued that special relativity's relativization of simultaneity (which is preserved in general relativity) establishes beyond reasonable doubt the Block view of time. If they are right, special relativity provides the solution to one of our two fundamental problems; I will examine this issue in Chapter 19. But before entering these murky waters, the basics of special relativity itself must be laid bare.

### 18.2 Lightspeed

Einstein's special theory of relativity may have many bizarre consequences, but they all flow from just two simple principles:

- The Relativity Postulate: The laws of nature are the same in all inertial frames of reference, so there is no experiment that can reveal whether one is at rest or in uniform motion.

- The Light Postulate: The speed of light in a vacuum is a constant, denoted c, independent of its source and the same in all inertial frames of reference.

Why these seemingly innocuous principles have such momentous consequences may not be immediately obvious, but the reasoning involved is not particularly complex, as we shall see. The difficult step, which Einstein alone took, was to take seriously the possibility that these principles might be true. At the time, when special relativity first appeared in print in 1905, this was far from obvious.

The Relativity Postulate is a strong formulation of the principle of Galilean equivalence. As Leibniz pointed out, although Newtonian physics postulates an absolute space, there is no empirical method of detecting absolute velocity, and this fact had troubled physicists over time. As the nineteenth century drew to a close, it seemed this problem might finally be solved: a way of measuring absolute velocity had finally emerged. The key lay in the behavior of light.

In the 1860s, James Clerk Maxwell published a comprehensive theory of electrical and magnetic phenomena, synthesizing much experimental work of previous centuries, especially that done by Faraday earlier in the century. Maxwell’s theory, expressed in four equations, accounted for all then-observable behavior of electrical and magnetic phenomena and showed how electrical and magnetic forces are intimately interrelated. In particular, he demonstrated theoretically how electric and magnetic fields could combine to form self-propagating electromagnetic waves. He predicted the velocity of these waves from his equations, which turned out to be the speed of light, already determined experimentally. It seemed improbable that this was a coincidence, so he proposed that light itself was an electromagnetic wave, which was later confirmed. Since waves can vary in frequency and wavelength, Maxwell also predicted other kinds of electromagnetic waves, which was again proved right: at the low-frequency end of the spectrum are radio waves, while at the high-frequency end are X-rays and gamma radiation.

Physicists had been studying waves prior to the 1860s, and since all known waves were perturbations on or in something—water, air, the surface of drums, and so on—everyone assumed that light waves also consisted of perturbations in a medium. But there was a problem. Light can pass through a jar from which all trace of gas has been removed, so the required medium was clearly of a very special sort: it completely permeates seemingly empty space, even the space between the stars.

Moreover, since light also passes through the solid glass of the jar itself, its medium extends through solid objects as well as seemingly empty space. This invisible but all-pervasive medium was given a name: the aether.Having posited this remarkable medium, questions soon arose about its properties: "aether-physics" was born. Since planets and other bodies are not noticeably retarded by anything when they move in a vacuum, the aether doesn't create any frictional drag. Since light seems to travel in straight lines, even in the close proximity of large moving bodies, these bodies cannot produce any distortion in the aether, which must therefore be very rigid. One question was of particular significance: what is the aether's state of motion? After finding reasons for ruling out various possibilities, it was generally agreed that the aether must be in a state of absolute rest (some took a further step and argued that the aether and Newtonian absolute space were one and the same entity). This consensus was to prove fruitful, for, as was soon realized, an experimental test for distinguishing absolute motion from absolute rest was now possible.

At this point, some basic properties of waves—as understood in Newtonian physics—become relevant. The velocity of a projectile depends on the velocity of the object that expels it. Suppose that shells fired from a jet fighter's wing cannon move at 2000 km/h when the plane is stationary. If the same shells were to be fired when the plane is traveling at 1000 km/h, their velocity would be 3000 km/h. Waves do not behave like this; their velocities vary depending on the medium (e.g., light is slowed by glass), but they are not affected by how fast their sources are moving. The sound waves created by a plane that flies overhead move at the same speed of 1200 km/h in all directions; they do not move faster in the direction the plane is flying. So if the plane is flying at 1000 km/h, and the pilot has an onboard device that detects and displays the sound waves radiating from the plane, he will see the waves directly in front of him moving forward at only 200 km/h, whereas in the opposite direction the waves are speeding away at 2200 km/h. Someone with a similar device on Earth, directly below the pilot, will see the waves traveling at the same speed in each direction.

In a precisely similar fashion, Maxwell's equations predict that light rays will travel from their point of origin in all directions at the same speed: three hundred thousand kilometers per second. If we set about measuring the speed of the light waves emitted by a particular source, we will only measure them as traveling at that speed if we are at rest relative to the aether when we conduct the tests. If we are moving through the aether, we will find that the waves moving in the same direction as ourselves have a slower speed than those moving in the opposite direction. In 1881, a young American physicist, Albert Michelson, set about testing this prediction using very precise equipment that he had invented himself. Assuming that the Earth moves through the aether as it orbits the Sun, it must have different states of motion relative to the aether at different times of the year. Michelson's equipment measured the time it took for light to travel along two paths of equal distance in a laboratory; one of these paths was aligned in the direction of the Earth's motion around the Sun, while the other was at right angles to this direction. If, when the experiment is conducted, the Earth is moving relative to the aether, the time taken for the two trips would be different, or so theory predicted (just as the pilot in the plane gets a different speed reading for sound waves moving in the same direction as him than for waves moving upwards, downwards, or rearwards). Michelson repeated the experiment at different times of the year but found no evidence whatsoever for any variations in light speed. Light, it seemed, travels at exactly the same speed in all directions, irrespective of the direction of the Earth's motion through the aether. This was an astonishing result and a worrying one, since it threatened accepted theory. So in 1887, Michelson, now working with Edward Morley, repeated the experiments to a much higher degree of precision. Again, no variations were detected, even though the equipment was sensitive enough to detect variations a hundred times smaller than those predicted to occur. All subsequent tests have confirmed the Michelson-Morley result.

The finding that light always travels at the same speed, irrespective of who or what is observing it and how fast they are moving relative to its source, is as remarkable now as it was unexpected then, for it conflicts with some of our most basic intuitions about motion. If a car is moving towards you on a collision course, you can diminish the speed at which it will hit you by running away from it or increase the speed by running towards it. The Michelson-Morley experiments show that light doesn't conform to this principle.

This result could not be explained by classical physics, but suggestions as to how to accommodate it were quickly forthcoming, the most notable of which was the compensatory theory proposed by Fitzgerald and developed in detail by Lorentz. The general idea is that when you are moving in the same direction as a ray of light, the light actually does overtake you at a slower speed than it would if you were motionless or moving in the opposite direction; however, the decrease is not detected because of changes caused by your motion through the aether: your measuring instruments contract in length, and your clocks slow down. Lorentz provided equations that show just how much contraction and time dilation (slowing) is needed to conceal the changes in the speed of light in different circumstances.

The solution that Einstein proposed also involves length contractions and time dilation—indeed, he made use of Lorentz's own equations—but in one crucial respect, it was far simpler. The compensatory theory says that a given ray of light has different speeds for different observers (in motion relative to it), but nature conspires to conceal these differences. In a bold stroke, Einstein did away with the need for a conspiracy. He reasoned that if nature makes it impossible for us to discover our state of motion concerning the aether, perhaps we are wrong in supposing that the aether exists. Why not take nature to be as it presents itself? In rejecting the aether, he also rejected the notions of absolute motion and rest, taking all inertial frames to be equivalent. He then accepted the consequence: a ray of light really does travel at the same speed relative to all observers, irrespective of their state of motion relative to it.

To get some impression of what this can mean in practice, suppose that Alice and Bob are in outer space. Alice points her torch in Bob's direction and switches it on. She sees the light ray moving at three hundred thousand kilometers per second towards Bob. Now Bob, as it happens, is moving away from Alice at the very high speed of half that, yet when he measures the speed at which the light passes him using his rod and clock, he finds that it is moving at the same three hundred thousand kilometers per second. How can this possibly be? Einstein's explanation runs thus: when Alice observes Bob, she will see that his measuring rod has contracted in length and his clock is running slow (compared with her clock). Since speed is distance traveled divided by time taken, Bob's readings are now comprehensible: given his altered measuring instruments, it is expected that his readings differ from Alice's. But this isn't quite the end of the story. Given the Relativity Postulate and the abandonment of absolute motion, Bob is perfectly entitled to regard himself as at rest and Alice to be moving at high speed. From Bob's standpoint, it is his readings that are correct and Alice's that are deviant since, when he observes Alice, he will see that her measuring rod has contracted and her clock is running slow. According to Einstein, both perspectives are equally valid, so there is no objective fact of the matter as to who is right. And there are many other perspectives on the same facts—those of potential observers in different inertial reference frames, all of whom are entitled to regard themselves as at rest, and all of whom will find Alice and Bob to have different states of motion relative to themselves.

The distinctive innovation of the Special Theory of Relativity is the replacement of one absolute for another. Newton's absolute space and time are both rejected, and a new absolute or invariant quantity is introduced: the velocity of light. Since velocity involves both distance and time, the only way for a given light ray to have the same velocity in frames of reference that are in motion relative to one another is for distances and times to vary in systematic ways in the relevant frames, and the Lorentz equations allow us to calculate the precise differences. Hence, the recognition of the new invariant required the rejection of the old: according to Newton, the distances and times between objects are both absolute quantities and thus invariant across frames of reference.

Time dilation and length contraction initially strike us as utterly bizarre phenomena because they are not observed to occur in ordinary life. When traveling on a moving train, we can make the phone call we agreed to make at 2 p.m. without using any complex equations to work out how much “train-time” differs from “land-time.” A fast-moving train does not look contracted or “scrunched up” to passersby, but this is because the relativistic effects only become significant at enormously high speeds. The time dilation and length contraction of a train moving at 150 km/h will be minute—about one part per hundred trillion.

Even a jet traveling at twice the speed of sound will only contract by less than the width of an atom. Compared with the speed of light, these speeds are snail-like.If a jet could be photographed moving at 85 percent of light speed, it would have half its normal length, and time on board would pass at half speed compared with time on the ground. Beings who normally travel at near-light speeds would be all too familiar with the relativistic effects we find bizarre.

The fact that we live in slow motion comparatively does not mean it is impossible to confirm predicted relativistic effects. Cosmic radiation striking our atmosphere produces showers of muons—subatomic particles—that speed towards the Earth's surface. Even though muons travel at about 95 percent of the speed of light, and are very short-lived, they still reach the surface. By virtue of their great speed, the length of their journey is shorter, and their internal clock runs slower, so the trip only occupies a small portion of their lifespan. On a more mundane level, very accurate atomic clocks can measure the time dilation produced by transatlantic flights. These experiments match the predictions of special relativity.

When Einstein worked out some implications of special relativity, he discovered that as an object's velocity increases, so does its inertial mass. As velocity approaches that of light, the energy needed to move it tends to infinity. This means no material object can be accelerated to the speed of light.

Surprisingly, this does not mean stars hundreds of light-years away cannot be reached within a human lifetime. For a ship moving near light speed, distances would shrink, and time would slow so much that a trip of a million light-years could take only about ten years of ship-time.

You might think that if a spaceship can cross a distance light takes a million years to travel in just ten years, it must be traveling faster than light, which is impossible. But there are two perspectives. From Earth’s point of view, light takes a million years to cross the distance. From the light's own viewpoint, which carries no clock or ruler, the journey takes no time and crosses no distance—time stops and the universe shrinks to zero size. Thus, from the ship's perspective, the trip takes longer than for the light. From Earth's frame, if light takes a million years, the ship will take millions more, and the return journey will be equally long.

While special relativity allows for traveling to distant parts of the universe within a human lifetime as measured by ship-time, significant practical obstacles remain. Perhaps the most difficult is attaining speeds close to the speed of light. This requires acceleration that produces inertial forces beyond what is survivable.

### Simultaneity

One of the most significant aspects of special relativity concerns the relativity of simultaneity—the idea that whether events occur at the same time depends on the observer’s frame of reference.

Einstein began by asking how we measure the time interval between events, especially those separated by distance. Suppose two points, X and Y, several miles apart, are struck by lightning. How can we tell if the strikes were simultaneous?

If we are midway between X and Y, we can observe flashes from the strikes. Knowing the speed of light and our distances, if we see the flashes simultaneously, the strikes were simultaneous. This relies on signals that travel at finite speed, so some calculation is always needed to determine event timing.

Alternatively, if observers are stationed at X and Y, each with synchronized clocks and radios, they can report the strike times. But synchronizing clocks over distance involves the same issues: messages themselves travel at light speed, so synchronization depends on relative positioning.

Einstein showed that people stationary relative to each other can agree on a common time system. However, observers moving relative to each other cannot agree on simultaneity.

Imagine a train speeding along a track at 60 percent of the speed of light. You stand beside the track as the midpoint of the train passes by, and lightning strikes both ends of the train simultaneously. You see the flashes at the same time and conclude the strikes were simultaneous; the light from the two ends traveled equal distances, and light speed is constant.

However, an observer sitting at the midpoint on the roof of the moving train sees things differently. Because the observer moves toward the flash from the front, that light reaches them before the flash from the rear, which has a longer distance to cover. This observer concludes the flashes were not simultaneous: the front was struck before the rear.

The observer on the train’s reasoning is valid within their frame of reference. You then see a third lightning bolt strike the train's front shortly after the first two strikes; the observer on the train sees that flash's light reach them simultaneously with the rear flash's light, concluding those two strikes were simultaneous.

Thus, three events—1, 2, and 3—have different time orderings depending on the observer. Using Einstein's method, you find events 1 and 2 simultaneous, with 3 occurring later. The observer on the train finds 1 first, followed by 2 and 3 simultaneously.

The presence of human observers is irrelevant; automatic devices would yield the same results. Given the Relativity Postulate, both perspectives are equally valid—each observer can consider themselves stationary, and there is no absolute fact about who is right.As a consequence, there is no fact of the matter as to whether the events in question occurred at the same time or not. In appreciating the symmetrical character of the situation, it is useful to consider it from the perspective of the person on the train; thus far, we have been describing things from the ground-based point of view. So imagine you are on the train conducting a simple experiment. You are sitting at the midpoint of your carriage, holding a switch in your hand. Connected to the switch by equal lengths of wire are two lamps at either end of the carriage. You flick the switch and see light arrive from each lamp at the same time. You naturally conclude that the lights came on simultaneously; after all, the light from each had the same distance to travel, and the speed of light is absolute.

Now suppose that at the moment you press the switch, you are directly opposite O, who is sitting at the trackside. What do you think O would observe and conclude? At the moment you flicked the switch, O was moving away from you at 0.6c in a rearward direction, so O was moving towards the light emitted by the rear bulb and away from the light coming from the forward end of the carriage. Therefore, he would see the rear light first, then the forward light later. Since O knows that the distance between you and the two bulbs is exactly the same, and given the absoluteness of the speed of light, O must conclude that the bulbs didn't come on at the same time.

The role of the Light Postulate is no less important; even if we assume—in line with the Relativity Postulate—that both perspectives are equally valid, the reasoning that leads to the conclusion that simultaneity is relative relies on the assumption that light rays travel at the same speed with respect to all observers, no matter how fast they are moving relative to their source.

Returning to the original case: O is justified in thinking that the front bulb lights up before the rear bulb, because he is situated right in the middle of the train, so the light has equal distances to travel, and the light coming from the front and rear is traveling at the same speed. If O found that the light from the front was traveling faster than the light from the rear, then he wouldn't be justified in thinking the front bulb lit first just because its light arrived first. But this is not the case. If O measures the speed of the light when it reaches him, he finds it is the same in both directions.

We have been looking at one example, but the lesson generalizes. In the context of the Special Theory of Relativity, simultaneity is not absolute but frame-relative. Spatially separated events that are simultaneous from the perspective of one inertial frame are not simultaneous from the perspective of other inertial frames. Since the perspectives of all inertial frames are equally valid, there is no sense in the idea that the events are “really” simultaneous or not.

### 18.5 Minkowski Spacetime

The new conception of space and time was clarified by Minkowski in 1908, in a lecture whose opening words became famous:

*“The views of space and time which I wish to lay before you have sprung from the soil of experimental physics, and therein lies their strength. They are radical. Henceforth, space by itself, and time by itself, are doomed to fade away into mere shadows, and only a kind of union of the two will preserve an independent reality.”*

Minkowski showed that although special relativity makes some quantities relative and frame-dependent, there are other quantities that are as absolute as any in Newtonian dynamics; it is just that these quantities are neither spatial nor temporal as we ordinarily understand the terms. This new clarity came from formulating special relativity as a spacetime theory. We have already encountered the general idea of spacetime and two different types of spacetime structure: Newtonian and neo-Newtonian. The spacetime structure of special relativity is interestingly different from both.

Newtonian spacetime is rich in structure. Any two points, no matter when or where they are located, are separated by a definite spatial and temporal interval, and these intervals are invariant: they are the same when viewed from any inertial frame. Newtonian spacetime also has an affine structure, meaning there is a distinction between curved and straight paths through spacetime, which is also invariant. The difference between absolute rest and absolute motion is well defined; likewise, the distinction between uniform motion and acceleration.

In neo-Newtonian spacetime, some of this structure is absent. Affine structure is retained—the distinction between straight and curved paths remains intact—and hence there is a real difference between accelerated and non-accelerated motion. There is an invariant spatial distance between points that occur at the same time, and non-simultaneous points are separated by an invariant temporal interval. What is lost is spatial distance between points at different times. There is consequently no distinction between absolute rest and absolute motion, and the notion of absolute velocity has no sense. It is for this reason that Newtonian dynamics, which does not rely on absolute velocity, is best formulated in neo-Newtonian spacetime.

However, although in special relativity neither spatial nor temporal distances are invariant, the speed of light is. Since there are no invariant velocities in neo-Newtonian spacetime, it is clear that we need a different kind of spacetime to make sense of special relativity.

Minkowski’s solution was to build a spacetime around the paths that light rays can take in a vacuum. The idea that luminal trajectories are built into the very fabric of spacetime is a natural response to the abolition of the aether. Since light is not a disturbance within any material medium, the only candidate for the structure that constrains or determines the paths light rays can take is spacetime itself. Because all known space is filled with electromagnetic radiation, the luminal structure of spacetime extends through the otherwise empty regions between stars and galaxies. For reasons explained shortly, this invariant feature of Minkowski spacetime is often called the "light-cone" structure.

The speed of light is not the only invariant quantity in Minkowski spacetime; the so-called "interval" between points and events is the same in all inertial frames. The spacetime interval is neither purely spatial nor purely temporal but a mixture of both. A useful analogy from geometry helps. By Pythagoras' theorem, the square of the hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle equals the sum of the squares of the other two sides.

Similarly, a given spacetime interval relates systematically to two other distances—but here the similarities end. First, only one of these distances is spatial; the other is temporal. Second, to calculate the interval, we subtract these distances rather than add them. The relevant formula expresses the interval as the difference between the square of the spatial distance and the square of the time interval multiplied by the square of the speed of light. Observers in different inertial frames who use this formula reach the same value for the interval between a pair of events, even though they disagree about the spatial and temporal distances between them. Since all inertial frames are equivalent, there is no fact of the matter as to the correct breakdown of the interval into space and time components; the only objective, frame-independent fact about the events is the magnitude of the spacetime interval that separates them.

---

A little more about the standard way of understanding the luminal structure of Minkowski spacetime will provide a useful aid in grasping the most philosophically significant feature of special relativity: the relativity of simultaneity. The key notion is that of a light-cone, a term apt in one sense but misleading in another. Imagine a light source O in outer space turned on for a brief time. Light spreads from the source in all directions, forming a spherical wavefront. As time passes, the spherical surface expands. Depicting this expansion in a single two-dimensional diagram is impossible, but if we represent only two dimensions of space, the expanding sphere becomes successive larger circles that together form the surface of a cone. 

We can represent all light arriving at source O by the surface of a second cone, expanding backward in time. When combined with the future-directed cone, representing light emitted by the source, they produce the characteristic hourglass shape often associated with light-cones. The points on these cones are those that can be connected by light rays traveling in a vacuum and are said to be “light-like” connected. The spacetime interval between such events is zero. Zero interval is not like spatial distance, since events separated by very large spatial distances can be connected by an interval of zero.- The region of spacetime that lies outside the cones is known as "absolute elsewhere"; the spatiotemporal separation between points in this region is such that only a signal traveling faster than light could connect them; such points are said to be "space-like" separated. Events that are very close together in time but spatially at a considerable distance fall into this category. The interval between space-like separated points is positive.

- The points lying inside the cones constitute the regions known as "absolute past" and "absolute future"; their spatiotemporal separation is such that they can be connected by signals traveling slower than light, and they are said to be "time-like" connected. The interval between time-like separated points is negative.

The past and future light-cones of a spacetime point form a four-dimensional structure, with one spatial dimension suppressed for visualization. One region is the point's "absolute future," the opposite is its "absolute past." Points on the surfaces of the cones are light-like connected; regions within the cones are connected by processes traveling slower than light. Signals from outside the cones would have to travel faster than light and thus lie in the "absolute elsewhere."

- For any space-like separated events, there is an inertial frame in which they are simultaneous and differ only in their spatial coordinates.

- For any time-like separated events, there is an inertial frame in which they occur at the same spatial coordinates and differ only in their time of occurrence.

- Assuming that no physical influence can travel faster than light, events that are time-like connected can be causally related; no causal relation can hold between space-like events. Minkowski spacetime embodies the causal structure of the universe.

It is possible for two planets, X and Y, to interact causally because they are linked by time-like paths through spacetime. Two other planets, X and Z, cannot causally interact since all paths between them are space-like at least in part. Causal influence is sometimes said to "thread the light-cones."

Although Special Theory of Relativity (STR) relativizes simultaneity, it does so only within strict limits: the relativization applies only to space-like separated events. Events that are time-like separated are non-simultaneous in all frames of reference. The temporal ordering of events inside the light cones is the same for all observers, though temporal distances may vary.

There exist "simultaneity hyperplanes"—three-dimensional slices of spacetime containing collections of space-like separated events that are simultaneous in different inertial frames. For example, one such hyperplane corresponds to events simultaneous in a particular observer’s frame, while others correspond to events simultaneous in frames moving relative to that observer. Events outside the light cones lie in the "absolute elsewhere" and can be simultaneous with the reference event from some frames but not others.

This structure applies at every spacetime point. The frame-relativity of simultaneity depends on the properties and extent of spacetime as well as the observer's location. For large-scale estimates of our universe’s age and size, much of spacetime may lie in our absolute past and future. Light-cone diagrams simplify these relations by representing light-like connections at 45 degrees.

In everyday life, STR does not abolish the present but makes it slightly uncertain over very short timescales. In a typical room, this indeterminacy spans millionths of a second; over hundreds of miles, it extends to thousandths of a second. While temporal ordering within these regions is frame-relative, causal ordering is not. The relativity of simultaneity applies only to events so close in time that no subluminal signal can connect them.

Maudlin proposes a test of understanding relativity: asking what it would be like to travel at 99.99% the speed of light. Common answers—feeling flattened or slowed—miss the point. Actually, you are already traveling at such a speed in some inertial frame; there is no absolute velocity to attribute to objects except light speed itself. Velocities below the speed of light are not intrinsic properties but depend on the chosen frame of reference. There are no physical effects of "traveling near the speed of light" as absolute states. Instead, various inertial frames provide equally valid ways of describing distances and times.

Relativistic effects like length contraction and time dilation might seem like illusions, similar to distorted reflections in mirrors. However, unlike mirror images—which are merely visual distortions—relativistic changes are real and depend on the observer’s frame. Observers moving relative to one another measure true differences in shapes and durations. STR shows that these properties are not absolute but frame-relative. Objects and processes have definite shapes and durations only relative to a given inertial frame; only spacetime intervals remain invariant.

Moreover, it would be mistaken to think the relativity of shape is only an optical effect. An object's frame-relative shape accurately corresponds to physical constraints, such as the shape of a hole it can pass through, which is itself frame-relative. Similarly, an external clock on a fast-moving spaceship observed from Earth appears to run slow, and this corresponds to actual differences in elapsed time for travelers and observers after the journey. Although counterintuitive, these effects are consistent and coherent within the framework of STR.

### STR

19. Relativity and Reality 

### 19.1 Reality Unconfined

We turn now from the physics of STR to its metaphysical interpretation. I want to focus on some influential lines of argument that attempt to draw very significant conclusions about the nature of time from the distinctive spacetime geometry of STR. These arguments target all those dynamic models of time according to which there is an ontological asymmetry between past, present, and future.

As we have seen, there are several such models. Presentists of all persuasions agree that the past and the future are unreal; the Growing Block theory of Broad and Tooley is more liberal and withholds reality only from the ever-shrinking future. In both cases, the present has special ontological significance, constituting as it does the frontier between what is real and what is not. In outlining these theories, I tacitly assumed that the present extends throughout the universe and is absolute (i.e., non-perspectival, non-frame-relative). Let us call models of time that posit an absolute and universal present, and hence an absolute and universal tide of becoming (or annihilation), "classical dynamic models," or CDMs. Given what we have seen so far, it is obvious that there are certain tensions, to say the least, between CDMs and STR. One of the questions we will be addressing in this chapter is whether these tensions are fatal to CDMs. We will also explore the options for non-classical dynamic models of time in the context of STR.

The mere fact that STR is a spacetime theory does not in itself mean it is a hostile environment for CDMs. Minkowski spacetime diagrams depict worldlines representing all stages of an object's history stretching across the page, and future light cones are represented in precisely the same way as past light cones. But while this may give the impression that a Minkowskian world is an eternal four-dimensional block of events, this interpretation is by no means obligatory. Newtonian physics can be formulated as a spacetime theory. Although there is no distinction drawn between past, present, and future in Newtonian spacetime diagrams, it does not follow that there is no such distinction in a Newtonian world; spacetime diagrams of the standard form may not include complete information about what they depict.

More threatening is what STR has to say about the present itself. The most obvious challenge posed by STR to CDMs runs thus: for all CDMs, the division between what is present and non-present has ontological significance. Under STR, however, the distinction between what is present and non-present is dependent on an arbitrary choice of reference frame. Isn't it absurd to suppose that the division between what is real and unreal can be similarly dependent?

Consider an event E on the recently discovered planet that orbits Epsilon Eridani (10.5 light-years from Earth). According to the Earth's frame of reference, E lies in the present, but according to Jupiter's frame of reference, it lies in the future. If, like CDM theorists, we hold that whether an event is real or not depends on where it stands in relation to the present, what is real relative to one state of motion is not real relative to another. Relative to the Earth, E is real. Relative to Jupiter, it lies in the future and is therefore unreal. Do we really want to relativize reality in this way? If not, then the only obvious option is to subscribe to the block-universe conception and hold that all events are real, irrespective of their spacetime location.

The suspicion that CDM theories and STR are irreconcilable can be put on a firmer footing. Rietdijk (1966) and Putnam (1967) argue that, provided we make certain plausible assumptions, it can be proved that the Block view is the only model of time compatible with STR.

Let R be the relation of "being real with respect to," so Rxy means that x is real with respect to y. Let us further suppose that R is reflexive (a thing is real with respect to itself), symmetrical (if Rxy, then Ryx), and transitive (if Rxy and Ryz, then Rxz). In a Newtonian world, each successive present is plausibly regarded as a three-dimensional universe-wide reality slice, or in the terminology of spacetime theories, the present is a single three-dimensional simultaneity hyperplane of momentary duration.

Now consider three events: E1, E2, and E3, each a point in spacetime. E1 is where two spaceships, S1 and S2, moving relative to each other, briefly meet and their radio antennas touch. The simultaneity hyperplane of S1 is P1. E2 lies on P1, so it is simultaneous with E1 relative to S1. E3 lies in the future relative to S1. If we suppose the Growing Block model applies, then E1 is real relative to E2 but not to E3; everything up to and including P1 is real, but nothing later than P1 is yet real.

However, P2 is the simultaneity hyperplane of S2, the other ship at E1. Since S2 moves relative to S1, P2 will differ from P1. From S2's perspective, E3 is simultaneous with E1, whereas E2 occurs earlier than either. This means that although from S1's view, E3 has yet to occur, it no longer seems sensible to say E3 is unreal. Since S2 is real relative to S1 (they are in contact), and E3 is real relative to S2, E3 must be real relative to S1 as well.

Extending this reasoning to all spacelike related locations around E1 shows that reality cannot be confined; it spreads through all future events, contradicting the Growing Block model.

Presentism faces a similar problem. If only what is present is real, meaning only what is simultaneous with E1, then from S1's perspective E4 lies in the past and so is not real. Yet E4 is simultaneous with E1 according to S2 and thus real relative to S2. Since S2 is real relative to S1, E4 must be real relative to S1 too.

If this reasoning is valid, dynamic models of time face serious challenges. Putnam goes further:

"I conclude that the problem of the reality and determinateness of future events is now solved. Moreover, it is solved by physics and not philosophy. We have learned that we live in a four-dimensional and not a three-dimensional world, and that space and time—or, better, space-like separations and time-like separations—are just two aspects of a single four-dimensional continuum with a peculiar metric ... Indeed, I do not believe that there are any longer any philosophical problems about Time; there is only the physical problem of determining the exact physical geometry of the four-dimensional continuum that we inhabit." (1967, original emphasis)

This is a momentous conclusion, but is the reasoning that supports it valid? And even if it is, are there ways it can be resisted? There are, and they fall into two camps. Some dynamists respond to the Rietdijk/Putnam argument by rejecting STR, at least in its standard form; I will be looking at this strategy in §19.4.

Others adopt a compatibilist approach and try to find ways to accommodate dynamism without rejecting the key principles of the Special Theory of Relativity (STR). We shall start by looking at the options open to compatibilists.

### 19.2 Compatibilism

A straightforward way to reconcile dynamism with STR is noted by Sklar. The problem arises because under STR, simultaneity is relative: different events are simultaneous in different frames of reference. If we suppose that (i) simultaneous events are "real with respect to" one another, and (ii) this relation is transitive, then it follows that all events are equally real. But Sklar asks, why assume that the "real with respect to" relation is transitive? Since embracing STR means accepting that simultaneity is non-transitive and frame-relative, what prevents one from taking the further step of holding that "real with respect to" is also non-transitive and frame-relative? It might even be argued that those who refuse this step fail to fully embrace the relativistic viewpoint.

To be sure, it is unusual to suppose that if event x is real with respect to y, and y to z, then x might not be real with respect to z.But so is the idea that simultaneity can fail to be transitive. Is relativizing existence any less odd than relativizing simultaneity? Those who dismiss any connection between temporality and existence may be inclined to think it is, but exponents of dynamic models of time do not share this view. Anyone who believes that time and existence are intimately connected might be very tempted to suppose that if simultaneity is frame-relative, existence must be too. Sklar concludes, "The science can change the philosophy and put the dispute in a new perspective, but it cannot resolve the dispute in any ultimate sense" (1977: 275).

While this relativization shows that STR does not in itself entail the Block conception, but does so only with the benefit of a questionable metaphysical assumption, it does nothing to assist us in choosing between dynamisms; it seems equally compatible with the Growing Block model and the different versions of Presentism. In a later essay (1981), Sklar argues that some Presentists may find themselves in an uncomfortable position. The Presentists in question are those who reject the reality of both past and future on the grounds of "epistemic remoteness": unlike what is happening here and now, our knowledge of the past and future is based on more or less unreliable inferences of one kind or another. Since the here and now is causally isolated from the events to which it is space-like related, the epistemically motivated Presentist should surely place these events in the same category as past and future events and hold them all to be unreal. Presentism thus collapses into solipsism of the most extreme sort: other than this spacetime point, nothing is real. However, while these considerations may lead epistemically motivated Presentists to reconsider their position, those who reject the past and future on purely metaphysical grounds—for example, the conviction that when an event is past, it is utterly gone—will not feel the same inclination.

Sklar suggests that relativizing the relationship of "determinate reality" is "by no means inconsistent or patently absurd" (1977: 275); but since relativizing here means rendering non-transitive, some may disagree. Of course, Compound Presentists willingly embrace the non-transitivity of coexistence, for they see no incoherence in the idea that events may undergo absolute annihilation as well as absolute becoming, and I suspect they are right. However, any dynamist who takes a different view on this issue and who also inclines toward compatibilism will have to look elsewhere. The version of compatibilism proposed by Stein (1968, 1991) may well fit the bill.

### 19.3 Time Fragmented

Stein suggests that anyone who wishes to reconcile STR with "real becoming" must answer two questions:

First, what is the spatiotemporal nature of the "stages" in which reality comes into being?

Second, what is the spatiotemporal criterion that, at any given stage, distinguishes the "definite" from the "not yet settled"?

He cites the following general principles to guide answers to these questions:

- The fundamental entity for distinguishing the "already definite" from the "still unsettled" is the here and now, that is, the spacetime point.

- If the state at point b is "already definite" as of point a, then whatever is definite as of b is also definite as of a. This means that "is already definite as of" is a transitive relation between points.

- The state at any point a is already definite as of point a itself.

- For any point a, there are points whose state is unsettled as of a.

Stein assumes a definite time-orientation throughout Minkowski spacetime, necessary to say that what lies in the future is not yet definite or settled. With these assumptions, he shows that for any given point x, the only points that are related to x and thus already definite as of x are those in x's causal past—that is, on or inside x's past light cone, including x itself. Points outside x's past light cone, which are space-like related to x, have the same status as those in x's future light cone; they are not real or definite with respect to x. No other assignment fits the assumptions.

This relativization of becoming to individual points fragments reality. There is no universe-wide division between what is real and what is not; instead, this division differs for every point, each having a unique past light cone. On Stein's model, what is real and definite for a given point does not extend beyond its past light cone. Consequently, no two observers will fully agree on what is real and definite unless they occupy the same point. Observers in relative motion will agree on what is real and definite but disagree about what is present. Since, in Stein's view, everything in the absolute elsewhere has not become and is not yet determinate, this disagreement does not concern anything factual for the relevant point.

Stein does not claim to prove that objective becoming actually occurs in Minkowski spacetimes; he remains neutral. His goal is to show that certain objections do not rule out the possibility of becoming occurring. While some principles he assumes are debatable, they are reasonable and defensible. Earlier objections target Continuous Dynamical Models, which posit a single universe-wide division between real and unreal, but these objections do not undermine Stein's approach. The concepts of "temporal becoming" and "spatially extended now" are conceptually independent, so there is no reason to think that becoming requires a spatially extended present—a notion explicitly rejected by special relativity.

Sklar commits a similar error by assuming the dynamist will want to adopt an extended becoming concept appropriate to Newtonian physics but alien to relativity.

Sklar's approach requires adopting a non-transitive notion of "real or determinate with respect to," but Stein's does not. Stein stipulates that the relation defining definiteness, called R, can only hold between points if one lies in the causal past of the other. Then R is transitive and reflexive but not symmetrical: if b is definite as of a because it lies in a's causal past, a is not definite as of b. This asymmetry prevents all points from being equally definite with respect to one another. Similarly, in the Growing Block theory, past events are real as of later times, but future events are not.

Stein's compatibilism may be coherent, but his account of what metaphysical "becoming" actually involves is somewhat unclear. He speaks of "temporal evolution as a becoming real, or becoming determinate, of what is not yet real or determinate," and distinguishes between parts of history that "had already become" and parts "not yet settled." It is uncertain whether the "not yet settled" parts of reality are wholly non-existent or exist in an incomplete form. This ambiguity may not matter. Perhaps the notion of a real but not fully determinate event can be coherent, allowing for dynamic models of time beyond previously explored ones. Stein's view applies to models positing a clear ontological distinction between what is real and what is not, where "becoming" is absolute—the creation of time and events, or spacetime points and events, from nothing.

A natural takeaway is that we cannot ask what is real as of a given moment defined as a temporal cross-section of the universe, but we can ask what is real as of a given place at a time, that is, at a spacetime point. What is real as of one point differs from what is real as of every other point.
